### Leveraging Diffusion Models for Parameterized Quantum Circuit Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20863v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Barta, Darya Martyniuk, Johannes Jung, Adrian Paschke
- **Abstract**: Quantum computing holds immense potential, yet its practical success depends on multiple factors, including advances in quantum circuit design. In this paper, we introduce a generative approach based on denoising diffusion models (DMs) to synthesize parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs). Extending the recent diffusion model pipeline of F\"urrutter et al. [1], our model effectively conditions the synthesis process, enabling the simultaneous generation of circuit architectures and their continuous gate parameters. We demonstrate our approach in synthesizing PQCs optimized for generating high-fidelity Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states and achieving high accuracy in quantum machine learning (QML) classification tasks. Our results indicate a strong generalization across varying gate sets and scaling qubit counts, highlighting the versatility and computational efficiency of diffusion-based methods. This work illustrates the potential of generative models as a powerful tool for accelerating and optimizing the design of PQCs, supporting the development of more practical and scalable quantum applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a generative approach using denoising diffusion models (DMs) to synthesize parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs).  It extends previous work on DM-based quantum circuit synthesis by incorporating continuous gate parameters alongside discrete gate selections.  The model is trained on datasets of quantum circuits designed for state preparation (GHZ states) and QML classification tasks. The results demonstrate the model's ability to generate diverse and high-performing PQCs, generalizing across gate sets and qubit counts, while avoiding computationally expensive parameter training typically required by other methods.  The authors also highlight the utility of classifier-free guidance (CFG) in modulating circuit synthesis to achieve specific performance targets and structural properties.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in extending DM-based quantum circuit synthesis to handle *continuous* gate parameters within a unified generative framework. Previous works focused on generating circuits with discrete gate sets.  The use of a CLIP encoder for textual conditioning to guide the diffusion process based on desired performance metrics (fidelity, accuracy) also adds a layer of control that hasn't been thoroughly explored in prior DM-based circuit generation research. While [26] uses a diffusion model for ansatz generation for VQE, this work focuses on incorporating continuous gate parameters. The novelty resides in the application of a specific technique rather than a wholly new field.

* **Significance:**  The significance stems from addressing a key bottleneck in automated quantum circuit design: the computationally expensive optimization of gate parameters. By generating circuits with both architectures *and* parameter values directly, the approach bypasses iterative classical optimization steps.  The demonstrated generalization ability across different gate sets and qubit numbers suggests potential for creating circuits adaptable to various quantum hardware platforms.  The findings also have implications for hardware-aware quantum circuit design, as evidenced by synthesizing circuits incorporating hardware-native gates.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Handling Continuous Parameters:** A key strength is the successful integration of continuous gate parameters into the DM-based synthesis pipeline. This significantly expands the expressiveness and applicability of generative circuit design.
    * **Generalization:**  The paper shows strong generalization across different gate sets and qubit numbers, indicating a robust learning capability beyond the training data.
    * **Classifier-Free Guidance:** The use of CFG to modulate circuit synthesis based on performance targets provides a powerful mechanism for controlling the characteristics of generated circuits.
    * **Computational Efficiency:** Bypassing costly parameter optimization accelerates circuit design.
    * **Clear and Well-Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making the approach and results easily understandable.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Qubit Scaling:** While generalization to 4 and 5 qubits is demonstrated, the performance at 5 qubits degrades significantly.  The paper acknowledges the need for further improvements in scaling the approach to larger qubit systems.
    * **Dataset Dependence:** The performance of DMs is highly dependent on the training dataset.  While the authors describe a strategic approach to dataset balancing, further investigation is needed to understand the sensitivity of the model to different dataset compositions.
    * **Parameter Dimension Limitations:** The approach is currently limited to one parameter encoding dimension. While this may not be a limiting factor for current datasets, it could become an issue with more complex gate sets.
    * **Lack of Direct Comparison:** There isn't a direct comparison with other automated PQC design methods (e.g., reinforcement learning, evolutionary algorithms) in terms of performance and resource utilization. This makes it difficult to definitively assess the superiority of the DM-based approach.
    * **Hardware Testing Absent:** The synthesized circuits aren't tested on real quantum hardware, which would provide valuable insights into their noise resilience and practical applicability.

* **Potential Impact:**  The approach has the potential to accelerate quantum algorithm development and enable the design of circuits that are better tailored to specific hardware constraints. The capacity to learn hardware-aware design heuristics (e.g., the observed layer-like motifs) is a particularly promising avenue for future research.  It could also contribute to automating the compilation and optimization of quantum code for near-term devices.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of automated quantum circuit design. The successful integration of continuous parameters into a DM framework is an important step forward. The generalization abilities and the control offered by CFG are also valuable features.  However, limitations in scaling, the lack of direct comparisons with alternative methods, and the absence of hardware testing temper the overall impact. The approach has opened new ways for the generative models to enhance the quantum circuit synthesis, but the paper could be more impactful with hardware testing or an ablation study.

### Respond to Change with Constancy: Instruction-tuning with LLM for Non-I.I.D. Network Traffic Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20866v1)
- **Authors**: Xinjie Lin, Gang Xiong, Gaopeng Gou, Wenqi Dong, Jing Yu, Zhen Li, Wei Xia
- **Abstract**: Encrypted traffic classification is highly challenging in network security due to the need for extracting robust features from content-agnostic traffic data. Existing approaches face critical issues: (i) Distribution drift, caused by reliance on the closedworld assumption, limits adaptability to realworld, shifting patterns; (ii) Dependence on labeled data restricts applicability where such data is scarce or unavailable. Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable potential in offering generalizable solutions across a wide range of tasks, achieving notable success in various specialized fields. However, their effectiveness in traffic analysis remains constrained by challenges in adapting to the unique requirements of the traffic domain. In this paper, we introduce a novel traffic representation model named Encrypted Traffic Out-of-Distribution Instruction Tuning with LLM (ETooL), which integrates LLMs with knowledge of traffic structures through a self-supervised instruction tuning paradigm. This framework establishes connections between textual information and traffic interactions. ETooL demonstrates more robust classification performance and superior generalization in both supervised and zero-shot traffic classification tasks. Notably, it achieves significant improvements in F1 scores: APP53 (I.I.D.) to 93.19%(6.62%) and 92.11%(4.19%), APP53 (O.O.D.) to 74.88%(18.17%) and 72.13%(15.15%), and ISCX-Botnet (O.O.D.) to 95.03%(9.16%) and 81.95%(12.08%). Additionally, we construct NETD, a traffic dataset designed to support dynamic distributional shifts, and use it to validate ETooL's effectiveness under varying distributional conditions. Furthermore, we evaluate the efficiency gains achieved through ETooL's instruction tuning approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ETooL (Encrypted Traffic Out-of-Distribution Instruction Tuning with LLM), a novel framework for encrypted traffic classification, designed to address the challenges of distribution drift and limited labeled data. ETooL leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) and a self-supervised instruction tuning paradigm to integrate knowledge of traffic structures, establishing connections between textual information and traffic interactions.  The framework incorporates a Traffic2Graph module to represent flow interactions as learnable graph structures, followed by instruction tuning phases utilizing BURST graph matching and traffic-specific instructions. The authors also contribute NETD, a dynamically distributed traffic dataset, to validate ETooL's effectiveness under varying distributional conditions. The paper demonstrates improved classification performance and generalization in both supervised and zero-shot traffic classification tasks, showing significant gains in F1 scores across various datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several elements of novelty. Primarily, the application of instruction tuning with LLMs to the problem of encrypted traffic classification, especially in handling out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios, is relatively new. The design of the Traffic2Graph module to represent complex flow interactions is also novel. While pre-training with traffic data (ET-BERT) exists, the integration of graph structures and LLMs through a specific instruction tuning process differentiates this work. However, there exist limited prior works on traffic sequence representation [12], [24]. Additionally, recent research has indicated LLMs’ abilities to generalize across structured data formats [48].

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing a critical challenge in network security: the brittleness of existing traffic classification methods to distribution drift. The proposed ETooL framework offers a potentially more robust and adaptable solution by leveraging LLMs. The performance gains demonstrated across multiple datasets, particularly in OOD scenarios, highlight the practical value of the approach. The NETD dataset, designed for dynamic distributional shifts, is also a valuable contribution to the research community. The use of flow-specific instruction tuning tasks (BURST Graph Matching) offers potential improvements in the comprehension of flow interaction within the LLM.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experiments across multiple datasets (including a newly created one) and scenarios (IID and OOD).
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The methodology is explained clearly, including the design of the ETooL framework and the construction of the NETD dataset.
    *   **Strong Performance:** The results demonstrate substantial improvements compared to existing state-of-the-art methods, particularly in OOD settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The ETooL framework is relatively complex, involving multiple components and tuning stages. This complexity may make it challenging to implement and deploy in practice.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The use of LLMs can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting its applicability in resource-constrained environments. The paper acknowledges challenges regarding GPU memory limitations.
    *   **Scalability:** While the authors demonstrate strong performance on the datasets used, the scalability of ETooL to very large and dynamic networks remains an open question.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** Although empirical results are strong, the paper lacks a deep theoretical justification for why the proposed architecture and tuning strategy are effective. There's an assumption that certain invariant properties are present, which isn't validated rigorously.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in encrypted traffic classification. It provides a promising direction for leveraging LLMs to address the challenges of distribution drift. The NETD dataset can also serve as a benchmark for evaluating the robustness of future methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, I would rate this paper a **Score: 7**. The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to a significant problem in network security. The empirical results are impressive, and the proposed NETD dataset is a valuable contribution. However, the complexity of the framework, the computational cost, and the lack of deep theoretical grounding limit its immediate impact. While the work offers promising results in improving the LLM's comprehension of flow interaction, it falls short of fully validating the assumptions that underlie the model. It provides ample opportunities for future work, but these aspects temper the overall score.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Step-Wise Formal Verification for LLM-Based Mathematical Problem Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20869v1)
- **Authors**: Kuo Zhou, Lu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated formidable capabilities in solving mathematical problems, yet they may still commit logical reasoning and computational errors during the problem-solving process. Thus, this paper proposes a framework, MATH-VF, which includes a Formalizer and a Critic, for formally verifying the correctness of the solutions generated by large language models. Our framework first utilizes a Formalizer which employs an LLM to translate a natural language solution into a formal context. Afterward, our Critic (which integrates various external tools such as a Computer Algebra System and an SMT solver) evaluates the correctness of each statement within the formal context, and when a statement is incorrect, our Critic provides corrective feedback. We empirically investigate the effectiveness of MATH-VF in two scenarios: 1) Verification: MATH-VF is utilized to determine the correctness of a solution to a given problem. 2) Refinement: When MATH-VF identifies errors in the solution generated by an LLM-based solution generator for a given problem, it submits the corrective suggestions proposed by the Critic to the solution generator to regenerate the solution. We evaluate our framework on widely used mathematical benchmarks: MATH500 and ProcessBench, demonstrating the superiority of our approach over existing approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MATH-VF, a framework for formally verifying solutions to mathematical problems generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). MATH-VF comprises two main components: a Formalizer, which translates natural language solutions into a formal language called SimpleMath (an extension of first-order logic), and a Critic, which evaluates the correctness of each statement in the formal context using external tools like computer algebra systems (SymPy) and SMT solvers (Z3).  The Critic provides corrective feedback when errors are detected.  The framework aims to address the limitations of existing verification methods, including informal verification (PRMs and Critic models) and formal verification using interactive theorem provers (ITPs). A key contribution is leveraging the sparsity of the solution graph to reduce the number of tokens input to the Critic, thereby saving computational resources. The authors evaluate MATH-VF on MATH500 and ProcessBench datasets, demonstrating its superiority over existing approaches in verification, identification, and refinement tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel combination of techniques. The idea of formal verification for LLM-generated solutions isn't entirely new, but the specific architecture of MATH-VF, combining a custom formal language, external tools, and a sparsity-aware Critic, distinguishes it from prior work. The design of "SimpleMath" as a more LLM-friendly formalization target and the exploitation of the solution graph's sparsity are notable novelties.  The training-free nature of the approach is also a plus, contrasting with methods that require fine-tuning LLMs on formal knowledge.

* **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the growing need to ensure the reliability of LLMs in mathematical reasoning.  As LLMs are increasingly used in applications requiring accurate problem-solving, robust verification mechanisms become crucial. MATH-VF addresses this need by providing a step-by-step verification framework that not only identifies errors but also offers corrective feedback. The empirical results, demonstrating improved accuracy over existing approaches, further support the potential significance of the framework. However, the limitations section highlights areas for future work (e.g., syntax error handling, effectiveness of steps), and how well the tool scales to more complex problems is unproven.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Effective Combination of Techniques:** The paper effectively integrates LLMs with formal verification tools, leveraging the strengths of both approaches.
    * **Practical Optimizations:** The exploitation of solution graph sparsity is a practical optimization that reduces computational costs.
    * **Training-Free Approach:** The training-free nature of MATH-VF makes it more accessible and adaptable to different LLMs.
    * **Corrective Feedback:** Providing corrective feedback is more valuable than simply identifying errors.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the framework and its components.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **SimpleMath Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations in the formalization of complex mathematical concepts within SimpleMath.  The success of the approach heavily relies on the accurate translation of natural language to this formal language, and failures in this step can propagate downstream.  Furthermore, the reliance on first-order logic might limit the framework's ability to handle more advanced mathematical concepts.
    * **Dataset Dependence:** The results are evaluated on specific datasets (MATH500 and ProcessBench). While these are standard benchmarks, it's essential to assess the generalizability of MATH-VF to other types of mathematical problems and datasets.
    * **Limited Baselines:**  While the paper compares MATH-VF against several baselines, a more comprehensive comparison with state-of-the-art formal verification techniques (even if those require more upfront training) would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Scalability concerns:** The framework relies on querying LLMs at each step of the process, which may introduce latency and scaling issues.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM-based mathematical reasoning by providing a more reliable and interpretable verification mechanism. Future research may build on MATH-VF by exploring more expressive formal languages, incorporating more sophisticated reasoning techniques, and addressing the scalability challenges.

* **Rigorous Rationale:**
The MATH-VF framework represents a valuable contribution to the growing field of LLM-enhanced mathematical problem-solving. It addresses a crucial limitation in existing LLMs' capabilities -- reliability. Although the approach relies on the inherent capabilities of LLMs for initial formalization, it compensates for LLM weaknesses by employing external symbolic tools for rigorous verification. The design choice of focusing on a more user-friendly and easier-to-formalize formal language ("SimpleMath") appears to have paid off, as reflected in the empirical results. MATH-VF is simple to implement, it does not require training and can be applied using different models and LLMs. However, there remains room for improvement in terms of handling complex mathematical concepts within the formal language, in scaling the tool for complex problems and in showing robustness across a wider range of mathematical tasks.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Divide-Then-Align: Honest Alignment based on the Knowledge Boundary of RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20871v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Sun, Jianan Xie, Zhongqi Chen, Qiang Liu, Shu Wu, Yuehe Chen, Bowen Song, Weiqiang Wang, Zilei Wang, Liang Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) augmented with retrieval systems have significantly advanced natural language processing tasks by integrating external knowledge sources, enabling more accurate and contextually rich responses. To improve the robustness of such systems against noisy retrievals, Retrieval-Augmented Fine-Tuning (RAFT) has emerged as a widely adopted method. However, RAFT conditions models to generate answers even in the absence of reliable knowledge. This behavior undermines their reliability in high-stakes domains, where acknowledging uncertainty is critical. To address this issue, we propose Divide-Then-Align (DTA), a post-training approach designed to endow RAG systems with the ability to respond with "I don't know" when the query is out of the knowledge boundary of both the retrieved passages and the model's internal knowledge. DTA divides data samples into four knowledge quadrants and constructs tailored preference data for each quadrant, resulting in a curated dataset for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). Experimental results on three benchmark datasets demonstrate that DTA effectively balances accuracy with appropriate abstention, enhancing the reliability and trustworthiness of retrieval-augmented systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Divide-Then-Align: Honest Alignment based on the Knowledge Boundary of RAG":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a key limitation of Retrieval-Augmented Fine-Tuning (RAFT) in Large Language Models (LLMs): the tendency to generate answers even when the retrieved knowledge is unreliable or when the query falls outside the model's knowledge domain.  The authors propose a post-training approach called Divide-Then-Align (DTA) to instill RAG systems with the ability to respond with "I don't know" in such situations. DTA involves dividing data samples into four "knowledge quadrants" based on whether the answer lies within the LLM's parametric knowledge and the retrieval knowledge. Tailored preference data is then constructed for each quadrant, resulting in a dataset used for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). Experimental results on three datasets show that DTA balances accuracy with appropriate abstention, improving the reliability of RAG systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to the problem of over-answering in RAFT models.  The core idea of dividing the data into knowledge quadrants based on both parametric and retrieved knowledge is a valuable contribution. While existing works have tackled over-answering in LLMs and RAG systems, few have specifically focused on the intersection of *both* knowledge boundaries and *systematically* creating preference data based on this division. However, the techniques utilized (DPO, SFT, Classification loss) are standard components, thus diminishing the novelty a bit.

*   **Significance:** The problem being addressed is significant, as it concerns the trustworthiness and reliability of RAG systems, especially in high-stakes domains. The paper makes a tangible contribution by introducing a method that balances accuracy and abstention. The authors also provide a solid evaluation framework with quadrant-based metrics which is a helpful tool for future research in this area. The practical implications of enabling more honest and reliable RAG systems are considerable, potentially enabling wider adoption in sensitive applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with the DTA approach.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation framework and metrics.
    *   Demonstrated effectiveness on multiple datasets.
    *   Ablation studies providing insight into component contributions.
    * Focus on an important real-world problem of generating accurate responses when knowledge is limited.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 for determining whether a query is within KBr raises concerns about subjectivity and potential bias in the knowledge boundary determination.
    *   The individual components of DTA (DPO, SFT, classification loss) are well-established, which diminishes the technical novelty somewhat, though the system and pipeline design are unique.
    *   The reliance on lexical matching to test the correctness of a response is simplistic and potentially error-prone, particularly for more nuanced or abstract questions. Human evaluation would have further strenghened the results.
    *   The paper doesn't deeply explore the hyperparameters and model architecture tuning, and a more thorough tuning might improve its performance to a higher degree.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RAG systems and honest alignment.  The DTA approach provides a concrete way to improve the reliability of these systems. The evaluation framework could become a standard for assessing abstention capabilities. This research could encourage the development of safer and more trustworthy LLM-based applications.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a solid and practical solution to a critical problem in RAG systems. The idea of quadrant-based preference data construction is insightful and well-executed. However, the incremental nature of the technical contributions (using existing methods in a novel combination) and the limitations in the evaluation methodology prevent it from achieving a truly high score. The reliance on external models for a pivotal part of their process without a good understanding of the internal workings of the external model is also a detriment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### In Context Learning with Vision Transformers: Case Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20872v1)
- **Authors**: Antony Zhao, Alex Proshkin, Fergal Hennessy, Francesco Crivelli
- **Abstract**: Large transformer models have been shown to be capable of performing in-context learning. By using examples in a prompt as well as a query, they are capable of performing tasks such as few-shot, one-shot, or zero-shot learning to output the corresponding answer to this query. One area of interest to us is that these transformer models have been shown to be capable of learning the general class of certain functions, such as linear functions and small 2-layer neural networks, on random data (Garg et al, 2023). We aim to extend this to the image space to analyze their capability to in-context learn more complex functions on the image space, such as convolutional neural networks and other methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of vision transformers (ViTs) to perform in-context learning (ICL) on image data.  The authors explore whether a decoder-only transformer can learn simple convolutional mappings from a few examples provided in the prompt. They evaluate this capability across four experiments: learning linear functions, learning shifted linear functions, learning randomized convolutional networks, and learning randomized ViTs.  The model receives input-output pairs (image and a function of that image) and predicts the function output for a query image, mimicking few-shot learning. Results show that transformers can indeed learn these mappings with limited data, sometimes outperforming models trained directly on the same support set with gradient descent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper explores ICL specifically with *vision* transformers, and applies ICL to image data using CNNs and smaller ViTs as the sampled functions to in-context learn. While ICL itself isn't entirely new, its application to vision transformers with the goal of learning convolutional mappings is a novel area to explore. Previous work like [5] mentioned in the paper only focused on learning induction heads, not on modeling entire convolutional or ViT architectures. However, the functions being learned and dataset are very simple (e.g., learning linear functions on CIFAR-10 downscaled to 8x8). A key limitation is that the "in-context learning" aspect really seems to be using the transformer as a meta-learner to *approximate* a regressor. This is different from what is meant by ICL in the LLM context, where the model leverages pre-trained knowledge from a vast corpus.

* **Significance:** The results suggest a potential way to train vision models effectively with minimal data, which could be significant in scenarios where labeled data is scarce.  It also provides insights into the inductive bias of transformers and their ability to learn complex mappings without explicit pre-training. The comparison with gradient-descent-trained models is useful, demonstrating that ICL can sometimes be more data-efficient. However, the experiments are conducted on very low-resolution images and with relatively simple target functions. Extending this to more realistic scenarios (higher resolution, more complex functions, different architectures) is crucial to assess the practical impact.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem statement and methodology.
    * Well-defined experiments with baselines.
    * Demonstrates that ICL is possible in a limited image context.
    * Shows potential for data-efficient learning.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Limited experimental scope (small images, simple functions).
    * Results might not generalize to more complex scenarios.
    * No discussion of computational cost/efficiency of ICL vs. standard training.
    * Lacks comparison with state-of-the-art few-shot learning approaches.
    * The concept of ICL here differs from the LLM understanding of ICL. The models in the paper are more akin to meta-learners.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a stepping stone for further research into ICL for vision tasks. It highlights the ability of transformers to learn convolutional mappings in-context, which could inspire new approaches to few-shot or zero-shot learning in vision. However, the practical implications are limited without demonstrating scalability.

**Overall:**

While the paper is a worthwhile exploration of a promising area, its novelty and significance are constrained by the narrow scope of its experiments and the simple target functions considered.  The use of the term 'in-context learning' is also slightly misleading as the models are really approximating meta-learners.  More extensive experimentation and comparison with existing methods would be needed to fully assess the potential of the proposed approach.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### Fork-Merge Decoding: Enhancing Multimodal Understanding in Audio-Visual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20873v1)
- **Authors**: Chaeyoung Jung, Youngjoon Jang, Jongmin Choi, Joon Son Chung
- **Abstract**: The goal of this work is to enhance balanced multimodal understanding in audio-visual large language models (AV-LLMs) by addressing modality bias without requiring additional training. In current AV-LLMs, audio and video features are typically processed jointly in the decoder. While this strategy facilitates unified multimodal understanding, it may introduce modality bias, where the model tends to over-rely on one modality due to imbalanced training signals. To mitigate this, we propose Fork-Merge Decoding (FMD), a simple yet effective inference-time strategy that requires no additional training or architectural modifications. FMD first performs modality-specific reasoning by processing audio-only and video-only inputs through the early decoder layers (a fork phase), and then merges the resulting hidden states for joint reasoning in the remaining layers (a merge phase). This approach promotes balanced modality contributions and leverages complementary information across modalities. We evaluate our method on two representative AV-LLMs, VideoLLaMA2 and video-SALMONN, using three benchmark datasets. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements on tasks focused on audio, video, and combined audio-visual reasoning, demonstrating the effectiveness of inference-time interventions for robust multimodal understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Fork-Merge Decoding" (FMD), a novel inference-time strategy to enhance multimodal understanding in audio-visual large language models (AV-LLMs). FMD addresses modality bias by processing audio and video features separately in the early decoder layers (the "fork" phase) before merging them for joint reasoning in later layers (the "merge" phase). This approach, requiring no additional training or architectural changes, aims to balance modality contributions and leverage complementary information. The authors evaluate FMD on VideoLLaMA2 and video-SALMONN using standard benchmarks (AVQA, MUSIC-AVQA, AVHBench), demonstrating consistent performance improvements, especially on tasks heavily reliant on audio.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of FMD is relatively simple but represents a practical and effective inference-time intervention. Separating the modalities early in the decoding process to allow for modality-specific reasoning before fusion is a reasonable approach to mitigate modality bias. While the individual components (modality separation, attention-guided fusion) aren't entirely novel on their own, their combination into a training-free, model-agnostic decoding strategy for AV-LLMs is a distinguishing factor.
    The comparison with DoLA, VCD and SID reveals that the proposed technique is tailored to AV-LLMs as it outperforms the existing techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important issue in AV-LLMs: modality bias. Over-reliance on one modality (typically video) can lead to hallucinations and flawed reasoning. FMD offers a way to improve the robustness and reliability of these models without incurring the cost of retraining. The consistent improvements across multiple models and datasets highlight the potential for FMD to be a valuable tool for improving AV-LLMs. The fact that it's training-free makes it readily applicable to existing models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity and Practicality:** The method is easy to implement and doesn't require any training or architectural changes.
    *   **Generality:** FMD can be applied to both token-wise and channel-wise fusion-based AV-LLMs.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The authors provide solid experimental results on a variety of benchmarks, demonstrating consistent performance improvements.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The layer selection analysis and ablation studies justify the design choices made in FMD.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are strong, the theoretical underpinning of why FMD works is relatively shallow. A more in-depth analysis of the attention mechanisms and hidden state representations could provide more insight.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:**  The Lfork parameter requires experimentation and tuning, adding a potential implementation hurdle.
    *   **Modality Weighting:** The proposed setting of fixed weights for integrating visual and audio is questionable. The use of attention scores derived from the final transformer layer to establish this weighting factor can be suboptimal. The effectiveness is confined to cases wherein each modality is sufficiently balanced and informative for each task.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the design and deployment of AV-LLMs. The ease of implementation and training-free nature of FMD could lead to its widespread adoption as a standard decoding strategy. It may also inspire further research into more sophisticated inference-time interventions.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and practical technique to address a crucial challenge in AV-LLMs. The consistent empirical gains and the simplicity of FMD are significant strengths. The limitations, such as the lack of in-depth theoretical analysis and reliance on tuned parameters, are valid concerns. While the novelty might not be groundbreaking, the practical impact and potential for widespread use justify a score of 7. The study's ability to generalize across two models and three standard benchmarks supports its robustness.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can LLMs Learn to Map the World from Local Descriptions?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20874v1)
- **Authors**: Sirui Xia, Aili Chen, Xintao Wang, Tinghui Zhu, Yikai Zhang, Jiangjie Chen, Yanghua Xiao
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in tasks such as code and mathematics. However, their potential to internalize structured spatial knowledge remains underexplored. This study investigates whether LLMs, grounded in locally relative human observations, can construct coherent global spatial cognition by integrating fragmented relational descriptions. We focus on two core aspects of spatial cognition: spatial perception, where models infer consistent global layouts from local positional relationships, and spatial navigation, where models learn road connectivity from trajectory data and plan optimal paths between unconnected locations. Experiments conducted in a simulated urban environment demonstrate that LLMs not only generalize to unseen spatial relationships between points of interest (POIs) but also exhibit latent representations aligned with real-world spatial distributions. Furthermore, LLMs can learn road connectivity from trajectory descriptions, enabling accurate path planning and dynamic spatial awareness during navigation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Can LLMs Learn to Map the World from Local Descriptions?"

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can construct coherent global spatial representations by integrating fragmented, locally relative human observations expressed in natural language. The research focuses on two core aspects: spatial perception (inferring consistent global layouts from local positional relationships) and spatial navigation (learning road connectivity from trajectory descriptions to plan optimal paths).  Experiments are conducted in a simulated urban environment, demonstrating that LLMs can generalize to unseen spatial relationships between Points of Interest (POIs) and exhibit latent representations aligned with real-world spatial distributions. Furthermore, the study finds that LLMs can learn road connectivity from trajectory descriptions, enabling accurate path planning and dynamic spatial awareness during navigation. However, their robustness to navigational perturbations is limited.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses an interesting and relatively unexplored area: the ability of LLMs to implicitly acquire structured spatial knowledge from natural language descriptions *without* explicit coordinate information. While prior work has explored LLMs and spatial information, this paper focuses on the *construction* of a global map from fragmented, linguistic cues, going beyond simply memorizing or recalling facts. The two-stage training approach, separating spatial perception from navigation, is a well-designed methodology for investigating this. However, the use of a simulated environment, while offering control, somewhat reduces the novelty of the findings regarding real-world applicability.

*   **Significance:** If LLMs can indeed build internal, structured spatial maps from language, this has significant implications for various applications. It could lead to more intuitive human-computer interaction, improve robotic navigation, and enhance AI systems' ability to reason about and plan in the real world. The paper's demonstration of implicit spatial abstraction within LLMs (encoding absolute coordinates in latent space) is particularly intriguing and opens up further avenues for research.  The analysis of robustness to perturbations, while revealing limitations, is important for highlighting the challenges in developing truly reliable spatial understanding.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined research questions and a clear experimental setup.
    *   Comprehensive analysis using explicit prediction tasks, latent representation analysis, and robustness evaluation.
    *   Demonstration of implicit spatial knowledge acquisition within LLMs.
    *   Rigorous control of the training data.
    *   Clear and concise writing.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The simulated environment, while providing control, limits the generalizability of the findings to real-world scenarios. Real-world language is far more ambiguous and complex than the templated descriptions used in the study.
    *   The reliance on simplified road networks also restricts the scope.
    *   Limited exploration of different LLM architectures and scales (beyond the selected Qwen model). Although additional experiments used LLama model, only 1 of the Qwen and Llama models were used.
    *   The robustness analysis reveals a significant vulnerability to perturbations, suggesting that the model's understanding of road network topology is somewhat fragile.
    *   Lack of in-depth comparison or extension of prior knowledge graph completion type tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research in this area. It provides a solid foundation for exploring how LLMs can acquire and utilize spatial knowledge from language. Future work could focus on:
    *   Evaluating the approach with real-world datasets and more complex language.
    *   Developing more robust spatial navigation capabilities.
    *   Investigating how to integrate spatial knowledge with other forms of reasoning and planning.
    *   Investigating the use of prompting or external tools (other KG data) to enhance spatial reasoning.
    *   Performing the reverse experiment of extracting spatial information from images

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of LLMs to implicitly learn structured spatial knowledge from natural language. While the limitations of the simulated environment and robustness to perturbations need to be addressed, the research provides a solid foundation for future work in this area.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel investigation into a potentially significant capability of LLMs. The methodology is sound, and the results are interesting. However, the limitations related to the simulated environment and the fragility of the learned representations prevent a higher score. The paper provides important insights but requires further validation and extension before its full impact can be realized.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Trans-EnV: A Framework for Evaluating the Linguistic Robustness of LLMs Against English Varieties
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20875v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyoung Lee, Seungho Kim, Jieun Han, Jun-Min Lee, Kitaek Kim, Alice Oh, Edward Choi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are predominantly evaluated on Standard American English (SAE), often overlooking the diversity of global English varieties. This narrow focus may raise fairness concerns as degraded performance on non-standard varieties can lead to unequal benefits for users worldwide. Therefore, it is critical to extensively evaluate the linguistic robustness of LLMs on multiple non-standard English varieties. We introduce Trans-EnV, a framework that automatically transforms SAE datasets into multiple English varieties to evaluate the linguistic robustness. Our framework combines (1) linguistics expert knowledge to curate variety-specific features and transformation guidelines from linguistic literature and corpora, and (2) LLM-based transformations to ensure both linguistic validity and scalability. Using Trans-EnV, we transform six benchmark datasets into 38 English varieties and evaluate seven state-of-the-art LLMs. Our results reveal significant performance disparities, with accuracy decreasing by up to 46.3% on non-standard varieties. These findings highlight the importance of comprehensive linguistic robustness evaluation across diverse English varieties. Each construction of Trans-EnV was validated through rigorous statistical testing and consultation with a researcher in the field of second language acquisition, ensuring its linguistic validity. Our \href{https://github.com/jiyounglee-0523/TransEnV}{code} and \href{https://huggingface.co/collections/jiyounglee0523/transenv-681eadb3c0c8cf363b363fb1}{datasets} are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Trans-EnV: A Framework for Evaluating the Linguistic Robustness of LLMs Against English Varieties":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Trans-EnV, a framework for automatically transforming Standard American English (SAE) datasets into multiple English varieties (dialects and ESL English) to evaluate the linguistic robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs).  Trans-EnV combines expert linguistic knowledge with LLM-based transformations to create datasets in 38 English varieties, which are then used to benchmark several state-of-the-art LLMs.  The results reveal significant performance disparities, particularly for ESL English, highlighting weaknesses in LLMs' ability to handle non-standard English. The paper emphasizes the importance of evaluating LLMs across diverse linguistic varieties to address fairness concerns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its *automated* approach to generating diverse English variety datasets using a combination of linguistic rules and LLM transformations.  Previous works often relied on manual curation or simple rule-based methods, which are less scalable and comprehensive.  The specific methodology of using an LLM for guided transformation (following linguistics expert knowledge) seems relatively novel in this context.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue: the lack of linguistic diversity in LLM evaluation. This is significant for fairness, accessibility, and ensuring equitable benefits from LLMs for users globally. By demonstrating that LLMs perform significantly worse on non-standard varieties, the paper provides compelling evidence for the need for more robust and inclusive evaluation practices. It also opens up new avenues for research to address LLM robustness issues specifically related to language variations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The framework covers a significant range of English varieties, considering both regional dialects and ESL English influenced by various L1s.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The combination of expert knowledge, LLM-based transformation, and semantic validation enhances the reliability of the generated datasets.
    *   **Scalability:** Leveraging LLMs for guideline implementation and sentence transformation enables scalability to multiple datasets and varieties.
    *   **Clear Experimental Results:** The results convincingly demonstrate the performance gaps between SAE and non-SAE varieties, providing valuable insights into LLM limitations.
    *   **Careful Data Collection:**  Efforts to validate the linguistic features and ensure data quality (e.g., using a researcher in second language acquisition) add to the credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:**  While LLMs are used to enhance scalability, the validity of the transformed sentences heavily depends on the capabilities and biases of the LLMs. Although the use of large LLMs such as Gemma-2-27B-Instruct [62] and LLaMA-3.3-70B-Instruct [23] as the feature transformer model T, and the semantic checker model S respectively could potentially have had some negative effect on the outcome of the data, they are not expected to be as biased as smaller models, potentially making it an avenue for future research, testing other models.
    *   **Potential for Oversimplification:** The simplification of vocabulary for ESL English, while aiming for realistic simulation, may still not fully capture the complexities of ESL learner language.
    *   **Evaluation Scope:** The evaluation is limited to QA tasks. While informative, it does not fully capture the potential impact of linguistic variations on other LLM applications (e.g., creative writing, summarization).
    *   **Limited Analysis of Error Types:**  The paper focuses on overall performance degradation but provides limited analysis of specific error types made by LLMs on different varieties. A deeper dive into these errors could provide valuable insights for targeted improvement.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence research in LLM evaluation and development.  It provides a valuable resource (Trans-EnV) for researchers to assess the linguistic robustness of LLMs.  It also highlights the need for more inclusive datasets and training strategies that address linguistic diversity.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, its strengths far outweigh its weaknesses. The automated approach to creating diverse language datasets, the compelling empirical evidence, and the potential influence on the field make it a significant contribution. The work is well-executed and addresses a critical gap in LLM research.

### Research on a Two-Layer Demand Response Framework for Electric Vehicle Users and Aggregators Based on LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20877v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaoyi Zhang, Chenggang Cui, Ning Yang, Chuanlin Zhang
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of electric vehicles (EVs) has increased the importance of demand response in smart grids. This paper proposes a two-layer demand response optimization framework for EV users and aggregators, leveraging large language models (LLMs) to balance electricity supply and demand and optimize energy utilization during EV charging. The upper-layer model, focusing on the aggregator, aims to maximize profits by adjusting retail electricity prices. The lower-layer model targets EV users, using LLMs to simulate charging demands under varying electricity prices and optimize both costs and user comfort. The study employs a multi-threaded LLM decision generator to dynamically analyze user behavior, charging preferences, and psychological factors. The framework utilizes the PSO method to optimize electricity prices, ensuring user needs are met while increasing aggregator profits. Simulation results show that the proposed model improves EV charging efficiency, alleviates peak power loads, and stabilizes smart grid operations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a two-layer demand response (DR) framework for electric vehicle (EV) charging that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework aims to optimize both the aggregator's profit and user satisfaction. The upper layer focuses on the aggregator, adjusting retail electricity prices to maximize profit. The lower layer targets EV users, employing LLMs to simulate charging demands and optimize costs and comfort.  A multi-agent LLM simulates user decision-making based on user profiles, environmental factors, and pricing signals. The Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) algorithm is used to optimize electricity prices while considering user needs.  Simulation results demonstrate improved EV charging efficiency, reduced peak power loads, and enhanced grid stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the application of LLMs to model and simulate EV user charging behavior within a demand response framework. While demand response and LLMs have been individually explored in the context of energy management, their integration in this specific two-layer framework for EV charging is a unique contribution.  The use of a multi-agent system driven by an LLM to capture the heterogeneity of EV user behavior also appears novel.  Previous demand response approaches often rely on simplified user models or statistical averages, lacking the nuanced understanding of individual behaviors that an LLM can potentially provide.  However, the paper doesn't sufficiently compare its approach with the existing literature.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the challenges posed by the increasing adoption of EVs and the need for efficient grid management.  Accurately modeling user charging behavior is crucial for effective demand response, and the LLM-based approach has the potential to improve upon existing methods.  A significant potential impact is improved grid stability and reduction of infrastructure costs associated with managing peak loads. The framework's ability to balance aggregator profits with user comfort is also important for ensuring user participation in demand response programs.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Integration of LLMs:**  The use of LLMs for user behavior modeling is a promising approach that can capture complexities and individual preferences that are often ignored in traditional models.
    * **Two-Layer Optimization:** The two-layer framework allows for a holistic optimization considering both aggregator and user perspectives.
    * **Dynamic Adaptation:** The framework's ability to dynamically adjust to real-time electricity prices, weather conditions, and user travel demands enhances its adaptability and practicality.
    * **Multi-agent Modeling:** Capturing heterogeneous user behavior using a multi-agent framework enhances realism.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper relies heavily on simulations. While the simulation results are promising, they need to be validated with real-world data to assess the model's accuracy and effectiveness.
    * **Limited Detail on LLM Implementation:** The paper lacks specific details regarding the LLM's architecture, training data, and fine-tuning process. This makes it difficult to reproduce the results and assess the robustness of the approach.
    * **Scalability Concerns:**  The computational complexity of using LLMs for simulating a large number of EV users is not addressed.  The scalability of the proposed framework needs to be evaluated.
    * **Comparison to Baselines:**  The paper could be strengthened by comparing its performance against more traditional demand response methods or user modeling approaches. It is difficult to quantitatively assess the value added by the LLM without a benchmark.
    * **Simplification of the grid model:** The model does not model the distribution network and considers a single node. This could limit the paper's impact on practical applications.

* **Potential Influence:**  If validated with real-world data, the proposed framework could influence the design and implementation of future demand response programs for EVs. It could also inspire further research on the application of LLMs in energy management.

* **Overall Assessment:** While the paper presents a novel approach with promising potential, the lack of empirical validation and specific details regarding the LLM implementation limits its significance. A strong comparison with existing methods is missing and needed to prove its merit.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: eess.SY
- **Score**: 6/10

### MSA at SemEval-2025 Task 3: High Quality Weak Labeling and LLM Ensemble Verification for Multilingual Hallucination Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20880v1)
- **Authors**: Baraa Hikal, Ahmed Nasreldin, Ali Hamdi
- **Abstract**: This paper describes our submission for SemEval-2025 Task 3: Mu-SHROOM, the Multilingual Shared-task on Hallucinations and Related Observable Overgeneration Mistakes. The task involves detecting hallucinated spans in text generated by instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) across multiple languages. Our approach combines task-specific prompt engineering with an LLM ensemble verification mechanism, where a primary model extracts hallucination spans and three independent LLMs adjudicate their validity through probability-based voting. This framework simulates the human annotation workflow used in the shared task validation and test data. Additionally, fuzzy matching refines span alignment. Our system ranked 1st in Arabic and Basque, 2nd in German, Swedish, and Finnish, and 3rd in Czech, Farsi, and French.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a system for detecting hallucinations in text generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) across multiple languages. The system, designed for SemEval-2025 Task 3 (Mu-SHROOM), utilizes a combination of task-specific prompt engineering to generate weak labels, an LLM ensemble verification mechanism (where one LLM proposes spans and others adjudicate), and fuzzy matching for refined span alignment. The ensemble simulates human annotation review. The authors achieved top rankings in several languages in the competition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Ensemble Approach:** The use of an LLM ensemble for verification is a strong point. By having multiple LLMs "vote" on the presence of hallucinations, the system reduces model bias and achieves more robust and reliable hallucination detection.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The emphasis on carefully engineered prompts is crucial for effective weak label generation. It demonstrates an understanding of how to elicit the best performance from LLMs for the specific task.
    *   **Fuzzy Matching:** Incorporating fuzzy matching to refine span alignment is a practical and effective way to address the minor inconsistencies that LLMs often introduce. This enhances the precision of hallucination detection.
    *   **Multi-Lingual Focus:** Addressing hallucination detection across multiple languages is valuable, especially given the challenges posed by morphological complexity and annotation inconsistencies in different languages. The insights gleaned from this multi-lingual approach are pertinent to future research.
    *   **Simulation of Human Annotation:** By simulating the human annotation process with LLMs, the study directly addresses and attempts to mitigate human annotation biases.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Novelty in Basic Approach:** The core ideas of using ensembles and prompt engineering are not entirely novel in the field of NLP and hallucination detection. Many other papers also leverage similar techniques. The key novelty should be in the specific implementation details and demonstrated effectiveness.
    *   **Reliance on Proprietary LLMs:** The paper relies heavily on specific LLMs (Gemini, Qwen, GPT-4, DeepSeek), making the system somewhat dependent on these specific models. If these models change or become unavailable, the system may need significant re-tuning.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation relies primarily on competition rankings. While these rankings are useful, a more comprehensive evaluation with ablation studies (e.g., showing the impact of the ensemble vs. a single model, the effect of fuzzy matching) would strengthen the paper significantly. This would allow a deeper understanding of what aspects of their system contribute the most to its success.
    *   **Annotation Consistency:** The paper identifies annotation variability as a weakness, and it would be beneficial to address this in more detail. Were annotation guidelines refined to better reflect observed biases across the individual LLMs during ensemble voting, or did the task rely heavily on post hoc mitigation of differences through fuzzy matching?

*   **Novelty and Significance:** While the paper presents a competent system that performs well in a shared task, its *novelty* is incremental rather than revolutionary. It combines existing techniques (ensembles, prompts, fuzzy matching) in a specific way for multilingual hallucination detection. The *significance* lies in its practical application and the insights gained about language-specific hallucination challenges. The simulation of human annotation also provides a valuable framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence will depend on how widely the specific techniques are adopted. The focus on multi-lingual hallucination detection is becoming increasingly relevant and may drive further research in this area. Open-sourcing the code and prompts would also significantly enhance its impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, I assign a score of **6**. This reflects the paper's strengths in ensemble verification, practical prompt engineering, fuzzy matching, and addressing a relevant problem in a multi-lingual context. However, the incremental novelty compared to prior art and the limited comprehensive evaluation, particularly without more detail on annotation, prevent a higher score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Generalizable Heuristic Generation Through Large Language Models with Meta-Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20881v1)
- **Authors**: Yiding Shi, Jianan Zhou, Wen Song, Jieyi Bi, Yaoxin Wu, Jie Zhang
- **Abstract**: Heuristic design with large language models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising approach for tackling combinatorial optimization problems (COPs). However, existing approaches often rely on manually predefined evolutionary computation (EC) optimizers and single-task training schemes, which may constrain the exploration of diverse heuristic algorithms and hinder the generalization of the resulting heuristics. To address these issues, we propose Meta-Optimization of Heuristics (MoH), a novel framework that operates at the optimizer level, discovering effective optimizers through the principle of meta-learning. Specifically, MoH leverages LLMs to iteratively refine a meta-optimizer that autonomously constructs diverse optimizers through (self-)invocation, thereby eliminating the reliance on a predefined EC optimizer. These constructed optimizers subsequently evolve heuristics for downstream tasks, enabling broader heuristic exploration. Moreover, MoH employs a multi-task training scheme to promote its generalization capability. Experiments on classic COPs demonstrate that MoH constructs an effective and interpretable meta-optimizer, achieving state-of-the-art performance across various downstream tasks, particularly in cross-size settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Generalizable Heuristic Generation Through Large Language Models with Meta-Optimization":

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a novel framework called Meta-Optimization of Heuristics (MoH) for generating effective and generalizable heuristics for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs). Unlike existing approaches that rely on manually predefined evolutionary computation (EC) optimizers and single-task training, MoH operates at the *optimizer* level. It uses large language models (LLMs) to iteratively refine a meta-optimizer, which then autonomously constructs diverse optimizers through self-invocation. These constructed optimizers evolve heuristics for downstream tasks in a multi-task training setting, promoting generalization. Experiments on classic COPs like TSP and online BPP demonstrate that MoH constructs effective and interpretable meta-optimizers that achieve state-of-the-art performance, especially in cross-size settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the meta-optimization approach for heuristic generation. Instead of directly using LLMs to generate or refine heuristics with a fixed EC framework, MoH focuses on *designing* the EC framework itself. This is a significant shift in perspective, allowing for broader exploration of the heuristic search space. The concept of recursively self-improving the optimizer using the LLM's reasoning capabilities is compelling. While other works have explored automated algorithm design, the application of LLMs to meta-optimize heuristic *generation*, particularly with the self-invocation mechanism, is a notable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to automate and improve the design of heuristics for solving COPs. Traditional heuristic design is time-consuming and requires specialized expertise. MoH aims to alleviate these limitations by leveraging the power of LLMs. The demonstrated improvement in generalization performance, particularly across different problem sizes, is a strong indicator of MoH's practical value. Furthermore, by generating interpretable optimizers, MoH offers insights into effective heuristic design strategies. The emphasis on multi-task learning for generalization is also a significant contribution to the literature on learning-based heuristics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined and clearly articulated problem setting and approach.
    *   Solid experimental validation on standard COP benchmarks.
    *   Demonstrated superiority over existing LLM-based and traditional methods.
    *   Emphasis on interpretability through analysis of the generated meta-optimizers.
    *   Addresses limitations of existing LLM-EC frameworks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper briefly mentions computational cost in Appendix B, it would benefit from a more thorough and detailed analysis. The meta-optimization process involving iterative LLM calls is likely computationally intensive and expensive. A discussion of the practical limitations imposed by these costs is warranted.
    *   **LLM Reliance:** The framework relies heavily on the capabilities of the LLM. Performance may be susceptible to the specific LLM used, its version, and the prompt engineering used. A more explicit acknowledgement and discussion of these dependencies is needed. Also, because the project is using close-source LLM(s), it would limit the reproducibility.
    *   **Scalability:** While the paper shows good generalization, it is limited to TSP and BPP. The applicability and effectiveness of MoH on more complex or high-dimensional COPs, or those with specific problem constraints, is not fully explored. The generalizability on real-world datasets remains unclear.
    *   **Ablation of meta-optimizer:** It will be valuable to run an ablation to understand the role and effectiveness of using LLM to refine a meta-optimizer. E.g., Does MoH still work if the initial seed optimizers are randomly changed, or if LLM is replaced by random mutation?

*   **Potential Influence:** If further validated and scaled, MoH has the potential to influence the design of heuristics for COPs in various application domains. It could reduce the reliance on human expertise and accelerate the development of effective solutions for previously intractable problems. The focus on generalizability could also lead to the development of more robust and adaptable heuristics that can perform well across diverse problem settings.

*   **Justification of Score:** MoH represents a valuable advance in LLM-driven heuristic generation through its innovative meta-optimization approach. While the paper has some limitations regarding computational cost, scalability, and LLM reliance, the strong experimental results and the shift in perspective warrant a strong score. It's likely not a perfect 10 (truly transformative change), but the work presents a meaningful and significant shift in automated heuristic generation.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 10/10

### EasyDistill: A Comprehensive Toolkit for Effective Knowledge Distillation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20888v1)
- **Authors**: Chengyu Wang, Junbing Yan, Wenrui Cai, Yuanhao Yue, Jun Huang
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we present EasyDistill, a comprehensive toolkit designed for effective black-box and white-box knowledge distillation (KD) of large language models (LLMs). Our framework offers versatile functionalities, including data synthesis, supervised fine-tuning, ranking optimization, and reinforcement learning techniques specifically tailored for KD scenarios. The toolkit accommodates KD functionalities for both System 1 (fast, intuitive) and System 2 (slow, analytical) models. With its modular design and user-friendly interface, EasyDistill empowers researchers and industry practitioners to seamlessly experiment with and implement state-of-the-art KD strategies for LLMs. In addition, EasyDistill provides a series of robust distilled models and KD-based industrial solutions developed by us, along with the corresponding open-sourced datasets, catering to a variety of use cases. Furthermore, we describe the seamless integration of EasyDistill into Alibaba Cloud's Platform for AI (PAI). Overall, the EasyDistill toolkit makes advanced KD techniques for LLMs more accessible and impactful within the NLP community.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation, including a score and a detailed justification, of the provided paper titled "EasyDistill: A Comprehensive Toolkit for Effective Knowledge Distillation of Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EasyDistill, a comprehensive toolkit designed to simplify and improve the effectiveness of knowledge distillation (KD) for large language models (LLMs). EasyDistill supports both black-box and white-box KD, offering functionalities such as data synthesis, supervised fine-tuning (SFT), ranking optimization, and reinforcement learning (RL). The toolkit accommodates both System 1 (fast, intuitive) and System 2 (slow, analytical) models, making it versatile for various LLM architectures. The authors emphasize ease of use, modularity, and extensibility. The paper also highlights the inclusion of robust distilled models (DistilQwen series), open-sourced datasets, KD-based industrial solutions, and integration with Alibaba Cloud's Platform for AI (PAI), thus bridging the gap between research and practical application.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Functionality:** EasyDistill appears to offer a wide range of tools and functionalities that cover various aspects of KD, which can be beneficial for both researchers and practitioners. The inclusion of data synthesis, SFT, ranking optimization, and RL techniques within a single toolkit is a definite plus.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The inclusion of pre-trained distilled models (DistilQwen), open-sourced datasets, and KD-based industrial solutions (EasyDistill-Recipes) is a significant strength. It provides practical examples and starting points for users, potentially accelerating the adoption of KD techniques. Integration with Alibaba Cloud's PAI also enhances its real-world deployability.
    *   **Addresses a Real Need:** The paper correctly identifies the challenges associated with effectively applying KD to LLMs, especially regarding the complexity and required expertise. A user-friendly toolkit that simplifies these processes is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results (Implied):** The paper refers to associated model cards on HuggingFace, suggesting that the developed `DistilQwen` models exhibit favorable performance compared to their non-distilled counterparts.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Technical Depth in the Paper:** The paper provides a high-level overview of the toolkit's functionalities, but lacks in-depth technical details about the implementation of each KD algorithm or the specific architectures of the distilled models. More technical details and equations would lend greater weight to the contribution.
    *   **Lack of Concrete Performance Comparisons:** While the paper refers to benchmark results and performance of the DistilQwen models, the main body of the paper doesn't showcase these comparisons directly. Including a table or graph within the paper itself would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** Many of the KD algorithms and techniques incorporated into EasyDistill are not entirely new. The novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of these methods into a single, user-friendly toolkit, combined with practical solutions.
    *   **Dependency on Alibaba Cloud's PAI:** While the PAI integration is a practical advantage, it also creates a certain level of lock-in. Users not on Alibaba Cloud might find this integration less relevant.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** While the paper mentions ethical considerations briefly, a more thorough discussion regarding potential biases in LLMs after KD would be beneficial.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The primary novelty of EasyDistill is the *systematic integration* of various KD techniques, practical recipes, and resources into a unified, user-friendly toolkit. While individual KD algorithms might not be groundbreaking, the accessibility and practical orientation of EasyDistill can significantly impact the NLP community. The open-sourcing of datasets and pre-trained models lowers the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners interested in exploring KD for LLMs. The toolkit streamlines and democratizes the KD process. However, this value depends significantly on the thoroughness of implementation and robustness of the provided models and solutions.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    EasyDistill has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Accelerating research in KD for LLMs by providing a readily available platform for experimentation.
    *   Facilitating the adoption of KD techniques in industry, enabling the development of more efficient and deployable LLM-based applications.
    *   Promoting open-source development and collaboration in the KD community.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. This score reflects the paper's strong practical value and potential to accelerate KD research and deployment. However, the limited technical depth in the paper, lack of detailed performance comparisons within the text, and the incremental nature of the algorithmic contributions prevent it from achieving a higher score. The integration of diverse KD techniques into a single tool and the inclusion of practical recipes and resources are strong points, suggesting that EasyDistill could significantly improve the accessibility and usability of KD for LLMs. However, the actual impact will hinge on the robustness of the implementation and the quality of the provided models and datasets. A higher score could be justified with more detailed performance evaluations within the paper itself.

**Score: 7**
