### Why Distillation can Outperform Zero-RL: The Role of Flexible Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21067v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Hu, Xingyu Lu, Liyuan Mao, YiFan Zhang, Tianke Zhang, Bin Wen, Fan Yang, Tingting Gao, Guorui Zhou
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has played an important role in improving the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs). Some studies apply RL directly to \textit{smaller} base models (known as zero-RL) and also achieve notable progress. However, in this paper, we show that using only 920 examples, a simple distillation method based on the base model can clearly outperform zero-RL, which typically requires much more data and computational cost. By analyzing the token frequency in model outputs, we find that the distilled model shows more flexible reasoning. It uses anthropomorphic tokens and logical connectors much more often than the zero-RL model. Further analysis reveals that distillation enhances the presence of two advanced cognitive behaviors: Multi-Perspective Thinking or Attempting and Metacognitive Awareness. Frequent occurrences of these two advanced cognitive behaviors give rise to flexible reasoning, which is essential for solving complex reasoning problems, while zero-RL fails to significantly boost the frequency of these behaviors.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates why distillation, using a small amount of high-quality data, can outperform zero-shot Reinforcement Learning (zero-RL) in improving the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs). Focusing on the Qwen2.5-32B model, the authors demonstrate that a distilled model, fine-tuned on only 920 examples generated by DeepSeek R1, surpasses existing state-of-the-art zero-RL models on reasoning-intensive benchmarks, despite zero-RL typically requiring significantly more data and computational resources. Through token frequency analysis, the authors find that the distilled model exhibits more flexible reasoning, characterized by greater use of anthropomorphic tokens and logical connectors, mirroring the linguistic style of its teacher model. Further investigation reveals that distillation enhances the presence of two advanced cognitive behaviors: Multi-Perspective Thinking/Attempting and Metacognitive Awareness. The paper shows these behaviors are crucial for tackling complex reasoning problems and that distillation is more effective than zero-RL in fostering them.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its demonstration that distillation, using a small, carefully curated dataset, can surpass the performance of zero-RL in enhancing reasoning abilities in LLMs. The analysis of token frequency and identification of Multi-Perspective Thinking and Metacognitive Awareness as key factors contributing to this improvement also offer novel insights. However, the basic idea of distillation is well-established, and zero-RL has a lot of hyperparameters that could affect the results.

* **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the development of LLMs.  The paper challenges the prevailing notion that extensive data and computational resources are always necessary for improving reasoning capabilities.  It suggests that focusing on high-quality data and potentially simpler techniques like distillation can be more effective and efficient. The identification and analysis of advanced cognitive behaviors opens up new avenues for research into improving LLM reasoning. However, the benchmarks used are very limited.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Research Question:** The paper addresses a specific and well-defined research question: Why can distillation outperform zero-RL with less data?
    * **Strong Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides convincing empirical evidence to support its claims, using established benchmarks and carefully controlled experiments.
    * **Detailed Analysis:** The token frequency analysis and the identification of advanced cognitive behaviors offer valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms of improved reasoning.
    * **Reproducibility:** The paper provides sufficient details regarding the experimental setup, making it easier for other researchers to reproduce the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model Scope:** The study focuses primarily on a single base model (Qwen2.5-32B) and one teacher model (DeepSeek R1).  Extending the analysis to other models would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    * **Narrow Task Domain:** While the benchmarks used are reasoning-intensive, they are primarily focused on mathematical problems.  Investigating other domains (e.g., commonsense reasoning, coding) would broaden the scope of the study.
    * **Limited Exploration of Zero-RL Parameters:** The paper does not extensively explore different parameter settings or optimization techniques for the zero-RL models. It's possible that better-tuned zero-RL models could achieve higher performance.
    * **Dependence on GPT-4o for Cognitive Behavior Analysis:**  The use of GPT-4o to identify advanced cognitive behaviors introduces a potential source of bias or subjectivity.  A more objective or automated method for identifying these behaviors would be desirable.
    * **Oversimplification of "Reward Hacking":**  The discussion of reward hacking in zero-RL is somewhat brief.  A more in-depth analysis of the specific mechanisms and mitigation strategies for reward hacking would be valuable.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of LLM research by shifting the focus towards data quality and simpler techniques like distillation. The insights into advanced cognitive behaviors could lead to the development of more targeted training methods for improving reasoning abilities. The paper may also encourage researchers to re-evaluate the data and computational requirements for achieving significant performance gains in LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** While the paper makes a novel and significant contribution by demonstrating the potential of distillation with limited high-quality data to outperform zero-RL in LLM reasoning, its scope is somewhat limited by the use of a single base model, a narrow task domain, and a reliance on GPT-4o for cognitive behavior analysis. The paper also has the potential to influence future research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### CXXCrafter: An LLM-Based Agent for Automated C/C++ Open Source Software Building
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21069v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengmin Yu, Yuan Zhang, Ming Wen, Yinan Nie, Wenhui Zhang, Min Yang
- **Abstract**: Project building is pivotal to support various program analysis tasks, such as generating intermediate rep- resentation code for static analysis and preparing binary code for vulnerability reproduction. However, automating the building process for C/C++ projects is a highly complex endeavor, involving tremendous technical challenges, such as intricate dependency management, diverse build systems, varied toolchains, and multifaceted error handling mechanisms. Consequently, building C/C++ projects often proves to be difficult in practice, hindering the progress of downstream applications. Unfortunately, research on facilitating the building of C/C++ projects remains to be inadequate. The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) offers promising solutions to automated software building. Trained on extensive corpora, LLMs can help unify diverse build systems through their comprehension capabilities and address complex errors by leveraging tacit knowledge storage. Moreover, LLM-based agents can be systematically designed to dynamically interact with the environment, effectively managing dynamic building issues. Motivated by these opportunities, we first conduct an empirical study to systematically analyze the current challenges in the C/C++ project building process. Particularly, we observe that most popular C/C++ projects encounter an average of five errors when relying solely on the default build systems. Based on our study, we develop an automated build system called CXXCrafter to specifically address the above-mentioned challenges, such as dependency resolution. Our evaluation on open-source software demonstrates that CXXCrafter achieves a success rate of 78% in project building. Specifically, among the Top100 dataset, 72 projects are built successfully by both CXXCrafter and manual efforts, 3 by CXXCrafter only, and 14 manually only. ...
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CXXCrafter: An LLM-Based Agent for Automated C/C++ Open Source Software Building":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenging problem of automatically building C/C++ open-source software (OSS). The authors observe that building C/C++ projects is often complex and prone to errors due to intricate dependencies, diverse build systems, and varied toolchains.  They propose CXXCrafter, an LLM-based agent designed to automate this process. CXXCrafter consists of three modules: a Parser Module to extract relevant information from the repository, a Generator Module leveraging LLMs to create Dockerfiles (build solutions), and an Executor Module that executes the build and provides feedback. The Generator and Executor modules operate in a dynamic interaction loop, iteratively addressing build issues.  The authors evaluate CXXCrafter on a dataset of popular C/C++ OSS projects and demonstrate a significant improvement in build success rates compared to default build commands and bare LLMs. They also analyze the contribution of each module and the efficiency/cost of the system. Finally, the paper discusses potential applications of CXXCrafter in downstream software analysis tasks like vulnerability reproduction and fuzzing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using an LLM *agent* for automating C/C++ builds shows promise, and combining static analysis (Parser) with the iterative feedback loop driven by an LLM (Generator and Executor) represents a sensible design. It distinguishes the work from simply using LLMs for code generation (e.g., build scripts). The authors claim to be the first to explore LLM agents to automate C/C++ builds, however, the combination of LLMs with automated agents for building projects has been explored previously.

*   **Significance:** The automation of C/C++ build processes has significant practical value.  Successful automation can benefit various downstream tasks such as static and dynamic analysis, vulnerability research, and security auditing. The paper highlights this clearly and motivates the need for a more robust solution than currently available. If CXXCrafter can be adapted to be more reliable than the other automated build methods it is compared against, it would be a valuable tool to the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Study:** The initial empirical study of C/C++ build failures provides valuable insights into the challenges of automating this process. This justifies the design choices made in CXXCrafter. The analysis of error causes is also helpful.
    *   **Modular Design:** The separation of concerns into Parser, Generator, and Executor modules is well-reasoned and promotes maintainability and adaptability.
    *   **Iterative Feedback Loop:** The dynamic interaction loop between the Generator and Executor modules is a key strength, allowing CXXCrafter to adapt to and resolve build issues iteratively.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement in build success rates compared to baselines. Component-wise analysis elucidates the value of the different components in the design of CXXCrafter.
    *   **Case Studies:** The inclusion of case studies helps to illustrate the advantages of CXXCrafter over manual building, specifically the benefits of the parser module in identifying build-related documentation and the use of historical build knowledge.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors made their implementation and data publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Hallucinations:** While the paper mentions the mitigation of LLM hallucinations, this is still a significant concern, particularly as the complexity of the project increases. More robust methods for preventing and detecting hallucinations could strengthen the approach.
    *   **Dependency Management Limitations:** While CXXCrafter addresses dependency issues, it can fail when installing complex dependencies, such as CUDA. A more comprehensive dependency management strategy would be beneficial.
    *   **Resource Intensive:** High disk storage requirements (50 TB) could be a barrier to adoption for some users. Strategies for minimizing disk usage, such as efficient caching mechanisms, should be explored.
    *   **Limited Application to Other Languages:** While the paper suggests a potential migration to Java, the design is heavily centered on C/C++ toolchains and build systems. A more generalized approach would enhance its versatility.

*   **Justification of Score:**
    CXXCrafter presents a valuable contribution to the field of software engineering by tackling the complex task of automating C/C++ builds. The use of an LLM agent with a dynamic interaction loop is a novel approach that demonstrates promising results. The empirical study, modular design, and strong experimental results support the effectiveness of the system. However, the limitations related to LLM hallucinations, dependency management, and resource intensity prevent a higher score. Additionally, some of the novelty has already been addressed by the prior research in the area of combining LLMs with automated agents for building projects. Taking these strengths and weaknesses into account, a score of 7 is assigned, reflecting CXXCrafter's potential to significantly improve the automation of C/C++ builds and support downstream software analysis tasks. However, the limitations outlined need further investigation and resolution for it to be significantly more valuable than other approaches.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Minute-Long Videos with Dual Parallelisms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21070v1)
- **Authors**: Zeqing Wang, Bowen Zheng, Xingyi Yang, Yuecong Xu, Xinchao Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based video diffusion models generate high-quality videos at scale but incur prohibitive processing latency and memory costs for long videos. To address this, we propose a novel distributed inference strategy, termed DualParal. The core idea is that, instead of generating an entire video on a single GPU, we parallelize both temporal frames and model layers across GPUs. However, a naive implementation of this division faces a key limitation: since diffusion models require synchronized noise levels across frames, this implementation leads to the serialization of original parallelisms. We leverage a block-wise denoising scheme to handle this. Namely, we process a sequence of frame blocks through the pipeline with progressively decreasing noise levels. Each GPU handles a specific block and layer subset while passing previous results to the next GPU, enabling asynchronous computation and communication. To further optimize performance, we incorporate two key enhancements. Firstly, a feature cache is implemented on each GPU to store and reuse features from the prior block as context, minimizing inter-GPU communication and redundant computation. Secondly, we employ a coordinated noise initialization strategy, ensuring globally consistent temporal dynamics by sharing initial noise patterns across GPUs without extra resource costs. Together, these enable fast, artifact-free, and infinitely long video generation. Applied to the latest diffusion transformer video generator, our method efficiently produces 1,025-frame videos with up to 6.54$\times$ lower latency and 1.48$\times$ lower memory cost on 8$\times$RTX 4090 GPUs.
- **Summary**: This paper presents DualParal, a novel distributed inference strategy for Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based video diffusion models. The core idea involves parallelizing both temporal frames and model layers across multiple GPUs, addressing the computational inefficiency and memory costs associated with long video generation. The method utilizes a block-wise denoising scheme, a feature cache to minimize inter-GPU communication, and a coordinated noise initialization strategy to ensure temporal consistency. Experimental results demonstrate significant reductions in latency and memory cost compared to state-of-the-art distributed methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the specific combination of existing techniques and their adaptation to the problem of efficient long video generation with DiT models. While sequence and pipeline parallelism are known, their naive combination is conflicting in diffusion models.  The block-wise denoising scheme is the key innovation, allowing these parallelisms to work together. The feature caching and coordinated noise initialization are further refinements that enhance performance and maintain video quality. The specific way they are integrated to address the conflict between synchronization requirements of diffusion models and parallelism is the main contribution.  While individual components might be present in other works (e.g., feature caching in different contexts, specific noise initialization strategies), their synergistic use within the DualParal framework is novel.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing demand for high-quality, long-duration video generation. DiT models offer superior generation quality but are computationally expensive, limiting their applicability. DualParal significantly reduces the computational burden, enabling the generation of minute-long videos with substantially lower latency and memory usage. This has important implications for various applications, including content creation, entertainment, and scientific visualization. The ability to generate arbitrarily long videos without quadratic increases in computational cost is a valuable advancement.

**Strengths:**

*   **Effective parallelization strategy:** DualParal successfully combines sequence and pipeline parallelism, overcoming the inherent conflicts.
*   **Block-wise denoising scheme:** This resolves the synchronization issues and enables asynchronous computation and communication.
*   **Feature caching:** Reduces communication overhead and redundant computations.
*   **Coordinated noise initialization:** Maintains global consistency across blocks.
*   **Experimental validation:** Thorough experiments demonstrate significant performance gains in terms of latency and memory usage.
*   **Clear and well-structured presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental nature of some components:** While the combination is novel, some individual components, like feature caching, are adaptations of existing techniques.
*   **Bubble time limitation:** DualParal's performance can be limited when the number of GPUs exceeds the number of blocks, causing GPU idling. (A limitation discussed in the appendix)
*   **Reliance on specific hardware:** Performance is heavily dependent on GPU capabilities and inter-GPU communication bandwidth. Generalizability to other hardware configurations may need further investigation.
*   The comparison could be improved by including some analysis of computational cost in terms of FLOPs in addition to latency, and memory usage. This would clarify the potential energy efficiency benefits of the proposed method.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of video generation by addressing a critical bottleneck: the computational cost of generating long videos with high-quality DiT models. The proposed DualParal framework offers a practical and efficient solution. However, the incremental nature of some components and the GPU constraints justify a lower score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Faithfulness-Aware Uncertainty Quantification for Fact-Checking the Output of Retrieval Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21072v1)
- **Authors**: Ekaterina Fadeeva, Aleksandr Rubashevskii, Roman Vashurin, Shehzaad Dhuliawala, Artem Shelmanov, Timothy Baldwin, Preslav Nakov, Mrinmaya Sachan, Maxim Panov
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) enhanced with external knowledge retrieval, an approach known as Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), have shown strong performance in open-domain question answering. However, RAG systems remain susceptible to hallucinations: factually incorrect outputs that may arise either from inconsistencies in the model's internal knowledge or incorrect use of the retrieved context. Existing approaches often conflate factuality with faithfulness to the retrieved context, misclassifying factually correct statements as hallucinations if they are not directly supported by the retrieval. In this paper, we introduce FRANQ (Faithfulness-based Retrieval Augmented UNcertainty Quantification), a novel method for hallucination detection in RAG outputs. FRANQ applies different Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) techniques to estimate factuality based on whether a statement is faithful to the retrieved context or not. To evaluate FRANQ and other UQ techniques for RAG, we present a new long-form Question Answering (QA) dataset annotated for both factuality and faithfulness, combining automated labeling with manual validation of challenging examples. Extensive experiments on long- and short-form QA across multiple datasets and LLMs show that FRANQ achieves more accurate detection of factual errors in RAG-generated responses compared to existing methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of hallucination detection in Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) systems.  It introduces FRANQ (Faithfulness-based Retrieval Augmented Uncertainty Quantification), a novel method that explicitly differentiates between *faithfulness* (semantic entailment by the retrieved context) and *factuality* (objective correctness).  FRANQ first assesses the faithfulness of a RAG-generated claim to the retrieved context and then applies different Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) techniques based on whether the claim is faithful or not.  The paper also introduces a new long-form QA dataset annotated for both factuality and faithfulness.  Extensive experiments demonstrate that FRANQ outperforms existing UQ methods and supervised baselines in detecting factual errors in RAG outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The most significant novelty lies in the explicit separation of faithfulness and factuality in the context of RAG hallucination detection. Existing approaches often conflate these two, which can lead to misclassification of factually correct statements as hallucinations if they are not directly supported by the retrieved context. FRANQ's architecture to separately quantify these attributes, combined with downstream calibration, represents a meaningful advance. The introduction of the FRANQ method to incorporate different UQ strategies depending on faithfulness is also noteworthy. Creating a new dataset with both factuality and faithfulness labels fills a critical gap in existing resources and allows a more comprehensive evaluation of RAG systems.

*   **Significance:**  Hallucinations are a major impediment to the adoption of LLMs in knowledge-intensive tasks.  Improving the reliability and trustworthiness of RAG systems is crucial. The paper makes a strong case for the importance of distinguishing between faithfulness and factuality, and provides a practical method for doing so. The experimental results are compelling, showing consistent and substantial improvements over existing methods across different datasets and LLMs.  This suggests that FRANQ has the potential to be widely adopted and contribute to building more reliable RAG systems. The new dataset will also serve as a valuable resource for the research community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of conflating faithfulness and factuality and its implications for RAG systems.
    *   **Methodological Soundness:** FRANQ's architecture is well-motivated and the choice of UQ techniques appropriate given the context. The decomposition of factuality into faithfulness and condition factuality is a notable strength
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation with extensive experiments on various datasets and LLMs.  Ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of different components of FRANQ.
    *   **New Dataset:** The creation of the long-form QA factuality dataset is a significant contribution.
    *   **High performance:** FRANQ shows a high and consistent improvement in performance over baseline methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on GPT-4o for Annotation:** The dataset annotation process relies heavily on GPT-4o, which introduces potential biases and limits the generalizability of the dataset. While manual validation is performed, some inherent biases may persist. This is a limitation common to many recent works, but it still deserves mention.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Using the LLM-Polygraph library, the authors note that it provides "ready-to-use, high-quality, but somewhat slow implementations." The overhead of computing AlignScore, MaxNLI, and Parametric Knowledge scores could be a barrier to deployment in resource-constrained environments. The LLM calls will inherently make this system slower than simply using RAG.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on hallucination detection in RAG systems by highlighting the importance of distinguishing between faithfulness and factuality.  The FRANQ method and the new dataset will provide a strong foundation for further research in this area. The method could also be adapted for use with other types of language models and tasks.

*   **Score Rationale:** The paper represents a significant and novel contribution to the field of RAG systems and hallucination detection. The method is well-motivated, rigorously evaluated, and addresses a critical problem. While the reliance on GPT-4o for annotation and the potential computational cost are limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses.

### DynamicVL: Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Dynamic City Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21076v1)
- **Authors**: Weihao Xuan, Junjue Wang, Heli Qi, Zihang Chen, Zhuo Zheng, Yanfei Zhong, Junshi Xia, Naoto Yokoya
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in visual understanding, but their application to long-term Earth observation analysis remains limited, primarily focusing on single-temporal or bi-temporal imagery. To address this gap, we introduce DVL-Suite, a comprehensive framework for analyzing long-term urban dynamics through remote sensing imagery. Our suite comprises 15,063 high-resolution (1.0m) multi-temporal images spanning 42 megacities in the U.S. from 2005 to 2023, organized into two components: DVL-Bench and DVL-Instruct. The DVL-Bench includes seven urban understanding tasks, from fundamental change detection (pixel-level) to quantitative analyses (regional-level) and comprehensive urban narratives (scene-level), capturing diverse urban dynamics including expansion/transformation patterns, disaster assessment, and environmental challenges. We evaluate 17 state-of-the-art multimodal large language models and reveal their limitations in long-term temporal understanding and quantitative analysis. These challenges motivate the creation of DVL-Instruct, a specialized instruction-tuning dataset designed to enhance models' capabilities in multi-temporal Earth observation. Building upon this dataset, we develop DVLChat, a baseline model capable of both image-level question-answering and pixel-level segmentation, facilitating a comprehensive understanding of city dynamics through language interactions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DVL-Suite, a new framework for benchmarking and improving multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for long-term urban dynamics analysis using remote sensing imagery. The suite consists of two components: DVL-Bench, a dataset with high-resolution multi-temporal images spanning several US cities and organized into tasks like change detection, speed estimation, and environmental assessment; and DVL-Instruct, an instruction-tuning dataset to enhance models' capabilities in multi-temporal Earth observation. The paper evaluates various state-of-the-art MLLMs on DVL-Bench, revealing their limitations in long-term temporal understanding and quantitative analysis. To address these, the authors develop DVLChat, a baseline model fine-tuned on DVL-Instruct, demonstrating improved performance in multi-temporal city understanding, captioning, and referring change detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive dataset and benchmark specifically designed for long-term urban dynamics analysis using remote sensing imagery. While MLLMs have been applied to various vision-language tasks, their application to *long-term* temporal analysis in Earth observation, especially with detailed annotations and diverse tasks, is less explored. The DVL-Instruct dataset and the DVLChat model also represent a novel contribution, aiming to improve the performance of MLLMs in this specific domain.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is multi-fold:
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper identifies a gap in existing research regarding the application of MLLMs to long-term Earth observation analysis. Existing methods primarily focus on bi-temporal or short-sequence comparisons, which limits the ability to understand complex urban dynamics.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** DVL-Bench provides a standardized platform for evaluating MLLMs in this domain, facilitating comparison and progress. The diverse tasks cover various aspects of urban understanding, from pixel-level change detection to regional-level analysis and narrative generation.
    *   **Instruction Tuning:** DVL-Instruct and DVLChat demonstrate the potential of instruction tuning to improve the performance of MLLMs on these specialized tasks, which will stimulate interest in exploring this approach in remote sensing applications.
    *   **Practical Applications:** Understanding urban dynamics is crucial for sustainable city development, disaster management, and environmental monitoring. The proposed framework has the potential to enable more effective use of MLLMs for these applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current MLLMs for long-term Earth observation analysis.
    *   **Comprehensive dataset:** DVL-Bench appears to be a well-curated and comprehensive dataset, with diverse tasks and annotations.
    *   **Systematic evaluation:** The evaluation of various state-of-the-art MLLMs provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Instruction tuning approach:** The DVLChat model demonstrates the effectiveness of instruction tuning for improving performance on these specialized tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** While 17 models were evaluated, the detailed analysis of results seems somewhat limited to only a few top performing/noteworthy models. Deeper insights into the failures or successes of a broader range of models would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Potential Bias:** The dataset focuses on US cities, which may limit the generalizability of the results to other regions with different urban development patterns.
    *   **Limited Scope of DVLChat:** DVLChat is presented as a baseline model, and its architecture is based on a few existing segmentation and visual question answering models. This can be seen as a limitation considering the rapid advancements in the architecture of MLLMs.
    *   **Qualitative Evaluation of DVL-Chat:** A deeper qualitative analysis and visual examples for DVL-Chat's caption generation and change localization would strengthen the analysis.

*   **Impact:** The paper will likely have a significant impact on the remote sensing and vision-language communities. It provides a valuable resource for benchmarking and improving MLLMs for Earth observation applications, and it encourages the development of new techniques for understanding complex urban dynamics. Further, the introduction of DVL-Chat would set a standard, while the DVL-Suite will likely become a widely-used benchmark for urban change analysis.

*   **Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of remote sensing and vision-language understanding. While the dataset, DVL-Suite, and DVLChat have potential and value, there are several areas that can be improved. The novelty of the paper is strong, and the potential impact on the research community is considerable, as the proposed benchmark facilitates further studies. The score reflects these observations, recognizing the value of the dataset and the task, while also indicating room for further innovation and stronger quantitative analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficient Large Language Model Inference with Neural Block Linearization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21077v1)
- **Authors**: Mete Erdogan, Francesco Tonin, Volkan Cevher
- **Abstract**: The high inference demands of transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) pose substantial challenges in their deployment. To this end, we introduce Neural Block Linearization (NBL), a novel framework for accelerating transformer model inference by replacing self-attention layers with linear approximations derived from Linear Minimum Mean Squared Error estimators. NBL leverages Canonical Correlation Analysis to compute a theoretical upper bound on the approximation error. Then, we use this bound as a criterion for substitution, selecting the LLM layers with the lowest linearization error. NBL can be efficiently applied to pre-trained LLMs without the need for fine-tuning. In experiments, NBL achieves notable computational speed-ups while preserving competitive accuracy on multiple reasoning benchmarks. For instance, applying NBL to 12 self-attention layers in DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B increases the inference speed by 32% with less than 1% accuracy trade-off, making it a flexible and promising solution to improve the inference efficiency of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Neural Block Linearization (NBL), a novel technique to accelerate inference for large language models (LLMs) by selectively replacing self-attention layers with linear approximations. NBL leverages Canonical Correlation Analysis (CCA) to quantify redundancy in attention layers and estimates the Linear Minimum Mean Squared Error (LMMSE) to generate the linear approximations.  A theoretical error bound, derived from CCA, guides the selection of layers suitable for linearization.  The approach avoids fine-tuning, making it applicable to pre-trained LLMs. Experiments show NBL can significantly speed up inference (e.g., by 32% on a DeepSeek model) while maintaining competitive accuracy across several reasoning benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing self-attention with a learned *linear* approximation is not entirely new (as hinted at in the related work on linear attention, and linearized LLMs, and even some compression methods). However, the specific combination of using CCA for layer selection, LMMSE for deriving the linear mapping, and theoretical error bound guidance seems like an innovation. The fact that this combination allows for substantial speedups *without fine-tuning* is a definite advantage and distinguishes it from some prior approaches. The selective nature of the block replacement, guided by the redundancy metric, also adds to the novelty.
* **Significance:** LLM inference efficiency is a very hot topic, and a practical technique that can accelerate inference while preserving accuracy has clear value. The avoidance of fine-tuning is a major practical benefit. The performance gains demonstrated are reasonably significant and could make a difference in real-world deployment scenarios. The theoretical grounding through CCA and LMMSE adds rigor to the approach and potentially provides insights for further optimization. This could potentially influence the way practitioners approach LLM compression and deployment, though its ultimate impact depends on how widely it is adopted and how well it compares to competing methods as the field evolves.
* **Strengths:**
    * **No Fine-tuning:** NBL can be directly applied to pre-trained LLMs without requiring computationally expensive fine-tuning. This is a significant advantage.
    * **Theoretical Grounding:** The use of CCA provides a principled way to quantify redundancy and guide layer selection. The LMMSE estimator ensures that the linear approximation is optimal in a well-defined sense.
    * **Competitive Performance:** Empirical results demonstrate that NBL can achieve significant speed-ups while maintaining competitive accuracy on a range of reasoning tasks.
    * **Flexibility:**  Applicability across different LLM architectures (Mistral, Llama, DeepSeek)
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Linearity Assumption:** The method inherently assumes a degree of linear relationship between the input and output of the attention layer. While CCA helps to identify layers where this assumption holds, it is still a limitation. This approach could be less effective on layers with highly non-linear transformations.
    * **Calibration Data Dependence:** The method relies on a calibration dataset to estimate the covariance matrices for CCA and LMMSE. Performance might be sensitive to the choice of calibration data and it should be selected to be representative of the downstream task.
    * **Complexity scaling:** O(d^3 + s*t*d^2) is still quite high although better in practice with GPU, and limits wider application.
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on reasoning tasks. While important, it would be beneficial to evaluate NBL on other types of tasks (e.g., generation, translation) and datasets.  A more thorough comparison to other *recent* acceleration techniques would also strengthen the paper.
* **Potential Influence:** NBL's no-fine-tuning characteristic and solid theoretical backing make it a promising alternative for optimizing LLM inference. Future research may investigate NBL-driven optimizations for other transformer layers, potentially leading to hardware-aware LLM deployments.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a novel and practically valuable approach for accelerating LLM inference. The combination of CCA and LMMSE, along with the no-fine-tuning aspect, contributes to its originality and practical significance. While the core idea of linearizing attention is not entirely new, the specific combination of techniques and the empirical results justify a reasonably high score. However, the limitations of the linearity assumption, calibration data dependency, and the limited scope of evaluation prevent it from reaching the higher end of the scale. The algorithm complexity still scales with the embedding dimension of the attention layer (d^3), which can be a bottleneck, limiting scalability.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uni3D-MoE: Scalable Multimodal 3D Scene Understanding via Mixture of Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21079v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Zhang, Yingzhao Jian, Hehe Fan, Yi Yang, Roger Zimmermann
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated considerable potential for comprehensive 3D scene understanding. However, existing approaches typically utilize only one or a limited subset of 3D modalities, resulting in incomplete representations of 3D scenes and reduced interpretive accuracy. Furthermore, different types of queries inherently depend on distinct modalities, indicating that uniform processing of all modality tokens may fail to effectively capture query-specific context. To address these challenges, we propose Uni3D-MoE, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)-based 3D MLLM designed to enable adaptive 3D multimodal fusion. Specifically, Uni3D-MoE integrates a comprehensive set of 3D modalities, including multi-view RGB and depth images, bird's-eye-view (BEV) maps, point clouds, and voxel representations. At its core, our framework employs a learnable routing mechanism within the sparse MoE-based large language model, dynamically selecting appropriate experts at the token level. Each expert specializes in processing multimodal tokens based on learned modality preferences, thus facilitating flexible collaboration tailored to diverse task-specific requirements. Extensive evaluations on standard 3D scene understanding benchmarks and specialized datasets demonstrate the efficacy of Uni3D-MoE.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper titled "Uni3D-MoE: Scalable Multimodal 3D Scene Understanding via Mixture of Experts" introduces a novel framework called Uni3D-MoE, which aims to overcome limitations in current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by utilizing a diverse set of 3D modalities for comprehensive scene understanding. The main issues addressed include the reliance on limited modalities and the ineffective processing of modality tokens for varied queries. Uni3D-MoE employs a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture that adapts to the specific requirements of different queries by routing tokens to specialized experts, each designed to handle a specific modality—this includes RGB images, depth maps, BEV maps, point clouds, and voxel data. Evaluations on standardized benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach in improving the understanding of 3D scenes. --- **Critical Evaluation:** **Novelty (7/10):** Uni3D-MoE presents a noteworthy advance in integrating multiple 3D modalities through a Mixture-of-Experts approach, which is relatively innovative within the context of multimodal large language models. The implementation of a learnable routing mechanism that dynamically selects relevant modalities based on the context and query requirements is a unique contribution that adds flexibility and specificity to 3D scene understanding. However, while the application of MoE to 3D scenes is a fresh perspective, the concept of Mixture-of-Experts itself is not new in machine learning. Many existing frameworks utilize MoE structures but often focus on simpler representations and tasks. Therefore, while the paper's approach is novel in its application, the underlying methodologies may not be groundbreaking. **Significance (6/10):** The impact of Uni3D-MoE on the field of 3D scene understanding is substantial due to its potential to enhance the accuracy and richness of representations derived from complex multimodal data. This framework's ability to combine various modalities allows for improved interpretative capabilities, which could be greatly beneficial in fields such as robotics, virtual reality, and autonomous navigation. Nevertheless, the paper's significance may be somewhat tempered by its reliance on benchmarks that, while established, might not fully represent the complexities encountered in real-world applications, thus raising questions about generalizability. Furthermore, the implementation details and computational efficiency aspects of scaling the MoE mechanism warrant further exploration to assess practical deployment in large-scale settings. **Strengths:** - Comprehensive approach integrating multiple modalities enhances scene understanding. - Innovative routing mechanism that adapts processing to query-specific needs. **Weaknesses:** - The underlying Mixture-of-Experts concept is not entirely novel. - Dependence on established benchmarks raises concerns about real-world applicability. **Conclusion:** Overall, Uni3D-MoE represents a significant step towards improved multimodal understanding in 3D scene analysis. It possesses elements of novelty, particularly in its integration method and routing mechanism, though the conceptual foundation borrows from existing paradigms. The implications of this work could catalyze advancements in various applications, yet additional validation in diverse real-world scenarios is necessary to truly gauge its robustness and impact. **Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMs Think, But Not In Your Flow: Reasoning-Level Personalization for Black-Box Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21082v1)
- **Authors**: Jieyong Kim, Tongyoung Kim, Soonjin Yoon, Jaehyung Kim, Dongha Lee
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have recently achieved impressive performance across a wide range of natural language tasks and are now widely used in real-world applications. Among them, black-box LLMs--served via APIs without access to model internals--are especially dominant due to their scalability and ease of deployment. Despite their strong capabilities, these models typically produce generalized responses that overlook personal preferences and reasoning styles. This has led to growing interest in black-box LLM personalization, which aims to tailor model outputs to user-specific context without modifying model parameters. However, existing approaches primarily focus on response-level personalization, attempting to match final outputs without modeling personal thought process. To address this limitation, we propose RPM, a framework for reasoning-level personalization that aligns the model's reasoning process with a user's personalized logic. RPM first constructs statistical user-specific factors by extracting and grouping response-influential features from user history. It then builds personalized reasoning paths that reflect how these factors are used in context. In the inference stage, RPM retrieves reasoning-aligned examples for new queries via feature-level similarity and performs inference conditioned on the structured factors and retrieved reasoning paths, enabling the model to follow user-specific reasoning trajectories. This reasoning-level personalization enhances both predictive accuracy and interpretability by grounding model outputs in user-specific logic through structured information. Extensive experiments across diverse tasks show that RPM consistently outperforms response-level personalization methods, demonstrating the effectiveness of reasoning-level personalization in black-box LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLMs Think, But Not In Your Flow: Reasoning-Level Personalization for Black-Box Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RPM, a novel framework for personalizing black-box Large Language Models (LLMs) at the *reasoning level*.  Unlike existing methods that focus on tailoring the final output (response-level personalization), RPM aims to align the *thought process* of the LLM with a user's personalized logic.  RPM works by first constructing statistical user-specific factors by extracting and grouping relevant features from the user's history.  It then builds personalized reasoning paths that reflect how these factors are used in context. During inference, RPM retrieves reasoning-aligned examples for new queries and conditions the inference on these retrieved examples and structured factors, guiding the model to follow user-specific reasoning trajectories.  The authors claim this enhances both predictive accuracy and interpretability. They present experimental results across diverse tasks, demonstrating that RPM outperforms response-level personalization methods and showing the effectiveness of reasoning-level personalization in black-box LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of reasoning-level personalization is novel and addresses a significant limitation of existing black-box personalization methods.  Shifting the focus from just matching outputs to modeling the underlying thought process is a worthwhile direction. The framework design, specifically the extraction of features, factor construction, and retrieval of reasoning paths, is well-structured and contributes to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem: generating personalized responses from powerful, but often generic, LLMs in scenarios where internal access is restricted. Successful personalization of this kind could have a wide range of applications, from recommender systems to personalized learning. The claimed improvements in both accuracy and interpretability are significant contributions.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current black-box LLM personalization approaches and motivates the need for a new paradigm.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** RPM is presented as a well-structured and clearly explained framework, with distinct steps and components.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The authors provide empirical evidence across a variety of tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of RPM over existing methods.  The ablation studies provide further insights into the contribution of individual components.
    *   **Human Evaluation:**  The inclusion of a human evaluation study further strengthens the results by providing insights into the quality of the generated reasoning and its alignment with user preferences.
    *   **Interpretability:**  The framework promotes interpretability by providing structured information about the user's reasoning process.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on LLMs for Key Components:**  The framework relies heavily on the LLM (GPT-4o-mini) for feature extraction, factor construction, reasoning generation, and potentially for influencing how these factors should be ranked/ordered - which is used before each of the steps to be personalized. It is only using black-box LLMs as a backbone that is then adapted/personalized. The efficacy is based on the choice and capabilities of this foundation LLM - and could be highly sensitive. The approach could be dependent on the LLM's specific biases and limitations. The paper would benefit from analyzing the sensitivity of the framework to different LLM backbones.
    *   **Complexity:** RPM is more complex than simple response-level personalization methods.  While the authors address the cost efficiency with an 81% lower API cost versus Fermi, it may still be a barrier to adoption in some scenarios - and this should be discussed further.
    *   **Limited Control:** While the paper aims for interpretability, it would be beneficial to explore ways to provide users with more control over the generated reasoning and influence how the personalization is constructed, providing greater accountability.
    *   **Generalizability to other LLMs and Datasets:** Additional experimentation is needed to assess the generalizability of RPM to various LLMs and datasets, especially if not as advanced of a starting place. For instance, on other tasks that benefit more from understanding context instead of having very clear statistical patterns.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by shifting the focus of black-box LLM personalization towards reasoning-level alignment. This could lead to the development of more sophisticated and personalized LLM applications. The detailed framework could also inspire further research in this area, and act as the baseline.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of black-box LLM personalization. It tackles a key limitation of existing methods by focusing on aligning the reasoning process of the LLM with user preferences. The RPM framework is well-defined, and the experimental results are compelling, especially the human evaluation regarding interpretability. The weaknesses of dependency on the pre-training of the LLMs and the complexity of the approach, while needing further analysis, don't overshadow the overall strength of the paper. Overall, it has the potential to inspire more research and improvements.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Position is Power: System Prompts as a Mechanism of Bias in Large Language Models (LLMs)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21091v1)
- **Authors**: Anna Neumann, Elisabeth Kirsten, Muhammad Bilal Zafar, Jatinder Singh
- **Abstract**: System prompts in Large Language Models (LLMs) are predefined directives that guide model behaviour, taking precedence over user inputs in text processing and generation. LLM deployers increasingly use them to ensure consistent responses across contexts. While model providers set a foundation of system prompts, deployers and third-party developers can append additional prompts without visibility into others' additions, while this layered implementation remains entirely hidden from end-users. As system prompts become more complex, they can directly or indirectly introduce unaccounted for side effects. This lack of transparency raises fundamental questions about how the position of information in different directives shapes model outputs. As such, this work examines how the placement of information affects model behaviour. To this end, we compare how models process demographic information in system versus user prompts across six commercially available LLMs and 50 demographic groups. Our analysis reveals significant biases, manifesting in differences in user representation and decision-making scenarios. Since these variations stem from inaccessible and opaque system-level configurations, they risk representational, allocative and potential other biases and downstream harms beyond the user's ability to detect or correct. Our findings draw attention to these critical issues, which have the potential to perpetuate harms if left unexamined. Further, we argue that system prompt analysis must be incorporated into AI auditing processes, particularly as customisable system prompts become increasingly prevalent in commercial AI deployments.
- **Summary**: Okay, here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Position is Power: System Prompts as a Mechanism of Bias in Large Language Models (LLMs)" investigates how the placement of demographic information in system prompts versus user prompts affects the behavior of commercial Large Language Models. The authors systematically analyze six LLMs, focusing on representational and allocative biases. They find that placing demographic information in system prompts leads to increased bias in both descriptions of demographic groups and resource allocation decisions compared to user prompts. They argue that the opacity of system prompts exacerbates these biases, hindering detection and mitigation, and call for their incorporation into AI auditing practices.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Important and Timely Topic:** The study tackles a crucial issue - the impact of system prompts on bias in LLMs. As LLMs become increasingly integrated into various sectors, understanding and mitigating these biases is essential for fairness and accountability. The focus on system prompts is particularly relevant as they represent a layer of influence often overlooked and difficult for end-users to access.
    *   **Systematic Methodology:** The paper employs a well-structured methodology using a comprehensive dataset of demographic descriptors and resource allocation scenarios. The use of sentiment analysis and Kendall's rank correlation coefficient provides quantitative measures of bias. The comparison of six commercially available LLMs offers insights into the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The study provides strong empirical evidence demonstrating that system prompt placement significantly affects model behavior, leading to measurable increases in both representational and allocative biases. The heatmaps and statistical analyses clearly illustrate these effects.
    *   **Focus on Transparency and Accountability:** The paper highlights the transparency challenges associated with system prompts and advocates for their inclusion in AI auditing processes. This recommendation is critical for promoting responsible AI development and deployment.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper clearly defines its research questions which guide the investigation of the placement of demographic information on the system.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Demographic Categories:** The paper focuses on specific demographic categories aligned with GDPR and US research on stigmatized groups. While grounded in regulations and prior work, this may not fully represent the spectrum of social identities and potential biases in other contexts.
    *   **Dependency on Sentiment Analysis Biases:** The sentiment analysis approach, while justified, is still susceptible to inherent biases present in sentiment analyzers. The comparison of relative differences somewhat mitigates this, but the issue isn't entirely eliminated.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Root Causes:** The study primarily focuses on measuring the effects of prompt placement without deeply investigating the underlying mechanisms that cause these biases. A more in-depth analysis of the model's internal processing might reveal more effective mitigation strategies.
    *   **Proprietary Model Access:** The reliance on commercial APIs provides limited access to the models' architecture and training data which influences the ability to fully understand why LLMs are biased.
    *   **Lacking of Alternative Solutions:** While the paper makes a case for including system prompts into the auditing process, there is a lack of proposals and/or solutions.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The paper makes a novel contribution by highlighting the previously underexplored influence of system prompts as a source of bias in LLMs. Prior work has focused on training data and model architectures, but the layered prompt architecture is an emerging area and this paper presents a compelling case for considering the often-overlooked system-level customization. It's significant because it demonstrates how deployers can inadvertently introduce or amplify biases through prompt engineering and highlights the urgent need for greater transparency and accountability in AI supply chains.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    The study has the potential to influence AI development practices, particularly regarding the design and implementation of system prompts. It can also inform the development of AI auditing frameworks and regulatory guidelines, promoting more responsible and ethical AI deployment. Moreover, it encourages further research into the mechanisms of bias propagation in LLMs and the development of effective mitigation strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**
This paper is a valuable and timely contribution to the field. It provides strong empirical evidence for a novel and relevant source of bias in LLMs. While some limitations exist regarding the scope and depth of the analysis, the study's systematic methodology, clear findings, and actionable recommendations warrant a high score. It significantly advances our understanding of bias in LLMs and encourages a more responsible approach to their development and deployment. The score reflects the significance of the findings and their potential impact on the field, while acknowledging the limitations outlined above.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 8/10

### BLUCK: A Benchmark Dataset for Bengali Linguistic Understanding and Cultural Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21092v1)
- **Authors**: Daeen Kabir, Minhajur Rahman Chowdhury Mahim, Sheikh Shafayat, Adnan Sadik, Arian Ahmed, Eunsu Kim, Alice Oh
- **Abstract**: In this work, we introduce BLUCK, a new dataset designed to measure the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in Bengali linguistic understanding and cultural knowledge. Our dataset comprises 2366 multiple-choice questions (MCQs) carefully curated from compiled collections of several college and job level examinations and spans 23 categories covering knowledge on Bangladesh's culture and history and Bengali linguistics. We benchmarked BLUCK using 6 proprietary and 3 open-source LLMs - including GPT-4o, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, Gemini-1.5-Pro, Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, and DeepSeekV3. Our results show that while these models perform reasonably well overall, they, however, struggles in some areas of Bengali phonetics. Although current LLMs' performance on Bengali cultural and linguistic contexts is still not comparable to that of mainstream languages like English, our results indicate Bengali's status as a mid-resource language. Importantly, BLUCK is also the first MCQ-based evaluation benchmark that is centered around native Bengali culture, history, and linguistics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BLUCK, a new benchmark dataset for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in understanding Bengali language and culture. The dataset comprises 2366 multiple-choice questions spanning 23 categories covering knowledge of Bangladesh's culture, history, and Bengali linguistics. The authors benchmarked BLUCK using several proprietary and open-source LLMs, finding that while these models perform reasonably well overall, they struggle in specific areas like Bengali phonetics and have limitations in Bengali-specific knowledge.  BLUCK is positioned as the first MCQ-based evaluation benchmark centered around native Bengali culture, history, and linguistics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper makes a strong claim of novelty by providing the first MCQ-based evaluation benchmark specifically for native Bengali culture and linguistics. Given the underrepresentation of Bengali in existing cultural and linguistic benchmarks, BLUCK addresses a significant gap.
    *   **Dataset Construction:** The dataset construction methodology appears rigorous, involving curation from various official Bangladeshi examinations, inspection by multiple authors, and digitization by proficient Bengali annotators. This enhances the dataset's reliability and representativeness.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper thoroughly evaluates a range of LLMs, including both proprietary (GPT-4o, Claude 3.5) and open-source models (Llama-3, DeepSeekV3), providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses in understanding Bengali.
    *   **Targeted Focus:**  The identification of specific areas where LLMs struggle (e.g., Bengali phonetics) is valuable for directing future research efforts.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** While 2366 MCQs are reasonable, it might be considered limited compared to some established benchmarks in other languages. A larger dataset would enhance the robustness and generalizability of the findings.
    *   **MCQ Format Limitation:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of using only MCQs, particularly in capturing complex reasoning processes. This format, while practical, may not fully assess the nuanced understanding of Bengali culture and linguistics.
    *   **Performance Gap:** The paper notes a 7% performance drop compared to MMLU. Though understandable, it suggests BLUCK's relative difficulty. A more in-depth analysis of *why* the drop occurs, tied to characteristics of the BLUCK dataset itself, would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Reproducibility:** While the paper mentions making the dataset publicly available, specific implementation details, like exact versions of models used and detailed prompting strategies should be described more precisely to ensure greater replicability.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The findings are specific to the models evaluated and the scope of the BLUCK dataset. Further research is needed to assess the broader generalizability of the findings to other LLMs and tasks.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addressing Underrepresentation:**  BLUCK's contribution in addressing the underrepresentation of Bengali in LLM evaluation is significant. It provides a valuable resource for researchers and developers working on improving the performance of LLMs for Bengali speakers.
    *   **Promoting Cultural Understanding:** The benchmark has the potential to promote a more culturally aware and linguistically accurate AI, which is crucial for building inclusive and equitable AI systems.
    *   **Stimulating Further Research:** The paper's findings can stimulate further research in areas such as Bengali phonetics and cultural understanding, fostering the development of more sophisticated and context-aware language models.

*   **Overall:**
    The paper makes a valuable and original contribution by providing a much-needed benchmark for evaluating LLMs in Bengali language and culture. The methodology is sound, and the evaluation is comprehensive. While there are some limitations regarding dataset size and MCQ format, the significance of addressing underrepresentation and promoting cultural understanding outweighs these drawbacks.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the paper's valuable contribution to a relatively neglected area. The novelty of the dataset, the rigorous methodology, and the identification of specific LLM limitations in Bengali language are positive aspects. The limitations in dataset size, format (MCQ), and generalizability are accounted for in the slightly lower score. The paper's significance in promoting cultural understanding and stimulating future research further justifies the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Thinker: Learning to Think Fast and Slow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21097v1)
- **Authors**: Stephen Chung, Wenyu Du, Jie Fu
- **Abstract**: Recent studies show that the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) can be improved by applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) to question-answering (QA) tasks in areas such as math and coding. With a long context length, LLMs may learn to perform search, as indicated by the self-correction behavior observed in DeepSeek R1. However, this search behavior is often imprecise and lacks confidence, resulting in long, redundant responses and highlighting deficiencies in intuition and verification. Inspired by the Dual Process Theory in psychology, we introduce a simple modification to the QA task that includes four stages: Fast Thinking, where the LLM must answer within a strict token budget; Verification, where the model evaluates its initial response; Slow Thinking, where it refines the initial response with more deliberation; and Summarization, where it distills the refinement from the previous stage into precise steps. Our proposed task improves average accuracy from 24.9% to 27.9% for Qwen2.5-1.5B, and from 45.9% to 49.8% for DeepSeek-R1-Qwen-1.5B. Notably, for Qwen2.5-1.5B, the Fast Thinking mode alone achieves 26.8% accuracy using fewer than 1000 tokens, demonstrating substantial inference efficiency gains. These findings suggest that intuition and deliberative reasoning are distinct, complementary systems benefiting from targeted training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the "Thinker" task, a novel environment augmentation technique for training Large Language Models (LLMs) on question-answering (QA) tasks. Inspired by Dual Process Theory in psychology, the Thinker task breaks down the QA process into four distinct stages: Fast Thinking (rapid, intuitive answering with a small token budget), Verification (evaluating the initial answer), Slow Thinking (deliberative refinement if the initial answer is incorrect), and Summarization (concise explanation of the reasoning). The authors design specific reward signals for each stage to train distinct agent capabilities, aiming to improve both intuition and deliberate reasoning. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that LLMs trained with the Thinker task outperform those trained on standard QA tasks. The analysis also suggests that Thinker-trained agents exhibit more direct reasoning and less overt self-reflection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of breaking down a QA task into sub-tasks designed to mimic Dual Process Theory is somewhat novel. While prior work has explored environment augmentation and self-correction, the explicit structuring of the task with stages dedicated to intuition, verification, and deliberation, along with targeted reward signals, represents a distinct approach. The use of a summarization step to distill reasoning is also a valuable component. The combination of these elements lends the work a moderate degree of originality.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of LLM reasoning. By explicitly training different cognitive components, the Thinker task offers a more nuanced and targeted approach to RL fine-tuning than simply rewarding correct answers. The reported performance gains on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, along with the observed reduction in self-reflection patterns, suggest a tangible benefit. Further, the demonstration of increased fast thinking performance has positive implications on increased inference efficiency.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of standard QA training and draws a compelling analogy to Dual Process Theory to motivate the Thinker task.
*   **Clearly Defined Task:** The four stages of the Thinker task are well-defined, and the rationale behind each stage and its associated reward signal is clearly explained.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The experimental results, including the comparison to baseline QA training, Open-Reasoner-Zero, and SimpleRL, provide evidence of the effectiveness of the Thinker task.
*   **Analysis and Insights:** The analysis of reflection patterns and response lengths offers valuable insights into the reasoning process of Thinker-trained agents. The ablation study on Fast Thinking Mode further clarifies the contribution of this initial intuition.
*   **Code Release:**  Making the implementation publicly available fosters reproducibility and encourages further research.
*   **Scalability Testing:** The demonstration of performance gains with a 7B model suggests promise for scaling the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Scale:** While the 7B model experiments are encouraging, the primary results are based on 1.5B parameter models, which may limit the generalizability of the findings to larger, more capable LLMs. Exploring larger models is crucial.
*   **Heuristic Hyperparameter Tuning:** The hyperparameter settings, particularly the token limits for each task stage, were determined heuristically. A more systematic hyperparameter optimization could potentially lead to even better performance.
*   **Task-Specific Design:** The Thinker task is tailored to QA, specifically mathematical reasoning. The generalizability of the approach to other types of tasks or reasoning domains is not explicitly addressed.
*   **The "Correct" Answer Bottleneck:** It should be noted that any approach using a reward signal based on accuracy relies upon a pre-determined answer. This answer may not be the only correct or optimal solution.

**Potential Influence:** The Thinker task has the potential to influence the field of LLM training by providing a more structured and targeted approach to RL fine-tuning. It could inspire further research into environment augmentation techniques that explicitly target different cognitive components. The emphasis on efficient reasoning and the Fast Thinking mode could also lead to practical benefits in terms of reducing inference costs.

**Justification of Score:**

Despite the weaknesses regarding limited model scale and heuristic hyperparameter tuning, the paper's strengths, including its novel task design, compelling motivation, solid experimental validation, scalability demonstration, and the provision of associated code, warrant a positive evaluation. The Thinker task offers a promising and potentially impactful approach to improving the efficiency and effectiveness of LLM reasoning. The observed gains across several benchmarked tasks further contribute to the potential.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conditional Diffusion Models with Classifier-Free Gibbs-like Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21101v1)
- **Authors**: Badr Moufad, Yazid Janati, Alain Durmus, Ahmed Ghorbel, Eric Moulines, Jimmy Olsson
- **Abstract**: Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is a widely used technique for improving conditional diffusion models by linearly combining the outputs of conditional and unconditional denoisers. While CFG enhances visual quality and improves alignment with prompts, it often reduces sample diversity, leading to a challenging trade-off between quality and diversity. To address this issue, we make two key contributions. First, CFG generally does not correspond to a well-defined denoising diffusion model (DDM). In particular, contrary to common intuition, CFG does not yield samples from the target distribution associated with the limiting CFG score as the noise level approaches zero -- where the data distribution is tilted by a power $w \gt 1$ of the conditional distribution. We identify the missing component: a R\'enyi divergence term that acts as a repulsive force and is required to correct CFG and render it consistent with a proper DDM. Our analysis shows that this correction term vanishes in the low-noise limit. Second, motivated by this insight, we propose a Gibbs-like sampling procedure to draw samples from the desired tilted distribution. This method starts with an initial sample from the conditional diffusion model without CFG and iteratively refines it, preserving diversity while progressively enhancing sample quality. We evaluate our approach on both image and text-to-audio generation tasks, demonstrating substantial improvements over CFG across all considered metrics. The code is available at https://github.com/yazidjanati/cfgig
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of reduced diversity in conditional diffusion models when using Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG). It argues that standard CFG, which linearly combines conditional and unconditional denoisers, doesn't correspond to sampling from a well-defined denoising diffusion model (DDM).  Specifically, it demonstrates that CFG effectively samples from a distribution tilted by a power of the conditional distribution, missing a Rényi divergence term that acts as a repulsive force to encourage diversity. Based on this analysis, the authors propose a novel Gibbs-like sampling procedure called Classifier-Free Gibbs-Like Guidance (CFGIG). CFGIG starts with a sample from the conditional distribution *without* CFG and iteratively refines it using alternating noising and denoising steps (employing CFG at low noise levels).  The experiments show improvements in both image and audio generation tasks compared to standard CFG, suggesting a better trade-off between quality and diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The paper provides a compelling theoretical analysis of the limitations of CFG from a probabilistic perspective. Identifying the missing Rényi divergence term is a key insight.
    *   The CFGIG algorithm is a reasonable approach building on the theoretical analysis, even though Gibbs-like samplers are not entirely new. The iterative refinement is intended to correct for diversity issues early in the process (where CFG doesn’t really “see” the full posterior).
    *   The theoretical justification for guidance using scores at two different noise levels is interesting, as this could theoretically explain recent empirical approaches.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The problem of balancing quality and diversity in conditional generative models is important in practice.
    *   The theoretical grounding provides a more principled alternative to purely heuristic approaches.
    *   The empirical results are promising, demonstrating improvements in various metrics, particularly on FDDINOV2 which could suggest better perceptual quality.
    *   The algorithm is fairly easy to implement, which could increase its adoption.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong theoretical motivation.
    *   Clear identification of CFG's limitations.
    *   Relatively simple algorithm (CFGIG).
    *   Demonstrated empirical improvements.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   CFGIG still relies on CFG, meaning that some of the CFG issues still occur. The low noise is intended to get past these issues.
    *   The reliance on a small noise level σ* introduces a hyperparameter that needs tuning and influences mixing. This is a potential limitation, and the paper acknowledges this.
    *   The Gaussian analysis is nice, but further theoretical analysis could discuss limitations of it and potential problems caused.
    *   The method uses more computational complexity, by being iterative.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by prompting a more theoretically-grounded understanding and development of guidance techniques in diffusion models. The proposed CFGIG algorithm offers a practical alternative to standard CFG, and the analysis could inspire other researchers to explore new guidance methods or training objectives that explicitly address the diversity issue.

**Justification:**

I believe the paper provides a novel perspective on a crucial limitation of a widely used technique (CFG).  The theoretical analysis is solid, and the proposed algorithm is grounded in this analysis.  The empirical results are supportive. The approach is not revolutionary, but represents a significant improvement and a step towards more principled guidance methods. Furthermore, the insights regarding score matching on two noise level may be key in unlocking future work.
