- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Lightweight Multi-Expert Generative Language Model System for Engineering Information and Knowledge Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21109v1)
- **Authors**: Bogdan Bogachov, Yaoyao Fiona Zhao
- **Abstract**: Despite recent advancements in domain adaptation techniques for large language models, these methods remain computationally intensive, and the resulting models can still exhibit hallucination issues. Most existing adaptation methods do not prioritize reducing the computational resources required for fine-tuning and inference of language models. Hallucination issues have gradually decreased with each new model release. However, they remain prevalent in engineering contexts, where generating well-structured text with minimal errors and inconsistencies is critical. This work introduces a novel approach called the Small Language Graph (SLG), which is a lightweight adaptation solution designed to address the two key challenges outlined above. The system is structured in the form of a graph, where each node represents a lightweight expert - a small language model fine-tuned on specific and concise texts. The results of this study have shown that SLG was able to surpass conventional fine-tuning methods on the Exact Match metric by 3 times. Additionally, the fine-tuning process was 1.7 times faster compared to that of a larger stand-alone language model. These findings introduce a potential for small to medium-sized engineering companies to confidently use generative AI technologies, such as LLMs, without the necessity to invest in expensive computational resources. Also, the graph architecture and the small size of expert nodes offer a possible opportunity for distributed AI systems, thus potentially diverting the global need for expensive centralized compute clusters.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper "A Lightweight Multi-Expert Generative Language Model System for Engineering Information and Knowledge Extraction." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Small Language Graph (SLG) system, a lightweight adaptation solution for large language models (LLMs) in engineering domains. The SLG is a graph-based architecture where each node is a small, fine-tuned language model ("expert") focused on a specific and concise text segment (e.g., a subsection of a Cessna aircraft's Structural Repair Manual). This aims to address the computational intensity of fine-tuning large models and mitigate hallucination issues, particularly in domains where publicly available data is limited. The authors compare SLG against fine-tuned standalone Llama models (both a larger and a size-matched one) using ROUGE-L, Exact Match (EM), and METEOR metrics. The results suggest that SLG can achieve significantly better performance on the Exact Match metric, potentially indicating a greater resistance to hallucinations, and is faster to train compared to a larger standalone model. The system also operates on a single mid-range GPU.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core idea of a graph-based system comprised of small, specialized language models is *somewhat novel*. While the broad concept of modular or expert systems isn't new in AI, applying it in this specific way to LLMs, particularly with the explicit goal of reducing hallucinations and improving resource efficiency in constrained engineering domains, offers a unique angle.
    *   The approach to data preparation, specifically isolating data chunks by engineering document subsections to minimize "knowledge overshadowing," contributes to novelty. This addresses a specific challenge within engineering applications.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's significance lies in its potential to make LLM technology more accessible to small and medium-sized engineering companies. By reducing computational demands, SLG could lower the barrier to entry for adopting AI-driven solutions. This could democratize access to advanced AI capabilities in a sector with substantial practical application.
    *   The claim of improved resistance to hallucinations, if further validated, is a substantial benefit. Hallucinations are a major impediment to deploying LLMs in safety-critical engineering contexts.
    *   The potential of extending this approach to distributed AI systems, as mentioned by the authors, offers a pathway for reducing the burden on centralized compute clusters, which is becoming increasingly important in AI research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper tackles a *real-world problem* (resource constraints and hallucination issues) that hinders the adoption of LLMs in engineering.
    *   The methodology is clearly described and fairly straightforward. The experiment setup, while not exhaustive, provides some evidence to support the claims.
    *   The paper is well-written and structured.
    *   There is a public repository with the relevant code.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited scope of comparisons:* The evaluation compares SLG to only two standalone Llama models. It would significantly strengthen the paper to include comparisons against RAG-based systems, prompt engineering techniques, other adaptation methods (e.g., adapter-based fine-tuning), and potentially larger Llama model.
    *   *Hallucination measurement:* Relying solely on the Exact Match (EM) metric as an indicator of hallucination resistance is a *major weakness*. EM can be sensitive to minor variations in wording and may not fully capture the presence of hallucinated information. A human evaluation or a more sophisticated hallucination detection method would be crucial. Without that, the authors are effectively just measuring how closely the output matched what it was trained on, without directly measuring factual correctness.
    *   *Orchestrator effectiveness:* The 70% success rate of the orchestrator in directing queries to the appropriate expert raises concerns. If the query is not correctly routed, the system's performance will be compromised. The paper needs to delve deeper into the reasons for this lower success rate and how it impacts overall system performance.
    *   *Limited Hyperparameter Exploration:* While the authors tune four hyperparameters, the exploration is not exhaustive.
    *   *Lack of detail regarding data preprocessing and data chunks*: The paper does not include specific instructions, or specific methods to divide the textual data into chunks.
    *   *Lack of failure cases/error analysis*: The paper does not showcase any particular error cases of the studied approaches.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper presents a potentially promising approach for adapting LLMs to resource-constrained engineering environments. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the reliance on the Exact Match metric as a proxy for hallucination resistance are significant shortcomings. The orchestrator’s mediocre efficiency also impacts its practicality. Although the concept is of interest, the evidence presented doesn't strongly support the claims. Further investigation is necessary to validate the system's effectiveness in real-world scenarios.

Score: 5

**Rationale:**
A score of 5 reflects that, while the paper has some novelty and tackles a relevant problem, the lack of robust validation and limited comparisons prevents it from being considered a substantial contribution at this stage. Addressing the weaknesses mentioned above would significantly increase the paper's impact and merit a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Differentiable Solver Search for Fast Diffusion Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21114v1)
- **Authors**: Shuai Wang, Zexian Li, Qipeng zhang, Tianhui Song, Xubin Li, Tiezheng Ge, Bo Zheng, Limin Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated remarkable generation quality but at the cost of numerous function evaluations. Recently, advanced ODE-based solvers have been developed to mitigate the substantial computational demands of reverse-diffusion solving under limited sampling steps. However, these solvers, heavily inspired by Adams-like multistep methods, rely solely on t-related Lagrange interpolation. We show that t-related Lagrange interpolation is suboptimal for diffusion model and reveal a compact search space comprised of time steps and solver coefficients. Building on our analysis, we propose a novel differentiable solver search algorithm to identify more optimal solver. Equipped with the searched solver, rectified-flow models, e.g., SiT-XL/2 and FlowDCN-XL/2, achieve FID scores of 2.40 and 2.35, respectively, on ImageNet256 with only 10 steps. Meanwhile, DDPM model, DiT-XL/2, reaches a FID score of 2.33 with only 10 steps. Notably, our searched solver outperforms traditional solvers by a significant margin. Moreover, our searched solver demonstrates generality across various model architectures, resolutions, and model sizes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of slow sampling in diffusion models, which limits their widespread deployment. It argues that existing ODE-based solvers, inspired by Adams-like multistep methods and Lagrange interpolation, are suboptimal. The key contributions are: 1) The discovery that the specific form of the Lagrange interpolation function is less crucial than the coefficients themselves; 2) The definition of a compact search space encompassing time steps and solver coefficients; and 3) The development of a differentiable solver search algorithm to find optimal parameters within this space. Experiments on ImageNet-256x256 with DDPM and Rectified-flow models demonstrate improved FID scores using the learned solver compared to traditional solvers. The paper also showcases the method's generalizability to various model architectures, resolutions, and sizes, including text-to-image models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a new perspective on ODE-based solvers for diffusion models. While solver optimization and timestep scheduling have been previously explored (e.g., Xue et al. 2024), the core idea of pre-integrating, reducing interpolation to coefficients, and defining a compact search space is novel.  It successfully challenges the common assumption that the specific form of interpolation function is paramount in Adams-like methods for diffusion models, which *is* a significant shift in perspective. The connection made between the interpolation function and the underlying properties of pre-trained diffusion models is strong.

*   **Significance:** Faster sampling in diffusion models is crucial for making them more accessible and practical. The paper contributes to this goal by providing a method that improves upon existing solvers without requiring retraining. The reported FID score improvements, particularly for Rectified-flow models, are significant. The claimed generalizability of the approach is also valuable, as it reduces the need to fine-tune solvers for each specific model. Further, the ability of the searched solver to allow a DDPM model to approach, even surpass, the performance of rectified flow under similar computation conditions is a worthwhile contribution, indicating the method taps into previously unoptimized potential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a well-defined theoretical justification for the proposed method.
    *   The differentiable solver search algorithm is clearly described.
    *   Experiments demonstrate tangible improvements in image generation quality and sampling speed across multiple architectures and datasets.
    *   The analysis showing that the interpolation function form is not critical provides a valuable insight.
    *   The open-source nature of the models makes future replications possible, and the code/models should be included to improve the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Although performance comparisons versus multiple models were shown, a single loss function (MSE) and single reference solver trajectory was used when optimizing the solver parameters.
    *   There is little discussion as to *why* the optimized solvers perform better and whether there is any intuitive rationale for the learned coefficients and timesteps. It’s treated more as a black box optimization.
    *   While the paper claims generalizability, the experiments are primarily focused on ImageNet. More experiments with diverse datasets (e.g., different modalities, more text-to-image) would strengthen the generalizability claim.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient diffusion models. Other researchers can build upon this work by exploring different search spaces, optimization strategies, and solver architectures. It can lead to the design of diffusion models with fewer sampling steps and improved generation quality. It could also affect research in distillation, making simpler models work better through refined inference.

**Overall:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to diffusion model solvers, supported by solid theoretical analysis and experimental results. While there are some weaknesses, such as a lack of intuitive explanation and reliance on MSE loss functions during solver parameter optimization, the paper's contributions are significant and could have a tangible impact on the field of generative modeling. The novel approach and significant gains from a fairly simple, differentiable approach warrants a good score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Will It Still Be True Tomorrow? Multilingual Evergreen Question Classification to Improve Trustworthy QA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21115v1)
- **Authors**: Sergey Pletenev, Maria Marina, Nikolay Ivanov, Daria Galimzianova, Nikita Krayko, Mikhail Salnikov, Vasily Konovalov, Alexander Panchenko, Viktor Moskvoretskii
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) often hallucinate in question answering (QA) tasks. A key yet underexplored factor contributing to this is the temporality of questions -- whether they are evergreen (answers remain stable over time) or mutable (answers change). In this work, we introduce EverGreenQA, the first multilingual QA dataset with evergreen labels, supporting both evaluation and training. Using EverGreenQA, we benchmark 12 modern LLMs to assess whether they encode question temporality explicitly (via verbalized judgments) or implicitly (via uncertainty signals). We also train EG-E5, a lightweight multilingual classifier that achieves SoTA performance on this task. Finally, we demonstrate the practical utility of evergreen classification across three applications: improving self-knowledge estimation, filtering QA datasets, and explaining GPT-4o retrieval behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EverGreenQA, a novel multilingual question answering (QA) dataset designed to address the temporality aspect of questions (evergreen vs. mutable). The authors argue that existing QA datasets often overlook this crucial factor, leading to inaccurate evaluations of Large Language Models (LLMs). The paper benchmarks 12 modern LLMs using EverGreenQA to assess their ability to recognize question temporality, both explicitly (through prompting) and implicitly (through uncertainty signals). Furthermore, they develop EG-E5, a lightweight multilingual classifier for identifying evergreen questions, achieving state-of-the-art performance. The authors demonstrate the practical utility of their approach in improving self-knowledge estimation, curating QA datasets, and explaining the retrieval behavior of GPT-4o.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key contribution of this paper lies in introducing the EverGreenQA dataset and the subsequent analysis of LLMs' temporal awareness. The dataset itself fills a gap in existing resources, providing a multilingual benchmark specifically tailored to evaluate the evergreen/mutable distinction. The development of the EG-E5 classifier as a SOTA tool for evergreen question detection is also noteworthy.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in the field of trustworthy QA – the lack of consideration for question temporality. LLMs' struggle with answering mutable questions is a known issue, and this work provides a valuable tool for identifying such questions and potentially mitigating their impact on QA system performance. The applications demonstrated, such as improving self-knowledge estimation and explaining GPT-4o's behavior, further highlight the practical relevance of the research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Creation:** The effort in creating a multilingual, human-curated dataset is a significant strength. The validation and annotation process, as detailed in the appendix, appears rigorous.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The benchmark evaluation of multiple LLMs using both explicit and implicit signals offers a thorough understanding of the current state of temporal awareness in these models.
    *   **Practical Applications:** Demonstrating the usefulness of EG-E5 in real-world applications solidifies the significance of the work.
    *   **Lightweight Classifier:** The EG-E5 is a major contribution due to it's lightweight nature making it easier to deploy.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** While the dataset is well-curated, its size (4,757 examples) might be considered a limitation. Larger datasets are generally more robust for training and evaluating models, although the provided analysis shows that its a strong addition to available data.
    *   **Model Coverage:** Although the analysis covers 12 models, there are more current and emerging models which are not covered within the current analyses.

*   **Overall Impact:** The paper is well-written and provides a clear and compelling argument for the importance of considering question temporality in QA. The introduction of EverGreenQA and EG-E5 will likely spur further research in this area and contribute to the development of more reliable and trustworthy QA systems.  The insights gained from analyzing LLMs' temporal awareness can also inform the design of more effective retrieval and knowledge updating mechanisms.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

This paper presents a significant step forward in addressing the temporality issue in QA. The creation of EverGreenQA provides a much-needed resource, and the EG-E5 classifier serves as a practical tool for various applications. While the dataset size is a constraint and it does not evaluate the most up-to-date models, the thorough evaluation and practical applications significantly elevate the contribution beyond incremental advancements. This work opens up new avenues for research and has the potential to influence the design of future QA systems. The lack of evaluation on truly low resource systems is also a downside to the paper.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Creativity in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21116v1)
- **Authors**: Yi-Cheng Lin, Kang-Chieh Chen, Zhe-Yan Li, Tzu-Heng Wu, Tzu-Hsuan Wu, Kuan-Yu Chen, Hung-yi Lee, Yun-Nung Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language model (LLM)-driven multi-agent systems (MAS) are transforming how humans and AIs collaboratively generate ideas and artifacts. While existing surveys provide comprehensive overviews of MAS infrastructures, they largely overlook the dimension of \emph{creativity}, including how novel outputs are generated and evaluated, how creativity informs agent personas, and how creative workflows are coordinated. This is the first survey dedicated to creativity in MAS. We focus on text and image generation tasks, and present: (1) a taxonomy of agent proactivity and persona design; (2) an overview of generation techniques, including divergent exploration, iterative refinement, and collaborative synthesis, as well as relevant datasets and evaluation metrics; and (3) a discussion of key challenges, such as inconsistent evaluation standards, insufficient bias mitigation, coordination conflicts, and the lack of unified benchmarks. This survey offers a structured framework and roadmap for advancing the development, evaluation, and standardization of creative MAS.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper is a survey on the use of Large Language Model (LLM)-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) for creative tasks, focusing on text and image generation. It argues that while existing MAS surveys focus on infrastructure, they overlook the dimension of creativity. The survey proposes a framework for understanding creativity in MAS, encompassing: a taxonomy of agent proactivity and persona design; an overview of generation techniques (divergent exploration, iterative refinement, collaborative synthesis) and relevant datasets/metrics; and a discussion of key challenges like evaluation inconsistencies, bias, coordination conflicts, and lack of benchmarks. The authors aim to provide a structured framework and roadmap for advancing the development, evaluation, and standardization of creative MAS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit and focused survey of creativity within LLM-based MAS. While other surveys cover MAS architectures and mechanisms, this one directly addresses the often-neglected dimension of computational creativity in this specific context. This framing is novel and useful to the community. It systematically links MAS principles and techniques to creative outcomes, going beyond just describing infrastructure to analyzing creative workflows.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the survey is substantial. By highlighting the lack of emphasis on creativity evaluation, agent persona influence, and ideation techniques, the paper addresses a critical gap in current MAS research. The proposed framework for analyzing agent proactivity, generation techniques, and the identification of key challenges like benchmark scarcity and bias offer a strong foundation for future research directions. The identification of the trade-off between agent proactivity and user trust is another very important insight. This paper would be invaluable to researchers seeking to design, evaluate, and standardize creative MAS systems, thereby contributing to advancements in human-AI collaboration and AI-assisted content creation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Structure and Organization:** The paper is well-structured, with clearly defined sections, making the information easily accessible.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey provides a fairly comprehensive overview of relevant techniques, datasets, and evaluation metrics related to creativity in MAS.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework for analyzing creativity in MAS offers a useful lens for understanding and evaluating these systems.
    *   **Identification of Key Challenges:** The discussion of challenges highlights important areas for future research and development.
    *   **Focus on a relatively unexplored area.** Most MAS papers focus on infrastructural and performance aspects. This is a new and welcome perspective.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope (modalities):** Although the paper addresses text and image generation, expanding the survey to include other creative modalities (e.g., music, 3D modeling, code generation) would have increased its impact and generalizability. The lack of discussion of robotics could be seen as another such weakness,
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the survey covers a wide range of topics, certain sections (e.g., bias mitigation) could benefit from more in-depth analysis and concrete examples.
    *   **Limited Examples:** In certain areas like datasets, the number of examples discussed is limited and a comprehensive table listing their properties would have strengthened the analysis. A more direct link between the "problems/challenges" identified and the surveyed techniques might also be useful in framing future research.
    *   **Limited scope of frameworks surveyed:** It only included MAS frameworks, excluding single-agent pipelines that have incorporated iterative methods or similar techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:** The survey is likely to significantly influence the research community by providing a clear roadmap and framework for studying creativity in MAS. It has the potential to stimulate new research on agent persona design, creative workflow coordination, evaluation methodologies, and bias mitigation in these systems. The identification of benchmark scarcity will likely encourage the development of more standardized evaluation resources.

    *   **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**
        The score assigned acknowledges the paper's novelty and significance in addressing a gap in existing literature. The paper provides a well-structured framework for analyzing and understanding creativity in LLM-based MAS and offers actionable recommendations for future research, datasets and benchmarks. The main concerns are its limited scope and a relative lack of depth in certain areas. A deeper and more comprehensive exploration would have further enhanced its impact. While certain aspects could be stronger, the overall contribution justifies a strong score.

### Learning Single Index Models with Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21135v1)
- **Authors**: Anqi Tang, Youming Chen, Shuchen Xue, Zhaoqiang Liu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models (DMs) have demonstrated remarkable ability to generate diverse and high-quality images by efficiently modeling complex data distributions. They have also been explored as powerful generative priors for signal recovery, resulting in a substantial improvement in the quality of reconstructed signals. However, existing research on signal recovery with diffusion models either focuses on specific reconstruction problems or is unable to handle nonlinear measurement models with discontinuous or unknown link functions. In this work, we focus on using DMs to achieve accurate recovery from semi-parametric single index models, which encompass a variety of popular nonlinear models that may have {\em discontinuous} and {\em unknown} link functions. We propose an efficient reconstruction method that only requires one round of unconditional sampling and (partial) inversion of DMs. Theoretical analysis on the effectiveness of the proposed methods has been established under appropriate conditions. We perform numerical experiments on image datasets for different nonlinear measurement models. We observe that compared to competing methods, our approach can yield more accurate reconstructions while utilizing significantly fewer neural function evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a method for recovering signals from non-linear Single Index Model (SIM) measurements using diffusion models (DMs) as priors.  Unlike prior work which either focuses on linear measurements or known non-linearities, this work aims at the more general case of semi-parametric SIMs, where the link function is unknown. The proposed approach, called SIM-DMIS, leverages a combination of DM inversion and sampling, and crucially, it starts the DM inversion process from an *intermediate* time point rather than the end (noise). The paper provides theoretical analysis supporting this approach and demonstrates its effectiveness through numerical experiments on image datasets under various non-linear measurement models (1-bit and cubic), showing improved reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency compared to existing methods. The authors claim the method doesn't require knowledge of the non-linear link function and needs fewer neural function evaluations (NFEs).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific combination of DM inversion and sampling within the context of SIMs with *unknown* non-linearities, especially the strategic use of intermediate inversion start times justified by theoretical insights on measurement noise levels. Previous DM-based recovery methods often assume linearity or a known non-linearity. The intermediate inversion is heuristically motivated and a theoretical justification is provided. While similar ideas exist for *sampling* (intermediate sampling) it's less common for the *inversion* aspect which is significant.

*   **Significance:** The problem is significant. SIMs are more realistic than linear compressed sensing, and many real-world problems introduce non-linear measurements (e.g., quantization, non-linear sensors). A method that can accurately recover signals from such measurements *without* knowing the exact non-linearity is a valuable contribution.  The demonstrated improvements in performance (PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS) and particularly the *significant reduction* in computational cost (NFEs) are important for practical applicability. The work builds upon established techniques (DMs, Generative priors), but the specific adaptation and optimization for SIMs is non-trivial.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem formulation and motivation.
    *   A theoretically grounded (although heuristic) approach.
    *   Experimental results demonstrating improved performance in accuracy and computational efficiency compared to relevant baselines.
    *   The ablation studies, provide insight into the impact of different parameters.
    *   The demonstration across multiple non-linear models (1-bit and cubic) adds robustness to the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical analysis relies on certain assumptions (e.g., regarding the link function and neural network properties) that may not always hold in practice.  While the paper attempts to justify them, the generality of these assumptions could be a point of concern.
    *   The parameter tuning process for Cs and C' is somewhat empirical and could benefit from a more principled approach or adaptive mechanism.
    *   While the method handles unknown non-linearities, there’s a potential limitation: the method still depends on setting t* (and thus C'), so the performance might degrade when the true non-linearity significantly deviates from what the parameter is tuned for. There is no analysis of sensitivity to variations in the nonlinearity, and the range of nonlinearities covered is somewhat limited to those similar to cubic or sign function.
    *   There's a lack of sensitivity analysis for the choice of "intermediate time". Justifying the choice from a theoretical perspective is good, but empirical evaluation of results achieved with different intermediate times is missing.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work can influence the field by:
    *   Providing a more practical approach to signal recovery in SIM settings.
    *   Inspiring further research into the integration of DMs for non-linear inverse problems.
    *   Motivating development of more robust and adaptive DM-based recovery methods.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the area of signal recovery using diffusion models. The combination of DM inversion and sampling, and the intermediate time approach, is a novel strategy for SIMs with unknown non-linearities. The practical benefits of faster computation with more accurate reconstruction are significant. However, the method is not without limitations.  The theoretical analysis is somewhat heuristic and depends on assumptions. Sensitivity analysis on different nonlinearities are missing.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging LLM and Self-Supervised Training Models for Speech Recognition in Chinese Dialects: A Comparative Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21138v1)
- **Authors**: Tianyi Xu, Hongjie Chen, Wang Qing, Lv Hang, Jian Kang, Li Jie, Zhennan Lin, Yongxiang Li, Xie Lei
- **Abstract**: Large-scale training corpora have significantly improved the performance of ASR models. Unfortunately, due to the relative scarcity of data, Chinese accents and dialects remain a challenge for most ASR models. Recent advancements in self-supervised learning have shown that self-supervised pre- training, combined with large language models (LLM), can effectively enhance ASR performance in low-resource scenarios. We aim to investigate the effectiveness of this paradigm for Chinese dialects. Specifically, we pre-train a Data2vec2 model on 300,000 hours of unlabeled dialect and accented speech data and do alignment training on a supervised dataset of 40,000 hours. Then, we systematically examine the impact of various projectors and LLMs on Mandarin, dialect, and accented speech recognition performance under this paradigm. Our method achieved SOTA results on multiple dialect datasets, including Kespeech. We will open-source our work to promote reproducible research
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of combining self-supervised pre-training with large language models (LLMs) for speech recognition in Chinese dialects.  It pre-trains a Data2vec2 model on a large dataset (300,000 hours) of unlabeled dialect and accented speech data and performs alignment training on a supervised dataset (40,000 hours).  The paper systematically examines the impact of different speech encoders, projectors, and LLMs on the ASR performance of Mandarin, dialectal, and accented speech.  The approach achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) results on multiple dialect datasets, including Kespeech, even with a smaller LLM and lower frame rate. The authors commit to open-sourcing their work to promote reproducible research. Key findings include the effectiveness of Data2Vec2 as a speech encoder, the superiority of fully connected layers as projectors in the early stages of training, and the benefits of a four-stage training strategy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty.  The general approach of combining self-supervised speech models (like Data2Vec2) with LLMs for ASR is not entirely novel (e.g., similar approaches are used in SLAM-ASR). However, it is novel in the specific context of *Chinese dialects*, where data scarcity is a significant challenge. The systematic comparison of different projectors and the proposal of a four-stage training strategy are also contributions, but arguably incremental. The claim of "SOTA" is a positive, however it is based on their own datasets, reducing novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a real-world problem: the lack of good ASR systems for Chinese dialects. By showing that the LLM+self-supervised training paradigm works well in this context, it provides a valuable contribution.  The systematic evaluation of different projectors provides practical guidance to researchers and practitioners. The four-stage training strategy is likely to be useful to other researchers. Open sourcing the code is a major strength, enhancing reproducibility and impact.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic Evaluation: The paper thoroughly evaluates different components of the architecture, including speech encoders, projectors, and LLMs.
    *   Large-Scale Data: The use of a large (300,000 hour) unlabeled dataset and a 40,000 hour labeled dataset demonstrates the scalability of the approach.
    *   SOTA results: The paper reports superior results compared with previous work.
    *   Open Source: The commitment to open-sourcing the code and models is a huge strength, promoting reproducibility and adoption.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited novelty: While the specific application to Chinese dialects is novel, the overall architecture is building upon existing techniques (Data2Vec2 + LLM decoder).
    *   Internal Datasets: The exclusive use of internal datasets for testing (besides Kespeech) is a limitation. It makes it more difficult to compare results with other published work and raises questions about the generalizability of the findings. More open-sourced datasets for Chinese dialects would improve the results.
    *   Lack of detail on datasets: More specifics about the datasets used (e.g., class balance, recording quality, etc.) would be beneficial.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of speech recognition, particularly in the challenging area of Chinese dialects. The systematic evaluation of different components, the effective use of large-scale data, and the achievement of SOTA results are all positive. However, the incremental nature of the architectural improvements and the reliance on internal datasets for evaluation limit its overall impact. The open-sourcing of code and models somewhat compensates. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FastFace: Tuning Identity Preservation in Distilled Diffusion via Guidance and Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21144v1)
- **Authors**: Sergey Karpukhin, Vadim Titov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Aibek Alanov
- **Abstract**: In latest years plethora of identity-preserving adapters for a personalized generation with diffusion models have been released. Their main disadvantage is that they are dominantly trained jointly with base diffusion models, which suffer from slow multi-step inference. This work aims to tackle the challenge of training-free adaptation of pretrained ID-adapters to diffusion models accelerated via distillation - through careful re-design of classifier-free guidance for few-step stylistic generation and attention manipulation mechanisms in decoupled blocks to improve identity similarity and fidelity, we propose universal FastFace framework. Additionally, we develop a disentangled public evaluation protocol for id-preserving adapters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FastFace: Tuning Identity Preservation in Distilled Diffusion via Guidance and Attention."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of adapting identity-preserving (ID-preserving) generation techniques to distilled diffusion models, which offer faster inference but may compromise identity fidelity. The authors propose FastFace, a training-free framework that enhances ID-preserving generation in distilled diffusion models through two main components: (1) decoupled classifier-free guidance (DCG), which allows separate control over text and ID conditioning strengths, tuned for few-step sampling; and (2) attention manipulation, where attention maps in decoupled blocks are modified to focus more on facial regions, improving identity similarity. The paper evaluates FastFace on various distilled checkpoints, using both stylistic and realistic generation scenarios, and introduces a disentangled evaluation protocol for ID-preserving adapters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the **training-free adaptation** of existing ID-preserving adapters to distilled diffusion models, enabling a more efficient pipeline without the need for per-model finetuning. The DCG and attention manipulation techniques are incremental but relevant adaptations of existing concepts, tuned specifically for the few-step inference regime.  The introduction of a more granular evaluation protocol, distinguishing between stylistic and realistic generation scenarios, is a beneficial contribution. However, the core ideas of classifier-free guidance and attention manipulation are already well-established.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem in the field. Distilled diffusion models are becoming increasingly popular, and the ability to efficiently integrate them with ID-preserving techniques is valuable. The training-free approach is also a strong point, as it reduces the barrier to adoption for users with existing ID-preserving adapters. The more refined evaluation methodology adds clarity and reproducibility to the field.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The training-free nature of FastFace makes it easy to use with existing ID-preserving adapters.
    *   **Universality:** The method is demonstrated to work across different distilled diffusion models (Hyper, Lightning, LCM, Turbo).
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The paper includes a good set of experiments and metrics, including face similarity, CLIP score, and LAION Aesthetic score.  The specialized metrics, FFC and FSC, are appropriate.
    *   **Detailed Ablations:** The paper is strong on presenting detailed ablations of the effect of different parameters.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental techniques:** The core techniques are adaptations of existing methods. The classifier-free guidance and attention manipulation approaches are evolutionary rather than revolutionary.
    *   **Dependency on base adapter quality:** FastFace enhances existing adapters; it does not create ID-preserving capabilities from scratch. Its effectiveness depends on the quality of the base adapter.
    *   **Limited single-step analysis:** The paper notes limitations for single step generation, however single step generation is currently not a main line for image synthesis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by making ID-preserving generation more accessible and efficient. It provides a practical solution that can be easily adopted by researchers and practitioners working with distilled diffusion models. The new evaluation methodology could also promote more standardized and rigorous evaluation of ID-preserving techniques.
*   **Justification for score:** The paper makes a useful engineering contribution by addressing an important practical problem and offers a solid, well-evaluated solution. However, the techniques are primarily adaptations of existing methods, limiting the theoretical novelty and significant impact. The proposed approach has a clear practical application and the potential to encourage more refined evaluation methods.

### IKMo: Image-Keyframed Motion Generation with Trajectory-Pose Conditioned Motion Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21146v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Zhao, Yan Zhang, Xubo Yang
- **Abstract**: Existing human motion generation methods with trajectory and pose inputs operate global processing on both modalities, leading to suboptimal outputs. In this paper, we propose IKMo, an image-keyframed motion generation method based on the diffusion model with trajectory and pose being decoupled. The trajectory and pose inputs go through a two-stage conditioning framework. In the first stage, the dedicated optimization module is applied to refine inputs. In the second stage, trajectory and pose are encoded via a Trajectory Encoder and a Pose Encoder in parallel. Then, motion with high spatial and semantic fidelity is guided by a motion ControlNet, which processes the fused trajectory and pose data. Experiment results based on HumanML3D and KIT-ML datasets demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms state-of-the-art on all metrics under trajectory-keyframe constraints. In addition, MLLM-based agents are implemented to pre-process model inputs. Given texts and keyframe images from users, the agents extract motion descriptions, keyframe poses, and trajectories as the optimized inputs into the motion generation model. We conducts a user study with 10 participants. The experiment results prove that the MLLM-based agents pre-processing makes generated motion more in line with users' expectation. We believe that the proposed method improves both the fidelity and controllability of motion generation by the diffusion model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IKMo, a novel method for image-keyframed human motion generation conditioned on both trajectory and pose constraints.  It proposes a diffusion model with a decoupled control strategy, where trajectory and keyframe pose inputs are processed through parallel control pathways. Optimization and encoding modules refine and encode these inputs before they are fused via a motion ControlNet to guide the motion generation process.  The authors also integrate MLLM-based agents to pre-process user inputs, extracting motion descriptions, keyframe poses, and trajectories from text and images. Experiments on HumanML3D and KIT-ML datasets demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods under trajectory-keyframe constraints.  A user study suggests that IKMo generates motions that align better with user expectations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of several existing ideas, rather than a single groundbreaking contribution.  The decoupling of trajectory and pose control, while conceptually sound, builds upon existing trajectory-guided methods like OmniControl and keyframe-guided techniques. The use of a multi-agent MLLM system for preprocessing user input is also incremental, leveraging existing language models and pose estimation techniques. The use of images as keyframe cues is innovative, but still depends on extracting poses from the images first. The design of the decoupled two-stage parallel modules handles trajectory constraints and keyframe pose constraints is an novel contribution as well.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work is moderate.  Improving the controllability of human motion generation is a valuable goal for applications in animation, virtual reality, and robotics.  IKMo's improved performance on standard datasets indicates a practical advancement in this area.  The user study also suggests that IKMo leads to more user-satisfying results than existing approaches. However, the improvements are incremental, and the dependence on intermediate pose extraction from images limits the overall robustness and fidelity of the system.

* **Strengths:**
    *  Clear and well-written explanation of the method and its components.
    *  Comprehensive experimental evaluation with comparisons to relevant baselines.
    *  Demonstrated improvements on established datasets (HumanML3D and KIT-ML).
    *  The integration of MLLM agents for pre-processing user input makes the system more user-friendly.
    * Comprehensive Ablation Study which reveals several modules contribute to generating high quality human motion generation results

* **Weaknesses:**
    *  Incremental novelty. The core ideas leverage existing techniques, leading to only moderate gains.
    *  Dependence on pose estimation from images is a bottleneck. Errors in pose estimation propagate through the system, limiting the overall quality of the generated motion. End-to-end models directly generate motion from images could solve this issue.
    *  Qualitative results, while promising, still show limitations in matching the target keyframe poses exactly.  The fine details, like hand motions, are not perfectly generated.
    *  Limited details are available for the multi-agent system used to extract and generate model input.
    * The limitations of multi-agent system such as reliance on intermediate pose extraction may introduce errors

* **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a moderate impact on the field. It provides a practical improvement over existing methods for controllable human motion generation. It provides valuable insights into designing motion generation systems that leverage multimodal inputs. The techniques and findings in this paper could inspire future research on end-to-end image-to-motion generation and more sophisticated methods for fusing multimodal constraints.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper. While the approach demonstrates measurable improvements in the quality and controllability of generated human motion, its novelty is somewhat limited. The core ideas build upon existing techniques, and the dependence on pose estimation from images is a significant bottleneck. However, the well-executed implementation, comprehensive evaluation, and user study demonstrate a practical and valuable contribution to the field, which could influence future research in controllable human motion generation.

### Assessment of L2 Oral Proficiency using Speech Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21148v1)
- **Authors**: Rao Ma, Mengjie Qian, Siyuan Tang, Stefano Bannò, Kate M. Knill, Mark J. F. Gales
- **Abstract**: The growing population of L2 English speakers has increased the demand for developing automatic graders for spoken language assessment (SLA). Historically, statistical models, text encoders, and self-supervised speech models have been utilised for this task. However, cascaded systems suffer from the loss of information, while E2E graders also have limitations. With the recent advancements of multi-modal large language models (LLMs), we aim to explore their potential as L2 oral proficiency graders and overcome these issues. In this work, we compare various training strategies using regression and classification targets. Our results show that speech LLMs outperform all previous competitive baselines, achieving superior performance on two datasets. Furthermore, the trained grader demonstrates strong generalisation capabilities in the cross-part or cross-task evaluation, facilitated by the audio understanding knowledge acquired during LLM pre-training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of speech large language models (LLMs) for automated L2 English oral proficiency assessment. It compares various training strategies (classification with cross-entropy loss, classification with fair average loss, and regression) and decoding approaches.  The study uses two datasets, Linguaskill and Speak & Improve Corpus 2025 (S&I), and compares the performance of the speech LLM (Qwen2-Audio) against strong baselines including wav2vec 2.0 and a cascaded BERT-based system. The results show that the speech LLM, particularly when trained with a fair average loss, outperforms previous baselines. The paper also investigates the generalisation capabilities of the trained models in cross-part and cross-task evaluations, showing robust performance even with limited training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Application of LLMs to a Relevant Problem:** The application of LLMs, specifically multimodal models like Qwen2-Audio, to automated L2 speaking assessment is a timely and relevant research direction. The growing need for scalable and consistent assessment methods makes this area significant.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Design:** The paper systematically compares different training strategies and decoding methods, providing valuable insights into how to best adapt LLMs for holistic scoring. The use of two distinct datasets adds robustness to the findings.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements over established baselines, showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The use of a fair average loss function to address the nuances of human scoring is a novel and effective technique.
    *   **Generalisation Analysis:** The investigation of cross-part and cross-task generalisation is a crucial aspect, highlighting the potential of speech LLMs to learn robust and transferable features.  The finding that read-aloud data can be surprisingly effective suggests practical benefits for data collection.
    * **Improved Performance in Low Resource Settings:** Demonstrated robust performance when training data is limited.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in LLM Architecture:**  The paper uses a pre-existing LLM architecture (Qwen2-Audio) and focuses primarily on training strategies rather than architectural innovations. While the application is novel, the underlying LLM is not.  The work is mostly focused on *adapting* an LLM rather than creating or fundamentally changing it.
    *   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** The paper focuses on quantitative metrics (RMSE, PCC, SRC) but lacks a qualitative analysis of the model's errors.  Examining *why* the model makes certain errors and analyzing the types of linguistic features it struggles with would provide a deeper understanding of its limitations and potential for improvement.  For instance, error analysis might reveal biases related to specific accents or grammatical errors.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Resources:** The paper does not provide extensive details about the computational resources required for training and inference, which are crucial for practical deployment. It only mentions that a batch size of 64 is used on Linguaskill.
    *   **Dependence on CEFR Descriptors:**  The prompt engineering relies on the CEFR descriptors, which may introduce bias or limit the model's ability to assess aspects of language proficiency not explicitly covered in the descriptors. An ablation study varying the prompt would strengthen the analysis.
    * **Data Splitting Transparency:** Lack of transparency on data splitting procedure can introduce bias or limit generalizability of results.

*   **Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by demonstrating the potential of speech LLMs for automated L2 speaking assessment.  The comprehensive evaluation and the superior performance compared to previous baselines provide strong evidence for the viability of this approach.  The generalisation analysis further enhances the practical value of the findings. While the architectural novelty is limited, the insights into effective training strategies and decoding methods are valuable for researchers and practitioners in the field.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.

*   The paper addresses a relevant problem with a timely approach (speech LLMs).
*   It demonstrates compelling empirical results, outperforming strong baselines.
*   The systematic evaluation of training strategies and the generalisation analysis are valuable contributions.

However, the limited architectural novelty, lack of detailed error analysis, and missing computational resource discussions detract from its overall impact. Additionally, while the LLM outperforms the baselines, the novelty of directly using LLMs to perform the speaking proficiency assessment may be considered incremental due to other work applying LLMs to assessment tasks, as acknowledged in the paper. The limitations warrant a score below 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### TAT-R1: Terminology-Aware Translation with Reinforcement Learning and Word Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21172v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Li, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Wenjie Yang
- **Abstract**: Recently, deep reasoning large language models(LLMs) like DeepSeek-R1 have made significant progress in tasks such as mathematics and coding. Inspired by this, several studies have employed reinforcement learning(RL) to enhance models' deep reasoning capabilities and improve machine translation(MT) quality. However, the terminology translation, an essential task in MT, remains unexplored in deep reasoning LLMs. In this paper, we propose \textbf{TAT-R1}, a terminology-aware translation model trained with reinforcement learning and word alignment. Specifically, we first extract the keyword translation pairs using a word alignment model. Then we carefully design three types of rule-based alignment rewards with the extracted alignment relationships. With those alignment rewards, the RL-trained translation model can learn to focus on the accurate translation of key information, including terminology in the source text. Experimental results show the effectiveness of TAT-R1. Our model significantly improves terminology translation accuracy compared to the baseline models while maintaining comparable performance on general translation tasks. In addition, we conduct detailed ablation studies of the DeepSeek-R1-like training paradigm for machine translation and reveal several key findings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TAT-R1, a terminology-aware machine translation model trained using reinforcement learning (RL) and word alignment techniques.  The core idea is to improve the accuracy of translating key terminology by designing reward functions that leverage word alignment information between the source text, reference translation, and the model's predicted translation.  The model extracts keyword translation pairs using word alignment and designs three novel alignment-based rewards:  answer-align-word, answer-align-order, and think-align-word.  These rewards are combined with COMET and format rewards to guide the RL training process.  Experiments demonstrate improved terminology translation accuracy compared to baseline models while maintaining comparable performance on general translation tasks.  The paper also presents ablation studies exploring the impact of different reward components and the effectiveness of the DeepSeek-R1-like training paradigm for machine translation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution is the integration of word alignment and specifically designed reward functions within an RL framework to improve terminology translation.  While RL for MT and terminology-aware MT have been explored before, the specific combination of word alignment for reward design and its application in a DeepSeek-R1-like setting appears novel. Using alignment information in this way helps to address the problem of accurately translating key terms without relying heavily on pre-existing terminology databases or term detection methods. It provides an alternative approach that explicitly encourages the model to learn correct term mappings.
*   **Significance:**  Accurate terminology translation is vital in many real-world MT applications.  By focusing on this often-overlooked aspect, the paper addresses a practical problem.  The empirical results demonstrate a clear improvement in terminology translation accuracy, which could have a positive impact on downstream tasks that rely on accurate terminology. The ablation studies also provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of different reward components and the role of reasoning processes in translation. The authors have made the code, data, and models publicly available, which will allow other researchers to reproduce the findings and build upon this work.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper proposes a clear and well-defined approach with a strong motivation.
    *   The reward functions are thoughtfully designed and intuitively explained.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in terminology translation accuracy.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the different aspects of the proposed approach.
    *   The paper follows good research practices by releasing the code, data, and models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The word alignment model used (SimAlign) is relatively simple and unsupervised. The quality of alignment impacts the reward, and more sophisticated alignment models (even if supervised) could lead to further improvement.
    *   The reward design could be refined. The authors assume nouns are "key elements," but terminology isn't limited to nouns. A more sophisticated term identification process would enhance the methodology.
    *   The experimental setup is based on a limited set of language pairs (mainly Chinese-English and English-German).  It's unclear if the approach generalizes well to other language pairs or domains.
    *   The reported results are based on a specific set of metrics, including COMET and BLEU. While these metrics are widely used, they have known limitations. Additional qualitative analysis and human evaluations could provide a more comprehensive assessment of translation quality.
    * The baseline is a basic fine-tuned LLM. There is no comparison to other terminology-aware MT methods (e.g., retrieval-augmented generation with terminology databases) which somewhat diminishes the comparative value of the approach.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on terminology-aware machine translation. The approach of using word alignment for reward design is promising and can be explored further with different alignment models and reward functions.  The insights from the ablation studies can guide the development of more effective RL-based translation models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to terminology-aware machine translation using RL and word alignment. The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. However, the limitations related to the word alignment model, reward function design, experimental setup, lack of comparison to other term translation approaches and metrics prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects this balance: it is a solid contribution with good potential for future work and impact, but it's not revolutionary in its current form.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SOLIDGEO: Measuring Multimodal Spatial Math Reasoning in Solid Geometry
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21177v1)
- **Authors**: Peijie Wang, Chao Yang, Zhong-Zhi Li, Fei Yin, Dekang Ran, Mi Tian, Zhilong Ji, Jinfeng Bai, Cheng-Lin Liu
- **Abstract**: Geometry is a fundamental branch of mathematics and plays a crucial role in evaluating the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing multimodal mathematics benchmarks mainly focus on plane geometry and largely ignore solid geometry, which requires spatial reasoning and is more challenging than plane geometry. To address this critical gap, we introduce SolidGeo, the first large-scale benchmark specifically designed to evaluate the performance of MLLMs on mathematical reasoning tasks in solid geometry. SolidGeo consists of 3,113 real-world K-12 and competition-level problems, each paired with visual context and annotated with difficulty levels and fine-grained solid geometry categories. Our benchmark covers a wide range of 3D reasoning subjects such as projection, unfolding, spatial measurement, and spatial vector, offering a rigorous testbed for assessing solid geometry. Through extensive experiments, we observe that MLLMs encounter substantial challenges in solid geometry math tasks, with a considerable performance gap relative to human capabilities on SolidGeo. Moreover, we analyze the performance, inference efficiency and error patterns of various models, offering insights into the solid geometric mathematical reasoning capabilities of MLLMs. We hope SolidGeo serves as a catalyst for advancing MLLMs toward deeper geometric reasoning and spatial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SOLIDGEO: Measuring Multimodal Spatial Math Reasoning in Solid Geometry":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SOLIDGEO, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the spatial and mathematical reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) in the domain of solid geometry.  The benchmark consists of 3,113 problems sourced from K-12 curricula and mathematics competitions, each paired with visual context and annotated with difficulty levels and fine-grained solid geometry categories.  The paper analyzes the performance of various MLLMs on this benchmark, revealing significant performance gaps compared to human performance and highlighting the limitations of existing models in handling complex solid geometry tasks. It emphasizes the importance of solid geometry for spatial intelligence and the need for more focused research in this area. The authors make the dataset publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is the introduction of SOLIDGEO, a dedicated benchmark for solid geometry reasoning in MLLMs. While existing benchmarks exist for mathematical reasoning, they often lack substantial solid geometry content or focus on basic recognition tasks. SOLIDGEO addresses this gap by providing a relatively large and diverse set of problems spanning a range of solid geometry concepts. The fine-grained categorization (8 categories) is also a valuable feature, enabling more detailed analysis of model capabilities. However, the benchmark heavily utilizes existing data, with only ~44% curated by the team.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights the importance of spatial reasoning, particularly in 3D, as a crucial aspect of general intelligence and a stepping stone toward AGI. By focusing on solid geometry, the authors emphasize a domain that is both challenging and under-explored in the context of MLLMs. Demonstrating the performance gap between MLLMs and human capabilities on SOLIDGEO underscores the need for advancements in spatial reasoning within these models. The detailed analysis of model strengths, weaknesses, and efficiency provides valuable insights for future research directions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset Creation:**  The creation of a relatively large and diverse dataset with real-world problems.
    *   **Fine-Grained Categorization:** The use of  8 solid geometry subcategories offers a more nuanced evaluation of model capabilities.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The thorough evaluation of numerous MLLMs and in-depth analysis of model performance.
    *   **Analysis of Model Behavior:**  Detailed analysis of efficiency metrics, error patterns, highlighting key challenges and limitations.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Composition:** While the size is a strength, a notable portion (~56%) of SOLIDGEO is derived from existing datasets.  The novelty is somewhat diminished by reusing existing resources. More detail regarding the specific augmentations the team implemented would strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the dataset incorporates English and Chinese, there exists potential for improvements by considering other languages to improve its applicability to the wider range of research.

*   **Potential Influence:**  SOLIDGEO has the potential to become a valuable resource for the MLLM research community.  It provides a standardized benchmark for evaluating and comparing models, and its fine-grained categorization facilitates targeted improvements in specific areas of spatial reasoning. The publicly available dataset could inspire further research and development of more sophisticated spatial reasoning techniques.

*   **Justification for Score:**  The creation of a dedicated and well-annotated benchmark for solid geometry is a valuable contribution. However, a more substantial emphasis on original content within the dataset and a broader exploration of dataset sources would have increased the paper's overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Walk Before You Run! Concise LLM Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21178v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng
- **Abstract**: As test-time scaling becomes a pivotal research frontier in Large Language Models (LLMs) development, contemporary and advanced post-training methodologies increasingly focus on extending the generation length of long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) responses to enhance reasoning capabilities toward DeepSeek R1-like performance. However, recent studies reveal a persistent overthinking phenomenon in state-of-the-art reasoning models, manifesting as excessive redundancy or repetitive thinking patterns in long CoT responses. To address this issue, in this paper, we propose a simple yet effective two-stage reinforcement learning framework for achieving concise reasoning in LLMs, named ConciseR. Specifically, the first stage, using more training steps, aims to incentivize the model's reasoning capabilities via Group Relative Policy Optimization with clip-higher and dynamic sampling components (GRPO++), and the second stage, using fewer training steps, explicitly enforces conciseness and improves efficiency via Length-aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (L-GRPO). Significantly, ConciseR only optimizes response length once all rollouts of a sample are correct, following the "walk before you run" principle. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that our ConciseR model, which generates more concise CoT reasoning responses, outperforms recent state-of-the-art reasoning models with zero RL paradigm across AIME 2024, MATH-500, AMC 2023, Minerva, and Olympiad benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper titled "Walk Before You Run! Concise LLM Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ConciseR, a two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to generate more concise Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning responses from Large Language Models (LLMs) without sacrificing accuracy. The first stage uses Group Relative Policy Optimization with clip-higher and dynamic sampling (GRPO++) and an entropy bonus to enhance reasoning capabilities. The second stage employs Length-aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (L-GRPO) to explicitly enforce conciseness by rewarding shorter responses only when all rollouts for a given sample are correct. The "walk before you run" principle ensures length optimization occurs only after adequate reasoning ability is established. Experimental results demonstrate that ConciseR outperforms existing zero-RL baselines on AIME 2024, MATH-500, AMC 2023, Minerva, and Olympiad benchmarks while significantly reducing response lengths. The authors publicly release their code, training dataset, and model checkpoints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to improving LLM reasoning by focusing on conciseness. While RL for CoT reasoning is not entirely new, the two-stage approach, especially the emphasis on ensuring accuracy before optimizing for length, is a notable contribution. The introduction of L-GRPO and its specific reward structure to penalize excessive length *after* accuracy has been reached offers a unique perspective compared to methods that penalize length during the entire training process. The GRPO++ with clip-higher and dynamic sampling and adding the entropy bonus appear to be reasonably standard additions to GRPO, however the combination of these techniques in the initial stage with GRPO++ is interesting to improve exploration.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem: the tendency of LLMs to generate verbose and sometimes redundant reasoning chains, leading to computational inefficiencies and, potentially, reduced accuracy. By promoting conciseness without compromising performance, ConciseR offers a valuable pathway towards more efficient and reliable LLMs. The extensive experimental results across multiple benchmarks provide strong evidence of the effectiveness of the approach. The analysis of the changing reasoning patterns, although based on keyword occurrences (which is a limitation), offers insights into how the model adapts its strategies during training. The failure case is also a nice touch, explaining why the original approach of L-GRPO failed. Overall, the paper's significance lies in providing a practical and well-evaluated method for generating concise CoT responses without sacrificing accuracy.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.
    *   A compelling rationale for the proposed approach ("walk before you run").
    *   A well-defined two-stage framework that isolates reasoning ability improvement from length optimization.
    *   Extensive experimental validation across multiple benchmarks.
    *   Ablation experiments and insightful analysis of the changes in reasoning patterns.
    *   Public release of code, dataset, and model checkpoints.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The analysis of reasoning patterns is based on keyword occurrences, which can be a crude measure of more nuanced changes in thinking styles. More sophisticated methods, such as analyzing semantic similarity between CoT steps, could provide a deeper understanding.
    *   The computational cost of the approach is not explicitly discussed. While the paper aims to improve efficiency by reducing response length, the overhead of RL training and sampling needs to be considered.
    *   While extensive, the benchmarks are primarily mathematical. It would be beneficial to evaluate ConciseR on other reasoning tasks, such as common-sense reasoning or natural language inference. The improvements and failure cases are interesting, but more samples with side-by-side analysis could improve the results.
    *   The novelty of each individual component, like clip-higher and dynamic sampling is limited.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   ConciseR could influence future research in RL-based LLM training, encouraging a more balanced approach to reasoning ability and efficiency.
    *   The "walk before you run" principle could be adopted in other contexts where multi-objective optimization is required.
    *   The public release of resources could facilitate further experimentation and development in this area.

**Overall Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, I assign it a score of **7.5**. This is because while the overall framework is interesting and has a strong emphasis on ensuring accuracy before optimizing the length, the overall findings and components are not completely novel and are extensions from existing works like DAPO. The paper proposes and shows that the two-stage method of accuracy followed by length optimization has merit, and does a reasonable job explaining the benefits of this two-stage method over previous one-stage method. While the results are good, the individual pieces that makeup ConciseR are not super cutting edge.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Normalized Attention Guidance: Universal Negative Guidance for Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21179v1)
- **Authors**: Dar-Yen Chen, Hmrishav Bandyopadhyay, Kai Zou, Yi-Zhe Song
- **Abstract**: Negative guidance -- explicitly suppressing unwanted attributes -- remains a fundamental challenge in diffusion models, particularly in few-step sampling regimes. While Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) works well in standard settings, it fails under aggressive sampling step compression due to divergent predictions between positive and negative branches. We present Normalized Attention Guidance (NAG), an efficient, training-free mechanism that applies extrapolation in attention space with L1-based normalization and refinement. NAG restores effective negative guidance where CFG collapses while maintaining fidelity. Unlike existing approaches, NAG generalizes across architectures (UNet, DiT), sampling regimes (few-step, multi-step), and modalities (image, video), functioning as a \textit{universal} plug-in with minimal computational overhead. Through extensive experimentation, we demonstrate consistent improvements in text alignment (CLIP Score), fidelity (FID, PFID), and human-perceived quality (ImageReward). Our ablation studies validate each design component, while user studies confirm significant preference for NAG-guided outputs. As a model-agnostic inference-time approach requiring no retraining, NAG provides effortless negative guidance for all modern diffusion frameworks -- pseudocode in the Appendix!
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Normalized Attention Guidance: Universal Negative Guidance for Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Normalized Attention Guidance (NAG), a novel, training-free method for improving negative prompting in diffusion models.  Negative prompting, which suppresses unwanted attributes in generated images, is crucial for control and content refinement.  The authors observe that the common Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) method often fails in few-step diffusion models due to divergent predictions between positive and negative branches. NAG addresses this by applying extrapolation in attention feature space, followed by L1 normalization and refinement. This approach stabilizes the guidance process, prevents out-of-manifold feature drift, and allows for more effective control across various architectures (UNet, DiT), sampling regimes (few-step, multi-step), and modalities (image, video). The method is presented as a universal plug-in that requires no retraining and demonstrates improvements in text alignment, fidelity, and human-perceived quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of NAG lies in its approach to negative guidance by operating directly within the attention space.  While prior work like NASA [13] also manipulates attention, NAG distinguishes itself through the integration of L1 normalization and feature refinement.  These mechanisms address a key limitation of existing methods, namely instability and out-of-manifold feature drift, especially in modern DiT architectures.  Extrapolating in the feature space is novel as it circumvents the reliance on aligned branches that plague CFG in limited sampling steps.

**Significance:**

The significance of NAG is multi-faceted:

*   **Improved Control:** NAG provides a more robust and controllable way to suppress unwanted attributes in generated images, enhancing the creative potential and practical applications of diffusion models (e.g., content safety, debiasing).
*   **Universal Applicability:** The method's claim of universal applicability across different architectures, sampling regimes, and modalities is significant.  It reduces the need for specialized guidance techniques for each specific setting.
*   **Training-Free:** The fact that NAG is training-free is a substantial advantage. It simplifies integration into existing diffusion pipelines and avoids the computational burden of retraining models.
*   **Few-Step Diffusion:**  The paper addresses a critical problem of few-step diffusion models, enhancing control in highly efficient inference scenarios.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of CFG and existing attention-based guidance methods.
*   **Well-Defined Solution:** The NAG method is well-defined and explained, with justifications for each component (extrapolation, normalization, refinement).
