*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation, including quantitative metrics (CLIP Score, FID, ImageReward), qualitative results, ablation studies, and user studies.  The breadth of tested architectures and sampling regimes strengthens the claim of universality.
*   **Geometric Interpretation:** The geometric interpretation of NAG is intuitive and aids in understanding the method's behavior.
*   **PyTorch Style Pseudo Code:** Providing the PyTorch style pseudo code enables reproducibility and enables easy utilization in other projects.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Failure cases:** While the paper demonstrates significant improvements in most cases, it also acknowledges some failure cases, where NAG struggles to fully suppress certain concepts or may introduce instability. Further investigation into the root cause and mitigation strategies for these failures would strengthen the work. Figure 11's failure case descriptions were vague and require better analysis of when NAG fails.
*   **Parameter sensitivity:** The ablation studies investigate the parameters *tau* and *alpha*, but a more systematic analysis of their impact across different models and datasets could be beneficial. A more rigorous guide for finding the optimal hyper parameters, or developing a more efficient automatic hyperparameter optimization, would be more helpful.
*   **Additional computational cost:** While the paper claims that NAG adds minimal computational cost, the experimental results reported are not significantly low, and can be improved.
*   **Limited comparative work:** The paper compares NAG with NASA, but doesn't extensively compare it to every other related work discussed in the paper.

**Potential Influence:**

NAG has the potential to become a widely adopted technique for negative guidance in diffusion models. Its training-free nature, universal applicability, and improved control are attractive features that could significantly impact the field.  It provides a strong baseline for future research on attention-based guidance and control mechanisms. It also provides a framework for analyzing attention feature manipulation and stabilizing the process via extrapolation, normalization, and refinement.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

NAG presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of diffusion models by providing a stable, effective, and training-free method for negative guidance. The method addresses a key limitation of existing approaches, particularly in few-step diffusion models, and is thoroughly evaluated across various settings.  While some limitations and failure cases exist, the strengths of NAG outweigh the weaknesses, making it a valuable addition to the diffusion modeling toolkit. The novelty of extrapolating in the feature space with added constraints, makes it a significant research contribution with high potential for future applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### PoisonSwarm: Universal Harmful Information Synthesis via Model Crowdsourcing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21184v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Yan, Sheng Sun, Zhifei Zheng, Ziji Hao, Teli Liu, Min Liu
- **Abstract**: To construct responsible and secure AI applications, harmful information data is widely utilized for adversarial testing and the development of safeguards. Existing studies mainly leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) to synthesize data to obtain high-quality task datasets at scale, thereby avoiding costly human annotation. However, limited by the safety alignment mechanisms of LLMs, the synthesis of harmful data still faces challenges in generation reliability and content diversity. In this study, we propose a novel harmful information synthesis framework, PoisonSwarm, which applies the model crowdsourcing strategy to generate diverse harmful data while maintaining a high success rate. Specifically, we generate abundant benign data as the based templates in a counterfactual manner. Subsequently, we decompose each based template into multiple semantic units and perform unit-by-unit toxification and final refinement through dynamic model switching, thus ensuring the success of synthesis. Experimental results demonstrate that PoisonSwarm achieves state-of-the-art performance in synthesizing different categories of harmful data with high scalability and diversity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PoisonSwarm: Universal Harmful Information Synthesis via Model Crowdsourcing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PoisonSwarm, a novel framework for synthesizing harmful information at scale using a model crowdsourcing approach.  Instead of relying on a single Large Language Model (LLM) which is often safety-aligned and thus unreliable for generating toxic content, PoisonSwarm decomposes the process.  It generates benign data using counterfactual methods, segments this data into semantic units, and then uses multiple LLMs, dynamically switched, to toxify each unit. This method aims to overcome the limitations of existing approaches that struggle with both reliability (due to LLM safety mechanisms) and content diversity when creating harmful datasets.  The paper demonstrates through experiments that PoisonSwarm achieves state-of-the-art performance in generating diverse and scalable harmful data, and emphasizes its effectiveness for adversarial testing and AI security enhancement. A key contribution is the strong-weak LLM collaboration, where strong LLMs are used for safer components of the task and weak LLMs for riskier operations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the "model crowdsourcing" approach coupled with a strong-weak LLM collaboration framework. While counterfactual generation and LLM-based data synthesis are not entirely new, PoisonSwarm combines these elements in a unique way to address the specific challenge of reliably and diversely generating harmful data.  The dynamic switching between models based on performance is also a novel aspect. Prior work often focuses on jailbreaking, but this work decouples the risky operations from the safer operations to overcome the limitations of current methods.
*   **Significance:** Generating high-quality, diverse harmful data is crucial for developing robust AI safety measures, specifically for adversarial testing and training of detection systems.  PoisonSwarm offers a potentially valuable tool for researchers and practitioners in this area. The paper makes an important statement on the challenges the AI industry faces as AI generated data continues to be used in research. The recognition that simply trying to jailbreak models isn't enough and that we need a more comprehensive approach is critical.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The decomposition of the harmful data synthesis process is well-reasoned and effectively mitigates the limitations of individual LLMs.
    *   The dynamic model switching mechanism adds robustness and improves the success rate of generation.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate significant performance gains over existing methods across several evaluation metrics.
    *   The interpretative analysis provides valuable insights into the characteristics of AI-generated harmful content and its impact on detection systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experiments are thorough, the choice of baselines could be expanded. Comparing to more state-of-the-art data augmentation techniques could strengthen the claims.
    *   The reliance on LLMs for evaluation metrics like toxicity and naturalness introduces some subjectivity. Ideally, human evaluations could be added to validate these metrics further.
    *   The paper lacks a thorough discussion on the ethical implications of generating and distributing harmful information, even for research purposes. This is a critical omission.
    *   While the counterfactual mappings improve diversity, the actual process of counterfactual transformation may introduce undesirable semantic shifts or biases that are not fully addressed.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence research in AI safety, adversarial training, and harmful content detection.  It presents a practical and effective method for generating diverse harmful data, which can be used to improve the robustness of AI systems against malicious attacks and harmful content. The study also prompts further research on the safe and ethical development of AI-driven data synthesis techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.5 to this paper. While the paper's core ideas are well-executed and the experimental results are promising, there are several limitations that prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The lack of a strong ethical discussion is a major concern, and the reliance on LLMs for evaluation metrics could be improved with human validation. The paper has a decent level of novelty, but could use a stronger comparison to different adversarial generation methods to demonstrate the significance of PoisonSwarm. However, the paper introduces an interesting take on adversarial testing and provides several promising experiments that should encourage more researchers to investigate this domain.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring the Latent Capacity of LLMs for One-Step Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21189v1)
- **Authors**: Gleb Mezentsev, Ivan Oseledets
- **Abstract**: A recent study showed that large language models (LLMs) can reconstruct surprisingly long texts - up to thousands of tokens - via autoregressive generation from just one specially trained input embedding. In this work, we explore whether such reconstruction is possible without autoregression. We show that frozen LLMs can generate hundreds of accurate tokens in just one forward pass, when provided with only two learned embeddings. This reveals a surprising and underexplored capability of LLMs - multi-token generation without iterative decoding. We investigate the behaviour of these embeddings and provide insight into the type of information they encode. We also empirically show that although these representations are not unique for a given text, they form connected and local regions in embedding space - a property that suggests the potential of learning a dedicated encoder into that space.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the latent capacity of Large Language Models (LLMs) for one-step text generation, challenging the conventional autoregressive paradigm.  It demonstrates that frozen, pre-trained LLMs can generate surprisingly long and accurate text sequences (up to hundreds of tokens) in a single forward pass using only two specially trained input embeddings (called "proto-tokens"). The research investigates the arrangement and information content of these proto-tokens, revealing their distinct roles, and analyzes the geometric properties of the embedding space. The results suggest that the proto-tokens do not simply store the text tokens but encode higher-level representations, and that the embedding space exhibits favorable characteristics for potential encoder development. Finally, they compare the throughput achieved to autoregressive generation and find their approach to be significantly faster.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to text generation by bypassing autoregressive generation, which has been the de facto standard in the field. While Kuratov et al. (2025) explored autoregressive generation from single embeddings, this paper's move to a single-pass, non-autoregressive generation is significant. The analysis of the proto-tokens' roles and the structure of the embedding space adds further depth. The idea of having two proto-tokens, with one acting as an "attention sink" is an interesting insight. However, the concept of pre-training an embedding to generate text is not completely novel, and the core idea extends the previous work in the field by removing autoregression.

*   **Significance:** The research offers several potential benefits. The possibility of fast, non-autoregressive text generation opens up new avenues for applications in resource-constrained environments, on-device processing, and fast context compression/decompression. Moreover, the finding that proto-tokens capture not just text but also potential contextual information and can be connected using Bezier curves hints at a learnable encoding of text that might bypass computationally intensive generation. The potential for faster inference is particularly significant. The experiments demonstrate that, while sacrificing some sequence length, significant speed-ups are possible.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the experimental setup and results.
    *   The ablation studies, especially the investigation of different token arrangements and the sharing of tokens, provide valuable insights.
    *   The analysis of the embedding space and the attempts to characterize the information encoded in proto-tokens are insightful.
    *   The throughput experiments demonstrate a clear advantage over autoregressive methods in terms of processing speed.
    *   The work is very reproducible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The work lacks a "killer app" or a clear practical application. The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   The architectural dependence (different behavior across model families) raises concerns about the generalizability of the findings. While the work focuses on Llama-3 and Pythia families, performance with other models (and the architectures) is not addressed.
    *   The paper does not provide a compelling explanation for *why* this single-pass generation is possible. A deeper theoretical understanding of the LLM's internal representations would strengthen the work.
    *   The compression ratio is limited. While impressive, hundreds of tokens compared to possible thousands using autoregressive models with the same model is a significant drawback.
    *   Experiment coverage of different LLM architectures and the role of architectural differences in achieving single-pass generation is lacking.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research in several directions:
    *   Development of encoders that map text into the proto-token embedding space.
    *   Exploration of new LLM architectures that are better suited for non-autoregressive generation.
    *   Applications of fast context compression/decompression in specific domains.
    *   Inquiry into the precise mechanisms by which LLMs encode and decode information in proto-tokens.

*   **Conclusion:** The paper makes a novel contribution by showing how LLMs can perform non-autoregressive text generation. It reveals the inherent capacity of LLMs to "compress" the essence of a text within a few learnable vectors, and then to decompress those vectors into coherent text in a single forward pass. While the study also identifies several challenges and limitations in this research direction, the demonstrated potential is substantial. The authors did a good job performing ablation studies and analyzing the latent space that the tokens occupy, as well as compare to autoregressive generation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper's novelty and significance are solid, but not groundbreaking. The move from autoregressive generation using a single embedding (Kuratov et al.) to single-pass generation *is* a meaningful step. The experiments are well-designed and executed, and the results offer interesting insights into the inner workings of LLMs. The throughput gains are potentially significant. However, the lack of a demonstrated practical application, limited generalizability, and absence of a clear theoretical understanding detract from the overall impact. If the authors could demonstrate a tangible use case or provide a deeper theoretical justification for their findings, the impact score would be higher. While the paper is clearly valuable, it requires further development before it can be considered a transformative contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unveiling Instruction-Specific Neurons & Experts: An Analytical Framework for LLM's Instruction-Following Capabilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21191v1)
- **Authors**: Junyan Zhang, Yubo Gao, Yibo Yan, Jungang Li, Zhaorui Hou, Sicheng Tao, Shuliang Liu, Song Dai, Yonghua Hei, Junzhuo Li, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: The finetuning of Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly advanced their instruction-following capabilities, yet the underlying computational mechanisms driving these improvements remain poorly understood. This study systematically examines how fine-tuning reconfigures LLM computations by isolating and analyzing instruction-specific sparse components, i.e., neurons in dense models and both neurons and experts in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures. In particular, we introduce HexaInst, a carefully curated and balanced instructional dataset spanning six distinct categories, and propose SPARCOM, a novel analytical framework comprising three key contributions: (1) a method for identifying these sparse components, (2) an evaluation of their functional generality and uniqueness, and (3) a systematic comparison of their alterations. Through experiments, we demonstrate functional generality, uniqueness, and the critical role of these components in instruction execution. By elucidating the relationship between fine-tuning-induced adaptations and sparse computational substrates, this work provides deeper insights into how LLMs internalize instruction-following behavior for the trustworthy LLM community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unveiling Instruction-Specific Neurons & Experts: An Analytical Framework for LLM's Instruction-Following Capabilities":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how fine-tuning impacts the internal computational mechanisms of Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve their instruction-following abilities. The authors introduce HEXAINST, a balanced instructional dataset, and SPARCOM, an analytical framework for identifying, evaluating, and comparing instruction-specific sparse components (neurons and experts in MoE models). SPARCOM consists of identifying specific neurons/experts, evaluating their functional generality and uniqueness, and comparing their alterations after fine-tuning. The research demonstrates functional generality and uniqueness of these components, revealing their critical role in instruction execution. The study provides insights into how fine-tuning shapes LLMs and their ability to internalize instruction-following behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of Approach:** The use of SPARCOM is a novel and well-defined framework for analyzing LLM internal mechanisms. By focusing on sparse components, the research delves into a focused and tractable area for understanding model behavior.
    *   **Balanced Dataset:** The construction of HEXAINST is a significant contribution, addressing the limitations of existing datasets with its balanced representation of different instruction categories. This helps to control for biases.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The three-step SPARCOM framework (identification, evaluation, comparison) provides a structured approach for analyzing instruction-specific components. The methodology is clearly articulated and applied consistently throughout the experiments.
    *   **Valuable Insights:** The findings regarding the functional generality and uniqueness of instruction-specific neurons/experts provide valuable insights into how LLMs process instructions and how fine-tuning affects their internal representations. The insights into MoE models and specialization in different expertise is very beneficial for future model development.
    *   **Contribution to Interpretability:** This work contributes significantly to the interpretability of LLMs, a critical area for building trustworthy and controllable AI systems.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The paper does a good job in studying the different types of models and instructions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope of Instructions:** While HEXAINST is balanced, the limited number of instruction categories (six) may constrain the generalizability of the findings to all possible types of instructions.
    *   **Computational Intensity:** Conducting experiments on all models and instructions takes much time and resources. While they have sampled certain instances, a wider range of instances could increase the reliability of results.
    *   **Limited model selection**: The current work only focused on several types of open-source models.
    *   **Limited MoE layers studies**: Due to resources constrains, there is no detailed investigation in different layers of MoE models.
    *   **Lack of Causal Intervention:** While the paper demonstrates correlations between specific neurons/experts and instruction-following, it does not establish causal relationships through interventions (e.g., ablating or manipulating specific components and observing the downstream effects). Without such intervention, it is difficult to claim causality with certainty.

*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper provides a strong foundation for further research into the interpretability of LLMs and the mechanisms underlying instruction following.
    *   The analytical framework can be adapted and extended to investigate other aspects of LLM behavior, such as reasoning and generalization.
    *   The insights gained from this research can inform the design of more efficient and controllable LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed study that significantly advances our understanding of the internal mechanisms of LLMs related to instruction following. The development of the HEXAINST dataset and the SPARCOM framework are important contributions that provide valuable tools for analyzing model behavior. The findings offer compelling evidence for the functional generality and uniqueness of instruction-specific neurons/experts, providing insights into how fine-tuning shapes LLMs.

While the scope of instructions and models is limited and the study lacks causal interventions, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. The research provides a strong foundation for future work in this area and has the potential to influence the design and interpretability of LLMs. The balance of novelty, methodological rigor, and potential impact warrant a score of 7.5.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sci-Fi: Symmetric Constraint for Frame Inbetweening
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21205v1)
- **Authors**: Liuhan Chen, Xiaodong Cun, Xiaoyu Li, Xianyi He, Shenghai Yuan, Jie Chen, Ying Shan, Li Yuan
- **Abstract**: Frame inbetweening aims to synthesize intermediate video sequences conditioned on the given start and end frames. Current state-of-the-art methods mainly extend large-scale pre-trained Image-to-Video Diffusion models (I2V-DMs) by incorporating end-frame constraints via directly fine-tuning or omitting training. We identify a critical limitation in their design: Their injections of the end-frame constraint usually utilize the same mechanism that originally imposed the start-frame (single image) constraint. However, since the original I2V-DMs are adequately trained for the start-frame condition in advance, naively introducing the end-frame constraint by the same mechanism with much less (even zero) specialized training probably can't make the end frame have a strong enough impact on the intermediate content like the start frame. This asymmetric control strength of the two frames over the intermediate content likely leads to inconsistent motion or appearance collapse in generated frames. To efficiently achieve symmetric constraints of start and end frames, we propose a novel framework, termed Sci-Fi, which applies a stronger injection for the constraint of a smaller training scale. Specifically, it deals with the start-frame constraint as before, while introducing the end-frame constraint by an improved mechanism. The new mechanism is based on a well-designed lightweight module, named EF-Net, which encodes only the end frame and expands it into temporally adaptive frame-wise features injected into the I2V-DM. This makes the end-frame constraint as strong as the start-frame constraint, enabling our Sci-Fi to produce more harmonious transitions in various scenarios. Extensive experiments prove the superiority of our Sci-Fi compared with other baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Sci-Fi: Symmetric Constraint for Frame Inbetweening" addresses a key limitation in current frame inbetweening methods based on Image-to-Video Diffusion Models (I2V-DMs).  It identifies that naively extending I2V-DMs to incorporate end-frame constraints results in weaker influence from the end frame compared to the start frame, leading to inconsistencies and artifacts in the generated intermediate frames. To overcome this, the authors propose a novel framework, "Sci-Fi," which introduces a lightweight module called EF-Net to enhance the end-frame constraint injection.  EF-Net encodes the end frame and generates temporally adaptive frame-wise features that are explicitly injected into the I2V-DM, effectively making the end-frame constraint as strong as the start-frame constraint.  Experimental results demonstrate that Sci-Fi produces more harmonious and consistent video transitions compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to frame inbetweening. The key innovation lies in the design of EF-Net and its strategic insertion into the pre-trained I2V-DM. The core idea of addressing the asymmetry between start and end frame influence through a tailored injection mechanism is a good one.

**Significance:**  Frame inbetweening is a crucial area in video processing, with applications in animation, video editing, and slow-motion effects. The paper addresses a concrete and important problem (inconsistent motion) in state-of-the-art I2V-DM based approaches. The improvements shown are visually compelling, suggesting a practical benefit.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Identification:**  The paper clearly identifies a genuine weakness in existing I2V-DM-based frame inbetweening methods, making a strong case for the need for a new approach.
*   **Technical Solution:**  The EF-Net module is relatively lightweight and appears to be well-designed for its purpose of enhancing end-frame influence. The description of its integration into the I2V-DM is clear.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper presents quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating the superiority of Sci-Fi over a range of baselines, including comparisons on standard datasets (DAVIS, Pexels) and a cartoon dataset.  The ablation studies further validate the design choices within EF-Net.
*   **User Study:** The inclusion of a user study provides compelling evidence that human observers prefer the results generated by Sci-Fi, adding weight to the claims of improved motion quality and visual appeal.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the concepts and methodology clearly.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the concept is novel, the technical contribution is more of an incremental improvement over existing I2V-DM techniques.  It's not a radically new paradigm shift but a targeted enhancement to address a specific problem.
*   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The reliance on pre-trained I2V-DMs is a common practice, but it also means that the performance of Sci-Fi is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying base model.  While acknowledged, it means its impact is limited by these.
*   **Limited Generalization:** The paper mentions limitations in scenarios involving fast and large-scale body movements and the movement of small objects.  A deeper discussion of these failure modes and potential solutions would be beneficial.
*   **Limited Scope of Applications:** The paper focuses on the specific task of frame inbetweening. It would be beneficial to explore the potential broader impact of the approach for other tasks such as object insertion or other video editing processes.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of frame inbetweening by addressing a real problem in existing I2V-DM-based methods.  The design of EF-Net is innovative, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the contribution is more of an incremental improvement rather than a revolutionary breakthrough. Although the paper does acknowledge some limitations, it could have also explored and proposed some solutions in regards to these limitations. Furthermore, the reliance on pre-trained models, while practical, somewhat restricts the potential impact of the work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pretrained LLMs Learn Multiple Types of Uncertainty
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21218v1)
- **Authors**: Roi Cohen, Omri Fahn, Gerard de Melo
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models are known to capture real-world knowledge, allowing them to excel in many downstream tasks. Despite recent advances, these models are still prone to what are commonly known as hallucinations, causing them to emit unwanted and factually incorrect text. In this work, we study how well LLMs capture uncertainty, without explicitly being trained for that. We show that, if considering uncertainty as a linear concept in the model's latent space, it might indeed be captured, even after only pretraining. We further show that, though unintuitive, LLMs appear to capture several different types of uncertainty, each of which can be useful to predict the correctness for a specific task or benchmark. Furthermore, we provide in-depth results such as demonstrating a correlation between our correction prediction and the model's ability to abstain from misinformation using words, and the lack of impact of model scaling for capturing uncertainty. Finally, we claim that unifying the uncertainty types as a single one using instruction-tuning or [IDK]-token tuning is helpful for the model in terms of correctness prediction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, as requested:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) inherently capture uncertainty during pre-training, even without explicit training for that purpose. The authors propose a method to identify linear "uncertainty vectors" within LLMs' latent spaces. These vectors correlate with the correctness of the models' own generations. The study reveals that LLMs encode multiple, distinct uncertainty representations associated with different datasets or knowledge types. Furthermore, the paper explores the impact of model depth, size, and training techniques (instruction-tuning, [IDK]-tuning) on uncertainty encoding. The results indicate that intermediate layers are most informative, model size isn't a decisive factor, and targeted training methods significantly enhance uncertainty capture.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its analytical framework for probing uncertainty within LLMs' latent spaces and its findings regarding the multiple, distinct forms of uncertainty encoding. While previous work has addressed model calibration and hallucination mitigation, this paper delves into *how* uncertainty is represented internally, rather than simply addressing its consequences. The identification of linear uncertainty vectors and their dataset-specificity is a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** Understanding how LLMs handle uncertainty is crucial for improving their reliability and mitigating hallucinations. The paper's findings offer potential avenues for developing targeted training strategies to enhance uncertainty awareness and reduce misinformation. Furthermore, it challenges the common assumption that simply scaling model size will address these issues.

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The paper presents a clear and reproducible methodology for identifying and evaluating uncertainty vectors.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The study involves a diverse set of LLMs, datasets, and training techniques, strengthening the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Insightful Findings:** The discovery of multiple, distinct uncertainty representations and their relationship to dataset type is particularly interesting and provides a new perspective on LLM behavior.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper includes a detailed analysis of the effects of model depth, size, and training methods, providing valuable insights for future research.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to follow the methodology and interpret the results.

* **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Linearity Assumption:** The reliance on linear probes might limit the ability to capture more complex, non-linear uncertainty representations.  The paper acknowledges this limitation.  While justified as a starting point, it's an area for future exploration.
    *   **Correctness as Proxy:** Using correctness as a proxy for uncertainty might not fully capture the nuances of uncertainty representation. There could be cases where a model is "uncertain" but still produces the correct answer, or vice versa.
    *   **Limited Scope on Real-World Applications:** While the findings are insightful, the paper could benefit from more discussion of how these uncertainty vectors can be directly applied to solve real-world problems, such as improving fact verification or building more reliable dialogue systems.
    *   **Dataset Bias:**  The paper acknowledges potential dataset bias influencing results. While this is a common limitation, further investigation into how specific datasets affect uncertainty vector properties would be beneficial.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on model calibration, hallucination mitigation, and interpretability of LLMs. The framework for probing uncertainty representations could be adapted for other types of analysis, and the findings regarding dataset-specificity could inform the development of more robust and adaptable LLMs. The work underscores the need for targeted training strategies beyond simple scaling.

* **Critical Nuance:** While the paper demonstrates that LLMs *do* capture uncertainty, it doesn't fully explain *why* they capture it in the ways they do, specifically the dataset-specific nature. This raises questions about the pre-training data and the learning process. Further investigation into the specific data patterns that contribute to these distinct uncertainty representations would strengthen the conclusions.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper represents a solid contribution to the field by providing a novel framework for analyzing uncertainty within LLMs and uncovering important insights about its representation. Its strengths include a well-defined methodology, comprehensive experiments, and insightful findings. While the paper has some limitations, such as the linearity assumption and proxy for uncertainty, its potential influence on future research in the field is significant. The discovery of multiple, distinct uncertainty representations is particularly notable and could have important implications for developing more reliable and trustworthy LLMs. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's significant contribution while acknowledging its limitations and areas for future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LMCD: Language Models are Zeroshot Cognitive Diagnosis Learners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21239v1)
- **Authors**: Yu He, Zihan Yao, Chentao Song, Tianyu Qi, Jun Liu, Ming Li, Qing Huang
- **Abstract**: Cognitive Diagnosis (CD) has become a critical task in AI-empowered education, supporting personalized learning by accurately assessing students' cognitive states. However, traditional CD models often struggle in cold-start scenarios due to the lack of student-exercise interaction data. Recent NLP-based approaches leveraging pre-trained language models (PLMs) have shown promise by utilizing textual features but fail to fully bridge the gap between semantic understanding and cognitive profiling. In this work, we propose Language Models as Zeroshot Cognitive Diagnosis Learners (LMCD), a novel framework designed to handle cold-start challenges by harnessing large language models (LLMs). LMCD operates via two primary phases: (1) Knowledge Diffusion, where LLMs generate enriched contents of exercises and knowledge concepts (KCs), establishing stronger semantic links; and (2) Semantic-Cognitive Fusion, where LLMs employ causal attention mechanisms to integrate textual information and student cognitive states, creating comprehensive profiles for both students and exercises. These representations are efficiently trained with off-the-shelf CD models. Experiments on two real-world datasets demonstrate that LMCD significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both exercise-cold and domain-cold settings. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/TAL-auroraX/LMCD
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes LMCD (Language Models as Zeroshot Cognitive Diagnosis Learners), a framework designed to address the cold-start problem in cognitive diagnosis (CD).  LMCD leverages large language models (LLMs) in two main phases: 1) **Knowledge Diffusion**, where the LLM generates enriched descriptions of exercises and knowledge concepts (KCs) to enhance semantic connections, and 2) **Semantic-Cognitive Fusion**, where the LLM integrates textual information with student-specific tokens using a causal attention mechanism to create comprehensive student and exercise profiles. These profiles are then used to train standard CD models. Experiments on two real-world datasets (NIPS34 and XES3G5M) demonstrate LMCD's superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods in both exercise-cold and domain-cold settings. A key contribution is the use of LLMs' causal attention mechanisms to model the relative difficulty of an exercise for a specific student.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the novel application of LLMs' causal attention mechanisms within the cognitive diagnosis framework. Existing works have used LLMs for encoding exercise text or generating summaries, but LMCD explicitly models relative difficulty by fusing semantic and cognitive information via attention mechanisms. This is a potentially significant shift in how CD models can be built, particularly when dealing with sparse data. Knowledge Diffusion is also a reasonable extension to existing LM approaches, providing richer context for both exercises and KCs.

*   **Significance:** The cold-start problem is a well-known and challenging issue in cognitive diagnosis, limiting the practical applicability of many CD models. If LMCD performs as the paper suggests, it could significantly improve the performance of CD systems in real-world educational environments where data is often scarce. The paper's focus on relative difficulty is also important because it acknowledges that the challenge of an exercise depends on the individual student. The empirical results support the claimed improvements. The consistent gains over baselines, especially in challenging cross-domain settings, are compelling.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the cold-start problem and its limitations.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The authors provide a solid rationale for using LLMs and specifically for their proposed Knowledge Diffusion and Semantic-Cognitive Fusion components.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The extensive experiments on two datasets provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of LMCD. Ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The authors have publicly released the code, enhancing reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **New Student Cold-Start:** As the authors acknowledge in the Limitations section, the method is limited to diagnosing new students, as the student embeddings need trained students for the method to be effective.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The computational cost of LLMs might limit its deployment in resource-constrained settings. The paper could discuss strategies for model compression or distillation.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper is empirically driven, with a limited theoretical justification for *why* the causal attention mechanism is so effective. A more in-depth analysis could strengthen the claims.
    *   **Baselines:** While baselines are relatively strong, it would have been helpful to include other recent advances.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the approach proves robust and scalable, it could significantly impact the design of personalized learning systems.  The explicit modeling of relative difficulty could become a standard practice in the field.  The code release should also encourage further research and adoption of the technique.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated approach to a significant problem in cognitive diagnosis.  The use of LLMs and, particularly, the causal attention mechanism for relative difficulty modeling is a valuable contribution. The empirical results are compelling. However, the limitations regarding new student diagnosis, the computational cost, and limited theoretical analysis prevent a higher score. It is a strong and promising contribution with clear potential for future research and impact, despite these shortcomings.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluation of LLMs in Medical Text Summarization: The Role of Vocabulary Adaptation in High OOV Settings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21242v1)
- **Authors**: Gunjan Balde, Soumyadeep Roy, Mainack Mondal, Niloy Ganguly
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) recently achieved great success in medical text summarization by simply using in-context learning. However, these recent efforts do not perform fine-grained evaluations under difficult settings where LLMs might fail. They typically report performance scores over the entire dataset. Through our benchmarking study, we show that LLMs show a significant performance drop for data points with high concentration of out-of-vocabulary (OOV) words or with high novelty. Vocabulary adaptation is an intuitive solution to this vocabulary mismatch issue where the LLM vocabulary gets updated with certain expert domain (here, medical) words or subwords. An interesting finding from our study is that Llama-3.1, even with a vocabulary size of around 128K tokens, still faces over-fragmentation issue with medical words. To that end, we show vocabulary adaptation helps improve the LLM summarization performance even in difficult settings. Through extensive experimentation of multiple vocabulary adaptation strategies, two continual pretraining strategies, and three benchmark medical summarization datasets, we gain valuable insights into the role of vocabulary adaptation strategies for customizing LLMs to the medical domain. We also performed a human evaluation study with medical experts where they found that vocabulary adaptation results in more relevant and faithful summaries. Our codebase is made publicly available at https://github.com/gb-kgp/LLM-MedicalSummarization-Benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper evaluates the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in medical text summarization, focusing on challenging scenarios with high Out-Of-Vocabulary (OOV) words and novelty (presence of words in the reference summary not found in the source document). The authors highlight that even large LLMs with substantial vocabulary sizes still struggle with over-fragmentation of medical terms. They benchmarked various vocabulary adaptation techniques, continual pretraining strategies, and datasets. The authors find that vocabulary adaptation improves summarization performance, particularly in difficult settings, and enhances relevance, coherence, and faithfulness. The results indicate that models improved on medical summarization tasks by vocabulary adaptation and refinement of the pre-training process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of this paper resides in its rigorous fine-grained benchmarking of LLMs for medical text summarization under realistic and challenging conditions. While vocabulary adaptation and continual pretraining are not novel *concepts*, their application and evaluation in this specific context, with a focus on OOV and novelty challenges, and especially considering *recent* (Llama-3.1) open source LLMs, constitute a valuable contribution. The paper goes beyond simply reporting overall scores and analyzes performance in specific, difficult scenarios. Further, the AdaptBPE integration is a nuanced improvement that addresses tokenization shortcomings of current LLMs, especially with over-fragmentation. The comprehensive experimentation, human evaluation, and release of the code contribute to its value. The finding that even large vocabulary models need adaptation and tokenization schemes needs modification for specialized domains is itself significant.

**Significance:**

The significance lies in several aspects:

*   **Addressing a Practical Problem:** Medical text summarization has the potential to greatly assist healthcare professionals. This paper identifies and tackles a key obstacle: the vocabulary mismatch between general-purpose LLMs and the specialized medical domain.
*   **Guidance for Practitioners:** The benchmarking provides valuable insights for those seeking to adapt LLMs for medical tasks. The comparison of different vocabulary adaptation strategies and continual pretraining approaches offers practical guidance.
*   **Highlighting Limitations:** The paper explicitly points out situations where vocabulary adaptation does *not* help, such as low OOV and novelty settings.  It clarifies the applicability of such techniques, preventing over-reliance in the wrong scenarios.
*   **Improving summarization fidelity:** Human evaluation by medical experts confirm improved reliability of summaries obtained by vocabulary adaption.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Benchmarking:** The extensive experimental setup, covering multiple models, datasets, adaptation techniques, and evaluation metrics, provides a comprehensive assessment.
*   **Fine-Grained Analysis:**  The focus on specific challenging scenarios, with OOV and novelty considerations, is a major strength.
*   **Practical Focus:** The paper addresses a realistic problem in a practical setting.
*   **Open Codebase:** Releasing the codebase promotes reproducibility and enables further research.
*   **Human Evaluation:** Validating the findings with medical experts adds credibility.
*   **Ablation and Error Analysis:**  The analysis where the methods failed is valuable for guiding future research.
*   **Inclusion of Recently Open Sourced LLMs** Llama 3.1 is tested, adding to the paper's up-to-date nature.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Sizes:** The models are limited by the available hardware and, as acknowledged, does not explore LLMs larger than 8B. Although the LLMs are reasonably sized, there is potential for scalability of these techniques to be significantly different on 70B models.
*   **Parameter-Efficient Training:** The experiments rely on LoRA, as parameter efficient fine tuning is the standard practice in current literature.
*   **Dataset size:** While the 20K subset selected is based on empirical validation, the pre-training data may not be extensive enough.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in medical text summarization. It can guide the development of more effective adaptation techniques, inform the design of specialized medical LLMs, and encourage more fine-grained evaluation in future studies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by conducting an important and thorough evaluation of LLMs in a challenging, real-world scenario. The fine-grained analysis, practical focus, and open codebase enhance its value. The paper does not introduce radically new ideas but provides a valuable and rigorous assessment of existing techniques with practical impact. I have to keep this down to earth, and be very very critical, since this is more of an engineering work than a new theoretic result.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReSCORE: Label-free Iterative Retriever Training for Multi-hop Question Answering with Relevance-Consistency Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21250v1)
- **Authors**: Dosung Lee, Wonjun Oh, Boyoung Kim, Minyoung Kim, Joonsuk Park, Paul Hongsuck Seo
- **Abstract**: Multi-hop question answering (MHQA) involves reasoning across multiple documents to answer complex questions. Dense retrievers typically outperform sparse methods like BM25 by leveraging semantic embeddings; however, they require labeled query-document pairs for fine-tuning. This poses a significant challenge in MHQA due to the high variability of queries (reformulated) questions throughout the reasoning steps. To overcome this limitation, we introduce Retriever Supervision with Consistency and Relevance (ReSCORE), a novel method for training dense retrievers for MHQA without labeled documents. ReSCORE leverages large language models to capture each documents relevance to the question and consistency with the correct answer and use them to train a retriever within an iterative question-answering framework. Experiments on three MHQA benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of ReSCORE, with significant improvements in retrieval, and in turn, the state-of-the-art MHQA performance. Our implementation is available at: https://leeds1219.github.io/ReSCORE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ReSCORE paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReSCORE, a novel method for training dense retrievers in multi-hop question answering (MHQA) systems without requiring manually labeled query-document relevance pairs. ReSCORE leverages large language models (LLMs) to estimate the relevance of a document to a question and its consistency with the correct answer. This estimated relevance is then used as a pseudo-ground truth label to train a dense retriever within an iterative question-answering framework. The experiments on three MHQA benchmarks (MuSiQue, 2WikiMHQA, and HotpotQA) demonstrate that ReSCORE significantly improves retrieval quality and leads to state-of-the-art MHQA performance.  The authors create a system called IQATR ("equator") which uses their ReSCORE-trained retriever.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLM-generated pseudo-labels for training dense retrievers for MHQA is reasonably novel. The paper effectively combines the concepts of relevance and consistency into a single pseudo-label generation process, addressing a key challenge in training retrievers for iterative MHQA systems where reformulated queries during iterations can be diverse.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to train dense retrievers for MHQA without the need for expensive, manually labeled data. This makes it more practical to apply dense retrievers to MHQA, which has been challenging due to the data requirements. The performance improvements on standard MHQA benchmarks, achieving state-of-the-art results, highlights the effectiveness of ReSCORE and its potential to advance the field. The thorough analysis of different pseudo-labeling strategies and query reformulation methods further strengthens the contribution. The iterative training aspect is also important because it deals directly with the nature of multi-hop question answering.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a practical problem:** Labeling data for MHQA retrieval is difficult and expensive.
    *   **Effective use of LLMs:**  The paper effectively leverages LLMs to generate pseudo-labels in a way that captures both relevance and consistency.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experiments on multiple benchmarks show significant improvements over baselines and state-of-the-art methods.
    *   **Thorough analysis:** The paper includes a detailed analysis of various components, such as different pseudo-labeling strategies and query reformulation methods, providing valuable insights into the method's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLM quality:** The quality of the pseudo-labels is dependent on the quality of the LLM used. The error rates of the LLM could be propagated to the retriever, which limits the system.
    *   **Computational cost:** The iterative training process and the reliance on LLMs make ReSCORE computationally expensive, especially for large datasets.
    *   **Limited generalizability:** While the paper shows strong results on the tested datasets, the method's generalizability to other MHQA datasets with different characteristics (e.g., datasets in different domains or with different question complexities) could be a limitation. While this would be the case with many retrieval models, it is important to keep in mind.
    *   **Limited Negative Sampling:** The paper samples a fixed number of top results. There is no explicit negative sampling other than the fact that the pseudo-GT targets are close to zero, creating a weak negative loss. A more comprehensive negative sampling scheme could improve the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** ReSCORE has the potential to influence future research in MHQA and retrieval-augmented generation. It provides a practical and effective approach to training dense retrievers for complex reasoning tasks. The idea of using LLMs to generate pseudo-labels can be extended to other tasks where labeled data is scarce. Furthermore, the iterative training framework can inspire new approaches for training retrievers in dynamic and complex environments.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the approach, its significance in addressing a practical problem in MHQA, the strong experimental results, and the potential to influence future research, but also acknowledging the limitations related to LLM dependency and computational cost, I would assign a score of **8**. The paper presents a solid contribution that advances the field of MHQA and offers valuable insights into training retrievers for complex reasoning tasks.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Breaking the Ceiling: Exploring the Potential of Jailbreak Attacks through Expanding Strategy Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21277v1)
- **Authors**: Yao Huang, Yitong Sun, Shouwei Ruan, Yichi Zhang, Yinpeng Dong, Xingxing Wei
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), despite advanced general capabilities, still suffer from numerous safety risks, especially jailbreak attacks that bypass safety protocols. Understanding these vulnerabilities through black-box jailbreak attacks, which better reflect real-world scenarios, offers critical insights into model robustness. While existing methods have shown improvements through various prompt engineering techniques, their success remains limited against safety-aligned models, overlooking a more fundamental problem: the effectiveness is inherently bounded by the predefined strategy spaces. However, expanding this space presents significant challenges in both systematically capturing essential attack patterns and efficiently navigating the increased complexity. To better explore the potential of expanding the strategy space, we address these challenges through a novel framework that decomposes jailbreak strategies into essential components based on the Elaboration Likelihood Model (ELM) theory and develops genetic-based optimization with intention evaluation mechanisms. To be striking, our experiments reveal unprecedented jailbreak capabilities by expanding the strategy space: we achieve over 90% success rate on Claude-3.5 where prior methods completely fail, while demonstrating strong cross-model transferability and surpassing specialized safeguard models in evaluation accuracy. The code is open-sourced at: https://github.com/Aries-iai/CL-GSO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Breaking the Ceiling: Exploring the Potential of Jailbreak Attacks through Expanding Strategy Space":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current black-box jailbreak attacks on Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that existing methods are constrained by their predefined strategy spaces, hindering their ability to bypass safety protocols in advanced, safety-aligned models. To overcome this limitation, the authors propose a novel framework called Component-Level Genetic-based Strategy Optimization (CL-GSO). CL-GSO decomposes jailbreak strategies into essential components based on the Elaboration Likelihood Model (ELM) theory (Role, Content Support, Context, Communication Skills). This decomposition allows for a larger and more diverse strategy space. The framework then uses genetic algorithms for efficient optimization within this expanded space, coupled with an intention consistency evaluation mechanism to ensure accurate assessment of jailbreak success. Experiments show that CL-GSO significantly outperforms existing methods on challenging safety-aligned models like Claude-3.5, achieving unprecedented jailbreak success rates. The paper also demonstrates strong cross-model transferability and superior evaluation accuracy compared to specialized safeguard models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of this paper lies in its strategy decomposition approach. Breaking down jailbreak attempts into ELM-inspired components and then recombining them through genetic algorithms is a genuinely original idea that differentiates this work from previous, largely prompt engineering-focused approaches. The concept of expanding the attack strategy space beyond predefined templates is significant.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings have substantial implications. By demonstrating that expanding the strategy space can lead to much higher jailbreak success rates, especially on models previously considered relatively secure, the paper highlights a critical vulnerability in current safety mechanisms. The findings question current security understanding and will hopefully guide developers to design even more robust safety mechanisms.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments provide compelling evidence of the effectiveness of CL-GSO. The achievement of over 90% success rate on Claude-3.5, where other methods fail, is particularly striking.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The CL-GSO framework is clearly articulated and well-motivated by the ELM theory. The genetic algorithm optimization and the intention consistency evaluation mechanism are logically integrated.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates the method across multiple datasets, models, and defensive strategies, providing a thorough assessment of its capabilities.
    *   **Cross-Model Transferability:** Demonstrating that the generated strategies transfer well to other models is valuable and indicates a potentially generalizable vulnerability.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on GPT-3.5 for Red-Teaming:** The choice of GPT-3.5 as a red-teaming model, while cost-effective, might limit the exploration of the strategy space compared to using a more powerful (though expensive) model.
    *   **Potential Over-reliance on ELM:** While the ELM theory provides a useful framework, the specific choice of the four components may not be exhaustive, and future research could explore alternative or additional components.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Specific Strategy Characteristics:** While the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of the expanded strategy space, it would be beneficial to have a more in-depth analysis of the specific characteristics of the successful strategies identified by CL-GSO. Why certain recombinations work better than others needs more investigation.
    *   **Ethical Concerns:** As the authors acknowledge, the ability to jailbreak LLMs at a high rate raises serious ethical concerns. While the authors commit to responsible disclosure, the potential for misuse of the techniques described requires careful consideration. The ethical considerations could be expanded further.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely have a significant influence on the field by shifting the focus from simple prompt engineering to a more comprehensive exploration of the attack strategy space. It may inspire new research directions in both jailbreak attacks and defense mechanisms for LLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper's novel approach to expanding the attack strategy space for LLMs, coupled with strong empirical results, justifies a high score. The limitations, however, hold it back from a perfect score. The reliance on GPT-3.5 for red-teaming, and the reliance of the components, means it is not yet perfect.
**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### RLJP: Legal Judgment Prediction via First-Order Logic Rule-enhanced with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21281v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Zhang, Zhiliang Tian, Shicheng Zhou, Haiyang Wang, Wenqing Hou, Yuying Liu, Xuechen Zhao, Minlie Huang, Ye Wang, Bin Zhou
- **Abstract**: Legal Judgment Prediction (LJP) is a pivotal task in legal AI. Existing semantic-enhanced LJP models integrate judicial precedents and legal knowledge for high performance. But they neglect legal reasoning logic, a critical component of legal judgments requiring rigorous logical analysis. Although some approaches utilize legal reasoning logic for high-quality predictions, their logic rigidity hinders adaptation to case-specific logical frameworks, particularly in complex cases that are lengthy and detailed. This paper proposes a rule-enhanced legal judgment prediction framework based on first-order logic (FOL) formalism and comparative learning (CL) to develop an adaptive adjustment mechanism for legal judgment logic and further enhance performance in LJP. Inspired by the process of human exam preparation, our method follows a three-stage approach: first, we initialize judgment rules using the FOL formalism to capture complex reasoning logic accurately; next, we propose a Confusion-aware Contrastive Learning (CACL) to dynamically optimize the judgment rules through a quiz consisting of confusable cases; finally, we utilize the optimized judgment rules to predict legal judgments. Experimental results on two public datasets show superior performance across all metrics. The code is publicly available{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/RLJP-FDF1}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RLJP, a novel framework for Legal Judgment Prediction (LJP) that enhances reasoning capabilities by integrating First-Order Logic (FOL) rules with Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing LJP models, which often rely on semantic matching and neglect the logical reasoning component crucial in legal judgments.  RLJP adopts a three-stage approach inspired by human exam preparation: 1) initializing judgment rules using FOL; 2) dynamically optimizing rules using a Confusion-Aware Contrastive Learning (CACL) mechanism with confusable cases; and 3) utilizing optimized rules to predict legal judgments via an LLM.  Experimental results on two public datasets (CAIL2018 and CJO22) demonstrate superior performance compared to baseline methods. The code is made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of FOL-based rule representation, dynamic rule optimization using contrastive learning specifically designed to handle confusable cases, and integration with LLMs.  While other works have used FOL or LLMs in LJP, the adaptive, data-driven approach to refining the logical rules, guided by a confusion-aware loss, appears to be a distinct contribution.  Specifically, the tree-splitting approach for rule optimization using confusable case sets and the CACL mechanism are the most innovative aspects.

*   **Significance:** LJP is a significant task in legal AI, with potential applications in assisting judicial professionals and providing legal consultation services. RLJP's focus on improving the logical reasoning component is a step towards more reliable and interpretable LJP systems.  The reported performance gains over strong baselines, including other LLM-based methods and semantic-based approaches, suggest that RLJP effectively addresses the limitations of existing models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation, identifying the gap in existing LJP models.
    *   Well-defined framework with a coherent and intuitive three-stage approach.
    *   The introduction of the Confusion-Aware Contrastive Learning (CACL) mechanism for dynamic rule optimization which is a novel loss function.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation against a variety of strong baselines on standard datasets.
    *   Demonstrated state-of-the-art results across various evaluation metrics.
    *   Publicly available code to facilitate future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Generalizability: Evaluation is conducted only on Chinese LJP datasets. While the approach is potentially language-agnostic, its effectiveness in other legal systems with different legal traditions, languages, and data availability remains uncertain.
    *   Interpretability Limitations: The paper acknowledges the limited interpretability of the judgment process, even with the explanation generated by FOL rules and COT.  A deeper analysis of the rules learned and the reasoning steps would be valuable.  How are conflicting rules resolved, and what biases might be introduced in the rule optimization process?

    *   Dependency on LLM: The approach relies heavily on LLMs for rule initialization, symbol definition, and optimization, making the system susceptible to the biases and limitations inherent in these models.  Further investigation into the impact of different LLMs and prompting strategies is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach to combining logical rules with LLMs has the potential to influence future research in LJP and other reasoning-intensive tasks. The CACL mechanism could be adapted to other domains where addressing confusable examples is important.  The framework could inspire more work on adaptive rule-based systems that can learn from data and refine their reasoning logic.
*   **Rigor:** Overall, the paper presents a rigorous and well-executed study, with clearly defined methods, comprehensive evaluation, and insightful discussion of the results. The description of the approach and experimental setup is complete and the ablations show the relative contributions of the components.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LJP by addressing the limitations of existing approaches and introducing a novel framework that effectively combines FOL rules with LLMs.  The dynamic rule optimization and the CACL mechanism are significant innovations. The empirical results are compelling. However, the limited generalizability and the dependency on LLMs prevent a higher score.  While the paper demonstrates strong performance on Chinese LJP datasets, its effectiveness in other legal systems needs further investigation. While the concept of legal reasoning via logic has been explored, the adaptive adjustment of logic through confusable examples improves on previous works, allowing for the nuanced interpretation of cases, compared to more rigid systems. The interpretability limitations also warrant a slightly lower score. If the paper offered an in-depth analysis of cases and how the rules changed through each stage of optimization, the score would be higher.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### PACT: A Contract-Theoretic Framework for Pricing Agentic AI Services Powered by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21286v1)
- **Authors**: Ya-Ting Yang, Quanyan Zhu
- **Abstract**: Agentic AI, often powered by large language models (LLMs), is becoming increasingly popular and adopted to support autonomous reasoning, decision-making, and task execution across various domains. While agentic AI holds great promise, its deployment as services for easy access raises critical challenges in pricing, due to high infrastructure and computation costs, multi-dimensional and task-dependent Quality of Service (QoS), and growing concerns around liability in high-stakes applications. In this work, we propose PACT, a Pricing framework for cloud-based Agentic AI services through a Contract-Theoretic approach, which models QoS along both objective (e.g., response time) and subjective (e.g., user satisfaction) dimensions. PACT accounts for computational, infrastructure, and potential liability costs for the service provider, while ensuring incentive compatibility and individual rationality for the user under information asymmetry. Through contract-based selection, users receive tailored service offerings aligned with their needs. Numerical evaluations demonstrate that PACT improves QoS alignment between users and providers and offers a scalable, liable approach to pricing agentic AI services in the future.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PACT, a contract-theoretic framework for pricing agentic AI services powered by Large Language Models (LLMs). PACT aims to address the challenges of pricing LLM services due to high infrastructure costs, multi-dimensional QoS (considering both objective and subjective aspects), and potential liability in high-stakes applications. The framework models QoS based on response time and user satisfaction, incorporates computational and infrastructure costs, and accounts for potential liability costs for the service provider. By using contract theory, PACT ensures incentive compatibility and individual rationality for users under information asymmetry, leading to tailored service offerings. Numerical evaluations are presented to demonstrate PACT's effectiveness in aligning QoS between users and providers and offering a scalable, liable approach to pricing agentic AI services.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of contract theory to the specific problem of pricing agentic AI services powered by LLMs, explicitly considering the complex interplay of QoS, user satisfaction, infrastructure costs, and liability. While contract theory itself is a well-established field, its application to this specific domain, incorporating liability considerations, is a relatively new contribution. Previous pricing models for AI services may not have adequately addressed the multi-dimensional nature of QoS or potential legal risks.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in the deployment and adoption of LLM-based agentic AI services. As these services become more prevalent, effective pricing strategies are essential for both service providers and users. The inclusion of liability considerations is particularly significant, as it acknowledges the potential risks associated with LLM outputs in high-stakes domains. The theoretical framework and numerical evaluations provide a solid foundation for further research and practical implementation.
    However, its significance depends on the practical impact of liability cost modeling which can be challenging to estimate or validate.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Model:** The paper develops a comprehensive model that captures the key factors influencing the pricing of agentic AI services.
    *   **Theoretical Rigor:**  The use of contract theory provides a solid theoretical foundation for the pricing framework. The paper includes essential proofs.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world problem and offers a practical solution that can be implemented by service providers.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the concepts and methodology.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplified Assumptions:** The model relies on certain simplifying assumptions, such as the discrete user type space and the specific functional forms for valuation and cost functions. These assumptions may limit the applicability of the framework in certain scenarios.
    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** While the numerical evaluations demonstrate the effectiveness of PACT, they are based on synthetic data and do not validate the framework with real-world data or user studies.
    *   **Liability Cost Estimation:** Quantifying potential liability costs can be challenging in practice and may require further investigation and collaboration with legal experts. The paper does not delve deeply into this aspect.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The model focuses primarily on response time and user satisfaction as the key dimensions of QoS. Other relevant factors, such as security or privacy, could be considered.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of pricing strategies for agentic AI services. It could also stimulate further research on the integration of contract theory and liability considerations in AI pricing models. The proposed framework can help service providers design more efficient and equitable pricing schemes.
    However, the reliance on liability costs may discourage widespread adoption unless the costs can be reliably assessed and addressed through insurance or other legal risk-management strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by applying contract theory to the complex problem of pricing LLM-based agentic AI services, especially by including aspects of liability. While there is room for improvement regarding empirical validation, real-world data, and further refinement, the theoretical model is well-constructed and addresses a critical need in the growing AI service landscape. The paper presents a novel framework with solid mathematical grounding, practical relevance, and the potential to stimulate further research in this emerging domain.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GT
- **Score**: 7/10

### Complex System Diagnostics Using a Knowledge Graph-Informed and Large Language Model-Enhanced Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21291v1)
- **Authors**: Saman Marandi, Yu-Shu Hu, Mohammad Modarres
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we present a novel diagnostic framework that integrates Knowledge Graphs (KGs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) to support system diagnostics in high-reliability systems such as nuclear power plants. Traditional diagnostic modeling struggles when systems become too complex, making functional modeling a more attractive approach. Our approach introduces a diagnostic framework grounded in the functional modeling principles of the Dynamic Master Logic (DML) model. It incorporates two coordinated LLM components, including an LLM-based workflow for automated construction of DML logic from system documentation and an LLM agent that facilitates interactive diagnostics. The generated logic is encoded into a structured KG, referred to as KG-DML, which supports hierarchical fault reasoning. Expert knowledge or operational data can also be incorporated to refine the model's precision and diagnostic depth. In the interaction phase, users submit natural language queries, which are interpreted by the LLM agent. The agent selects appropriate tools for structured reasoning, including upward and downward propagation across the KG-DML. Rather than embedding KG content into every prompt, the LLM agent distinguishes between diagnostic and interpretive tasks. For diagnostics, the agent selects and executes external tools that perform structured KG reasoning. For general queries, a Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Graph-RAG) approach is used, retrieving relevant KG segments and embedding them into the prompt to generate natural explanations. A case study on an auxiliary feedwater system demonstrated the framework's effectiveness, with over 90% accuracy in key elements and consistent tool and argument extraction, supporting its use in safety-critical diagnostics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a diagnostic framework that integrates Knowledge Graphs (KGs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve system diagnostics, particularly in safety-critical systems like nuclear power plants. The framework utilizes the Dynamic Master Logic (DML) model for functional modeling. It includes an LLM-based workflow for automated DML logic construction from system documentation and an LLM agent for interactive diagnostics. The generated logic is encoded in a KG (KG-DML), which supports hierarchical fault reasoning. Users interact with the system using natural language queries, which are interpreted by the LLM agent. The agent selects appropriate tools for KG reasoning, including upward and downward propagation, and employs a Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Graph-RAG) approach for general queries. A case study involving an auxiliary feedwater system demonstrates the framework's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem: improving system diagnostics in complex engineering systems, a traditionally difficult task. Integrating LLMs and KGs is a promising approach, as it aims to combine the strengths of both technologies. While the individual components of this approach are not necessarily novel, the *integration and specific application within the DML framework* appear to be the core innovation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Real-World Problem:** The focus on safety-critical systems like nuclear power plants highlights the practical relevance of the research.
